FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 TUESDAY, MAY 10, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C. 

The committee met at 10:20 a.m. in room 2172, Rayburn Building, the Honorable Clement J. Zablocki, presiding.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The committee will come to order. The committee meets this morning to resume hearings on the foreign aid bills, H.R. 

12449 and H.R. 12450. We are indeed privileged and honored in having one of our colleagues before us this morning, the Honorable 

Neal Smith of Iowa. 

You may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF HON. NEAL SMITH, A REPRESENTATIVE IN CONGRESS FROM THE STATE OF IOWA 

Mr. SMITH. Mr. Chairman, I appreciate the opportunity to be here.

I have a prepared statement. If I may I will submit it for the record. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Without objection, it is so ordered. 

(The statement referred to is as follows:) STATEMENT BY CONGRESSMAN NEAL SMITH TO THE HOUSE FOREIGN AFFAIRS COMMITTEE IN SUPPORT OF H.R. 5255 

Mr. Chairman, I appreciate having this opportunity to testify in support of 

H.R. 5255 which I recommend either be passed as a separate bill or included in 

H.R. 12449. One of the reasons I introduced this bill is because one of the leading universities in the country, Iowa State University, is in the District I represent and conversations that I have had with the President of the University and professors at the University have convinced me that performance and accomplishment at the present time are less than they would be under the longer term institutional arrangements provided in the bill. 

This bill authorizes the creation and maintenance of foreign development centers at universities which are engaged in foreign technical assistance projects. At the present time contracts with universities for advice, counsel and help concerning development projects in a particular country, are usually for a period of not to exceed three years. Quite often the university accepting the contract finds it difficult to find competent professional personnel to carry out the contract on such a short-term basis. Therefore, they often recruit a substantial part of their personnel for the contract and since the personnel recruited cannot be offered tenure, it is often difficult to attract the most competent individuals away from some other position which would include tenure and security. 

If a university could plan to specialize in the problems for a certain country or area for a period of ten years, it could not only attract more competent people but could also utilize the experience gained to a much better advantage for the government. When someone finishes a short-term contract and then moves on to some other area of responsibility without even leaving associates in a continuing program fully exposed to the knowledge and experience he gained, it cannot help 891

892 

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

but reduce the effectiveness of the program in the less developed countries involved. 

At the present time, many university staff members have to secure a leave of absence from their university to take on a foreign assignment; their advancement at the university is retarded; and their experience gained is not utilized after their short-term contract had expired. Under H.R. 5255, a university could mobilize a more competent staff for a long range program and even though some of the personnel left from time to time, there would be continuity and good use made of the experience they gained. The university would also be able to develop longer term programs in the countries involved. 

I believe everyone will agree that short-term projects will not do the best job in many of these countries. In most cases the greatest need is for longer term plans involving education and other programs which produce greater results for a dollar spent, but which results are not apparent for several years. These longer term plans could fit in with other university activities and schedules in such a way that the most competent staff members at the university would be willing to take foreign technical assistance assignments within their field of interest. Many such professors who have already attained a high standing in the professional field are not willing to take a part-time assignment concerning the problems of a particular country if they have no reason to believe that the result of their research and study will be used over a period of years. 

By directly involving the university and the most competent members of their professional staff in the technical assistance program in a way which is now practically impossible, it would greatly increase the number of professional people engaged in the development activities and broaden the base so that a better balanced program of economic assistance would be recommended. 

Mr. Chairman, this bill does not propose a new program in the technical assistance field but merely proposes a way to carry out the existing technical assistance programs so that we could secure better results. This kind of an approach has proven highly successful in the field of agricultural research. 

Agricultural universities, including Iowa State University, have performed research work on a continuing basis under existing Federal-State programs. 

Under this approach they were able to recruit competent staff and have gleaned benefits from all of the research performed through the years. This bill proposes that this successful formula be applied to planning economic assistance programs.

I strongly urge that this approach now be applied in the field of foreign technical assistance and that the committee either recommend H.R. 5255 for passage in the House of Representatives or that the committee include the provisions of H.R. 5255 as one section of H.R. 12449, the Foreign Assistance Act now before the committee.

Mr. SMITH. Mr. Chairman, I do not come here this morning proposing a new program in the technical assistance field but merely to propose a way to carry out the existing technical assistance program so it can work better and we can secure better results. The kind of auproach proposed here is the same one we have used at agricultural colleges for 100 years, and it has proved exceedingly advantageous in that case.

These agricultural colleges, including the one in my district, Iowa State University, have performed research work on a continuing basis under existing Federal-State programs. Under this approach they are able to recruit the most competent staff members and glean the benefits from all the research they perform over the years. This proposal here is to use that same approach in the technical assistance field. 

In this approach I am proposing that we create and maintain foreign development centers at the universities like the University of 

Wisconsin, the University of North Carolina, the Iowa State University, and the University of California. At the present time these technical assistance contracts are for a period of not to exceed 3 years. 

When this is the case it is very difficult to recruit the best personnel

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

893 to carry out the contract. You cannot offer a university, cannot offer tenure to people who are going to be carrying out only a 2 or 3 year contract. It is often difficult to involve the top personnel in those particular fields at that university because they see it as just a short-term proposition and they may not be able to use their experience in future years that they gain in working on that particular contract. 

I think we end up with less competent personnel than we would if we had longer-term contracts to the universities. Also it would be a continuing program so that the experience gained could be used in future years as they do in research projects on agricultural work at the present time. At the present time also if a university staff member wants to work on a technical assistance program he has to take a leave of absence from the university.

This, of course, is not desirable in many cases. It sets them back on promotions and so forth where if they used a longer term contract and plans they could involve their best personnel in the program. I think it would result in better administration of the program and better results for the United States and for the recipient countries. 

Technical assistance I think is undoubtedly here to stay. It has been here for about 20 years. It is the least controversial program in the foreign aid program. Those that are against foreign aid should want to make the dollars go further, and those who are for foreign aid want better results. It seems to me that the approach in H.R. 5255 should be adopted. I hope it will either be passed out as a separate bill or included as one section in H.R. 12449. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. I thank the gentleman. I want to commend our colleague from Iowa on his proposed bill, H.R. 5255. It is a very important and thoughtful suggestion. Chairman Morgan requested and has received an interim reply from the Department of State concerning the gentleman's bill. Without objection we will include it at this point in the record. 

(The report is as follows:) Hon. THOMAS E. MORGAN, Chairman, Committee on Foreign Affairs, House of Representatives DEPARTMENT OF STATE, 

Washington, D. C., October 26, 1965. 

DEAR MR. CHAIRMAN: This is in response to your recent letter asking the Department to expedite its report on H.R. 5255, "To authorize the Department of 

State through the Agency for International Development to encourage and assist colleges and universities in the establishment, strengthening and maintenance of programs on foreign development and for other purposes" introduced • .9 

February 22, 1965 by Congressman Neal Smith.

As I pointed out in my earlier letter of June 7, 1965, the Department and the Agency for International Development recognize the importance of the proposals in Congressman Smith's bill and the study I referred to is continuing. 

Meanwhile, the President's September 16 speech at the Smithsonian Institution has brought significant new dimensions to the question of increasing the international resources of American educational institutions. As you know, he committed the United States to a "broad and long-range plan of world-wide educational endeavor." A special task force is about to begin work on specific recommendations for the plan including a proposal "to help our schools and universities increase their knowledge of the world and the people who inhabit it." 

The President has expressed his intention to submit a program based on the plan to Congress by January.

894 

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

After the task force has prepared its report, the Department will be able to make more detailed comment which will take this report into full account along with the findings of the study I previously referred to. 

The Bureau of the Budget advises that, from the standpoint of the Administration's program, there is no objection to the submission of this letter. Sincerely,

DOUGLAS MACARTHUR II, Assistant Secretary for Congressional Relations. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The proposal is under study and as soon as the report is made available to the committee from the Department of State, I am sure the committee will give full consideration to the gentleman's proposal.

The question comes, how did you determine the amounts of $80 million for fiscal 1966, $100 million for fiscal 1967, and $125 million for fiscal 1968 ?

Mr. SMITH. I don't claim any superior knowledge in knowing exactly what amounts should be included. I tried to estimate about what it seemed it should be. I also received some advice from some people that had been working with colleges and universities in this field and thought this was about the right amount. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Would it be possible for you to have available for the committee the suggestions of those who were the experts in this regard? 

Mr. SMITH. Yes. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. So the committee can justify the amounts, if the committee considers your proposal and includes its proposal in the bill.

I have no further questions of the gentleman from Iowa. 

Mr. GROSS. Mr. Chairman, I wonder if my colleague from Iowa has any comment to make on the proposal that this be a 4-year instead of a 1-year program, which in effect would preclude an annual review by the Foreign Affairs Committee of the House? Would you care to comment? I don't know if you have gone into that feature of the bill. 

Mr. SMITH. You are speaking of the overall bill. I know there are some very controversial features in the overall bill but I am assuming that technical assistance will outlive many sections of this bill. It is more of a continuing program and some sections of this bill will be phased out from time to time even in 1 year or in 4 years. 

I was trying to deal with a permanent section of the bill. My personal opinion is that there are some foreign aid programs that work better on a 4-year basis but 1 year is enough on some of them. 

Mr. GROSS. Will the gentleman agree with me that ordinarily it is wise that Congress review these programs each year? The Appropriations Committee, of which the gentleman is a member, reviews most of the programs every year. 

Mr. SMITH. We do review them through the appropriations process annually. We can cut them off annually. We can't really revise them on an annual basis that way. 

Mr. GROSS. The Appropriations Committee members can't revise them but they review the program and the results obtained. 

Mr. SMITH. That is right.

Mr. GROSS. It is my opinion, and I would hope the gentleman would agree that this program ought to be continued on a 1-year basis so that it is subject to annual review.

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

895 Mr. SMITH. In looking at technical assistance on a 10-year basis, wasn't proposing that any other foreign aid program be on a 10-year basis. I think this one is here to stay, and that we ought to try to make it work the best way possible. I just want to take a leaf out of the book we have used for 100 years at Iowa State University and other universities like that, and put it on the basis that we have had for agricultural research. 

Mr. GROSS. I don't want to prolong this discussion. I hope the gentleman is wrong in his belief that this costly foreign handout program is going to continue for another 10 years. 

I thank you.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Fountain? 

Mr. FOUNTAIN. I have no questions to ask of Mr. Smith. I would simply like to observe that we are delighted to have Congressman Smith here before the committee. We served together on the House 

Government Operations Committee for a number of years and he was a member of the subcommittee which I had the honor of chairing. 

Mr. SMITH. Those were good days. The gentleman's committee under his leadership has saved millions of dollars for the taxpayers. 

Mr. FOUNTAIN. That is right. Congressman Smith was one of the most informed, competent members of that committee and he certainly reflected credit upon himself and his constituency here in the Congress. We hated to lose him to Appropriations. It is a pleasure to have him here this morning. 

Mr. SMITH. I thank the gentleman for those comments.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Roybal. 

Mr. ROYBAL. I want to compliment the gentleman from Iowa for having introduced H.R. 5255. I agree with the intent of the bill. I am, however, concerned about the fact that many universities that are now doing community development research are in my opinion doing an inferior job. They seem to go back to old reports, rewrite them and send them to the Department as being something that was done as part of their contract.

How much money is involved in this particular bill for fiscal year 1966?

Mr. SMITH. There are some figures in the bill but as I said before, I don't claim to know exactly what the figure should be. I think if you take whatever amount of money this committee thinks should be authorized for technical assistance and spend it in the way provided in the bill instead of under the present procedure, we should receive more for the money.

Just as you said, as it is at the present time they have a 2-year contract. They have to recruit some new personnel. They will probably pick up a report someone else made and take as fact some of the assumptions in the report and go from there, instead of having developed a competent and continuing staff to work in a particular area. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Then the real intent of your bill is to provide the mechanism that is necessary so that a real study is made? 

Mr. SMITH. I am proposing a different procedure be used in the technical assistance field, not proposing a new program. I am not going to argue about what the amount should be. Whatever amount is spent, it seems to me it should be spent under this kind of approach instead of the way we have been doing it.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. I want to again thank our colleague and on behalf of the committee extend our appreciation for his thoughtful presentation, and again commend him for the bill he has introduced. We are looking forward to the submission of the information the committee requested. 

Mr. SMITH. Thank you very much.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The committee will next hear Chancellor John T. 

Caldwell, North Carolina State University at Raleigh, speaking as chairman, International Affairs Committee, National Association of

State Universities and Land-Grant Colleges. 

Chancellor Caldwell is accompanied by Elton L. Johnson, director,

National Association of State Universities and Land-Grant Colleges. 

STATEMENT OF CHANCELLOR JOHN T. CALDWELL, NORTH CARO- LINA STATE UNIVERSITY AT RALEIGH, CHAIRMAN, INTERNA- TIONAL AFFAIRS COMMITTEE, NATIONAL ASSOCIATION OF STATE UNIVERSITIES AND LAND-GRANT COLLEGES 

Mr. CALDWELL. Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I appreciate very much your hearing a representative of the Association of State Universities and Land-Grant Colleges on this important bill you have before you, H.R. 12449. I do have a colleague here, Mr.

Russell Thackrey, who is executive director of the State Association of Land-Grant Colleges, and Dr. Johnson whom you have mentioned is director of our International Rural Development Office which we have set up here in Washington. There is a representative also of the staff of the American Council on Education.

This association for whom I speak, the National Association of State Universities and Land-Grant Colleges, Mr. Chairman, has 97 member institutions. We don't parade these figures with any feeling of braggadocio but I do want you to know the extent of our involvement in American higher education. We grant 31 percent of all the baccalaureate degrees that are granted in this country, 51 percent of all degrees in engineering, which is of extraordinary importance in this country as well as in developing countries, 59 percent of all the doctoral degrees, and 100 percent of all the doctoral degrees in the field of agriculture.

President Truman announced the point 4 program in 1949 which was the beginning of our technical assistance abroad. Immediately the president of our association, who then was the present president of Michigan State University, Dr. John Hannah, wired President

Truman the full support of our member institutions. 

Indeed the first administrator of that international cooperation program, called point 4, was Dr. Bennett of Oklahoma State University, another of our member institutions. We have been involved since the beginning in a very significant way. 

Just a few weeks ago I can't keep up from day to day with the precise involvements, but there were 119 institutions of higher education pursuing contracts with the Agency for International Development. Eighty of those were our member institutions. They held about 192 separate contracts with the U.S. Agency and the annual

expenditure was running around $26 million. In addition to that, of course, we use foundation funds to accomplish objectives in this international development area.

In 1963 when a new awareness came upon the Federal agencies of the importance of agriculture in the developing economies of these underdeveloped nations, they called upon us for renewed efforts in this area, and we established in Washington the International Rural Development Office-Dr. Johnson is our present director of this officewhich was set up to assist AID in using us and us in making ourselvs available to the AID organization.

We, therefore, have a very special interest in the bill that is before your committee, H.R. 12449. We hope especially that certain features can be enacted into law. I hope we are not overreading what is written into this bill, but as we read it there are these three features which we think are very favorable to a more adequate and more fruitful exploitation of the university resources in this effort. The first is the concept of the 5-year authorization of funding for the program of technical assistance. I will come back to that.

The second one is that this bill would provide funds to strengthen the universities to perform the functions that they are called upon to perform in international development. 

Thirdly, we see in this bill a fresh idea of funding university technical assistance through grants, in other words not complete reliance upon the typical contract method which is usually narrowed to proj- ects.

Foreign assistance is about 25 years old in this country. We have learned many lessons, or we should have, and we think in the universities we have. One of the lessons is that economic development is an extraordinary complex process. We know far too little about it. 

We think that too we have learned oversimplified and piecemeal and short-lived development efforts are apt to produce only shortlived results. We have come to feel as the State Department and the 

Agency have, that agricultural development must have very high priority.

Fourth, for the long pull we think a nation must have an institutional capability that produces human competence, human competence that can innovate, that can organize, that can manage, that can staff all of the many interrelated and intricate processes of economic growth.

This is where the universities primarily come in-it is in this institutional building, knowledge building, producing a career climate, people who are not only trained but committed to do a job in their own countries. Human resource development is what we are concerned with.

Economists tell us today this is the primary ingredient of economic growth and development. There are many areas of service in which our universities have been engaged, Mr. Chairman, in this overseas development. We have engaged in public health activities, in building schools of business and administration to develop more human competence in that area, systems of education that we felt would more adequately serve those countries, vocational training, home economics and in a very pronounced way our institutions have been involved in agriculture.

Let me speak to the first of the provisions of this bill which we think would enable us to help the aims of the U.S. Government in this area. 

The 5-year authorization: We think it is unrealistic to expect to bring into being overnight in some underdeveloped country an institution such as a university or to improve one that may be only the semblance of a university in a 4-year period or 2-year period or a 5-year period or even a 10-year period sometimes. That has taken us many, many years in our own developed society to produce the kind of institutional capability that can produce the manpower we need in our own country.

We think a 5-year authorization is a psychological boost to a program which has been all too often in the past characterized by yearto-year appraisal and analysis and uncertainty, and though 5 years is not long enough to develop these institutions, it is a step in the right direction. It would serve both the needs of the nation, this Nation, insofar as it is trying to develop an institution in this foreign country, and it certainly will aid us in making our own commitments from the university standpoint to help in this effort. 

The second provision that is in this act we like-it is new and it has been needed and we hope very deeply it can be enacted, and that is in section 103 and I shall read from it: "Funds made available under this section may be used for assistance on such terms and conditions as the President may specify to research and educational institutions in the United States for the purpose of strengthening their capacity to develop the resources likely to be needed for programs concerned with economic and social development of less developed countries." 

Mr. Chairman and gentleman, the resources of our universities are really stretched out these days. We have to offer instruction at all levels, undergraduate and graduate, at the most advanced levels. We are performing research in the development of our own Nation and its competitive posture. We are engaged in extension and public service over a wide range of activities. So we have to answer to trustees, to legislators, to taxpayers, to special public, like a grape grower or whoever it might be, whenever we divert our manpower into other activities that seem not to be directly related to the local interest.

We have new calls upon our time and our energies locally, so any other new and expanded cause that we think might be justified theoretically has to be justified practically at home. So anything that the Congress of the United States can do to help us enlarge our own local intrainstitutional capacities, that is our personnel and our resources to help in this foreign assistance program will be of benefit to the program itself and to us. 

We are going to continue to meet our domestic requirements and to have manpower involved in these foreign assistance activities. We need funds to support our internal strengthening, to give us more personnel in depth, to provide a career-minded people for international service and to give stability to our staffing of these programs, so we can develop our position with you and at home. 

The Hatch Act funding-and I am so pleased that Mr. Smith, whom you just heard, had opened up this statement today with some praise of the fashion in which the Hatch Act funding of agriculture research has gone.

Since 1887 this has been a steady reliable source of funding, but it has enabled our schools of agriculture to develop great departments of career-minded researchers who were committed not only to their specialties but to making this knowledge available to people who could use it in the agricultural economy of this Nation. It is that kind of reliable funding which has enabled our institutions to do the magnificent job that is so evident to everybody in the world today in our own agricultural programs in the United States. It is on this basis that we would like to urge this particular part of the bill that is before you.

There is another aspect of this bill, the suggestion that the funding of the university programs and technical assistance programs might be accomplished also through grants as well as through contracts. 

Up to now the funding has been entirely through the contract method, the project contract funding device.

We have accomplished a great deal under it. The contract document has been steadily improved. Our relationships with the Agency for 

International Development has steadily improved. We have learned how to work with each other. We have learned how to make the contract document a more efficient document and device for working. 

But it is not inherently adapted to program development. It is much more adapted to projects which are of a short-run character which can be defined with some specificity in advance. It tends to be an inflexible type of document where too many details are settled in advance of doing any of the work. 

We feel that a grant type of funding which would be extended over some years is inherently more suited to the utilization of a university, it is more inherently suited to institutional building, it has in it more of the possibility of setting a goal and planning a program to move toward that goal and giving plenty of flexibility. 

As we move along and gain experience in our own evaluation of what we are doing we can change our personnel when we need to, change the composition of the team that is working in the area, and can be more comprehensive.

The contract document tends to be narrower, more inflexible, more characterized by details which should not be settled in advance. Grant funding will still protect the Government. I will refer again to the 

Hatch Act funding. Although appropriations under the Hatch Act, gentlemen, as you know, are annual appropriations, none of those funds are released to the institutions such as my own for research in agriculture until we have presented a program which has to be accepted.

This does not mean presenting a detail of all the positions that will be employed in accomplishing the program. It is a broad outline of a program to move toward an objective. Then the funds are released when that is approved.

Furthermore, there is plenty of opportunity for review, reporting, and an audit of what has been accomplished. This device has been proven to be so valuable in building our own institutions, we feel that it carries through into institutional building programs abroad, and protects the Government entirely in what it desires to seek. 

We have some illustrative statements from other university presidents around the country, the president of Texas A. & M. University,

president of Indiana University, Michigan, president of our Iowa 

State and Ohio State, Purdue University, University of Wyoming,

Oklahoma State, which I am attaching here. I won't read them. But they are supportive of one or the other of the phases of this bill which 

I have indicated to you. 

Let me come to a conclusion and invite your questions, please. Mr.

Chairman, we have this conviction which I would like to state, that we believe our Government is properly and unavoidably engaged in its vast foreign assistance program and will be for some time. We believe, secondly, that assistance in the development of institutions, the development of knowledge, that is, in the development of people, all by nature take time, and by nature are the most enduring of whatever we do to help these developing societies as a matter of foreign policy. 

Thirdly, we feel the universities of this country have a special competence for this kind of a task. If 12449 is enacted you can expect the universities to come forward with proposals that are sounder than before, with programs that can be carried out with continuity, with more effective interaction among the disciplines that would be involved among the universities and with fuller utilization of the broad potential of university competence.

We believe this bill would help the U.S. Government, help put the underdeveloped world on is feet, and we are ready to dig in and to help even more than we have in the past. 

As far as the funding, let me say this, we, the universities in our association are now spending, as I told you, under Government contract relationships about $25 or $26 million a year. I don't know how much of this contemplated bill, of the authorized funding, would go into university type of relationships, but we note the present funding under section 212 of this bill runs to about $202 million in 1966, the authorization that is proposed for 1967 would run about $231 million. 

I note Mr. Smith's comment that he sees about $80 million, going up to $125 million in this technical assistance in which universities would be involved, $80 million would be about three times what we are now spending. All I would make is a generalization to say that under a proper type relationship fully committed to a continuing overseas development program of institution building, the universities could use, profitably and effectively, a whole lot more money than is now being expended under these programs, whether it ought to be $80 million or $100 million, I am not prepared to say right now. It would be hard. But an authorization higher than what is presently being expended is certainly justified and we would like to support it and offer our services again to the U.S. Government in furthering our interests abroad. 

Thank you. 

(The complete statement of Mr. Caldwell is as follows:) STATEMENT OF JOHN T. CALDWELL, NORTH CAROLINA STATE UNIVERSITY AT RALEIGH; CHAIRMAN, INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS COMMITTEE, NATIONAL ASSO- CIATION OF STATE UNIVERSITIES AND LAND-GRANT COLLEGES 

Mr. Chairman and Members of the Committee on Foreign Affairs, my name is John T. Caldwell and I am Chancellor of North Carolina State University at

Raleigh, which is a major campus of the consolidated University of North Carolina. I am present today to represent the National Association of State Univer sities and Land-Grant Colleges regarding the Foreign Assistance Act of 1966,

H.R. 12449. Our Association is an organization composed of 97 State and Land- 

Grant universities and colleges located in all the States and Puerto Rico. The 

Association has the distinction of being the oldest organization of higher education in the United States (founded in 1887) and has a democratic procedure for voting by appropriate members in an annual convention to arrive at its policies. Interim decisions or actions are taken by the executive committee representing the Association. Member institutions of the Association enroll approximately one million students and currently grant about 31 percent of all baccalaureate degrees. We confer 51 percent of the degrees in engineering, and about 59 percent of all degrees at the doctoral level, including 100 percent of all doctor's degrees in agriculture, which is of special significance in foreign assistance programs.

In addition to our instructional programs at all levels, vast research and extension activities combine to place us deeply in the service of State and Nation. We have historically committed our energies to national purposes which needed us. When President Truman inaugurated his Point IV program in 

1949, the President of a member institution, Oklahoma State University, became the Administrator. Our Association President, Dr. John A. Hannah of

Michigan State, pledged the support of our member institutions. 

Since then our involvement in overseas technical assistance to developing countries has grown in volume and significance. Although the statistics change from month to month, a recent count found 119 institutions of higher learning engaged in technical assistance programs. Over 80 of these universities and colleges are State universities or Land-Grant colleges or both. They were operating 192 or more separate contracts with the Agency for International Development in a total of 39 countries with an annual expenditure of about $25,000,000. 

In addition to contracts with the Agency for International Development, they were using millions of dollars in funds from the major Foundations. 

Most frequently the contracts were held by a single university but sometimes by a consortium of universities. And oftentimes the separately contracting universities collaborated informally on work in the recipient country. Responding -in 1963 to a new awareness in the Federal agencies of the fundamental importance of agriculture to developing economies, our Association established in 

Washington an International Rural Development Office. This office, funded originally by Foundation grants (but hereafter by membership assessment), exists primarily to assist our institutions and the Agency for International Development in the efficient utilization of university competencies in foreign technical assistance.

We, therefore, have a special interest in H.R. 12449, the Foreign Assistance 

Act of 1966. And we are here to express our very real hope that certain features of this Bill can be enacted into law. I speak particularly of the inauguration of three new elements which can be enormously helpful to the Government in utilizing university resources in international development: first, the five-year authorization provision for university programs; second, the provision of funds for strengthening the universities to perform the tasks of international development; third, the funding of university technical assistance activities by grants. 

Mr. Chairman, the United States has been formally engaged in foreign assistance programs for a quarter century or more. But especially since Point IV in 1949 we have systematically undertaken to help the developing nations, some young, some very old, to get on their feet. As a nation we have learned many lessons, or indeed we should have learned them. Certainly the universities have learned much from their involvement in overseas technical assistance efforts. 

Several lessons are emerging which bear directly upon the legislation before you today. One lesson is that economic development is an exceedingly complex process about which we know far too little. Another is that oversimplified, piecemeal, short-lived development efforts are more than apt to produce only short-lived results.

A third lesson is that agricultural development must have high priority. And a fourth is that hand-outs are good only for emergencies. For the long development pull, a nation must have an institutional capability that produces the kind of human competence that in turn innovates, organizes, manages and staffs the processes of economic growth.

And this is where universities come into the picture. Human resource development, knowledge-building, the production of career commitment, hence institution-building, are our business.

House bill 12449 contains important steps which we support toward recognizing the long-term element in economic development and how to use the universities more efficiently.

THE FIVE-YEAR AUTHORIZATION 

The institutions of a society which develop, undergird and replenish the individual and social competencies cannot be produced overnight. For this reason we applaud the proposal in H.R. 12449 for five-year funding authorizations, especially as the universities are involved. Not that a five-year period is an adequate measure of the time it takes to bring any social institution into being. The process more than likely takes a generation-or two generations or maybe four. But five-year authorizations would add a measure of psychological stability to a foreign aid program too often characterized in the past by year-toyear uncertainty. The five-year authorization begins to recognize the desirability of choosing long-range goals and building programs toward their achievement. 

It is unrealistic in the extreme to expect to develop in two years or ten years in a sub-marginal society what has taken twenty or fifty years to develop in our own. Our present day agricultural experiment stations have produced their greatest miracles only since 1920 (some would say since 1940). Yet they were inaugurated by the Congress in 1887.

To be sure, the contracting universities now make five-year "contracts" with AID. But the requirement of renegotiation each year tends to make meaningless the five-year concept, or at least seriously reduces its value. 

So we applaud even a small step toward recognizing the long-range requirements of effective technical assistance. 

STRENGTHENING OUR UNIVERSITIES 

Mr. Chairman, the universities we represent, and indeed all the colleges and universities of the Nation, are stretched almost to the limits today in meeting their wide range of responsibilities. Those institutions which have undertaken to perform foreign assistance contracts are called upon to justify their diversions of personnel and energies away from pressing domestic obligations to overseas operations. They answer to trustees, to legislators, to taxpayers and to special publics such as the American farmers who look to them for increasing ability to adjust to changing conditions of production and in the market place. 

It is essential that in the process of staffing overseas operations with our better scientists and administrators we do not destroy the continuity of research and extension programs at home. While the United States is helping develop competent agricultural universities with plan pathologists or soil scientists in India, we are expected to meet the ever-present pressure of agricultural progress and change in our own boundaries. It has taken time to develop scientific departments at home with sufficient specialization and expertness in depth to do the research, do the undergraduate and advanced teaching, advise our sophisticated agricultural entrepreneurs, and to work with our own substandard farm families. 

Only a relatively few departments in any specialty have manpower to spare and none with real comfort. Yet we engage in foreign aid projects. We do it because of the compulsions of national interest and the challenge to the professional scientist to contribute his skill in a different culture. 

Under Hatch Act funding combined with State appropriations our university colleges of agriculture have developed departments which have many commendable characteristics, such as stability, career-mindedness, high morale and sense of significance, the ability to interact with other disciplines, and the capacity for existing discovery, relevant application and wide dissemination. To these departments we must now add funds for more personnel and support to provide adequate reservoirs of career-minded scholars for foreign technical assistance. 

We, therefore, welcome, Mr. Chairman, the recognition in H.R. 12449 that the institutions need funds for international strengthening, something which the present year-to-year project type of funding has ignored from the beginning. 

ADVANTAGES OF "GRANTS" TYPE OF FUNDING 

Mr. Chairman, let me now point out why we endorse so strongly the method of funding university operations by grants, another concept contained in H.R. 12449.

Our experience with the contract type of funding has been instructive. The universities and the Agency for International Development can point to some

successful accomplishments all over the world under university contracts. But the experience also has demonstrated the limitation of this type of Government- University relationship. The contract document has been steadily improved, but its limitations persist. It is characteristically geared to assigning projects rather than to comprehensive, well thought-out long-range programs, to specifics rather than to goals. It tends to be more legalistic, even accounting-oriented, rather than to program accomplishment. More than likely the contract is negotiated with the university by a program officer in the Agency for International Development only to have its interpretation and evaluation in different hands. Again, despite improvements, it has a built-in tendency toward inflexibility. Too many decisions of detail are made prematurely. Changes in program dictated by circumstances in the field, and by evaluation, are subject only for formal renegotiation nearly always in terms of a "project." 

On the other hand, the grant device is inherently more suited (1) to the utilization of the university, and (2) to the accomplishment of institution building so basic to the development mission. The grant has great potential for program planning, flexibility and comprehensiveness. 

Yet in using the grant device, the Government would not abdicate its role of goal setting, program approval, evaluation and audit. These desirable controls are all present in the traditional and marvelously successful administration of the Hatch funds under which the agricultural experiment stations have been developed and supported since 1887. The Hatch funds are allocated on a formula basis with full implication that they will continue indefinitely. 

But before funds are released to the universities, programs must be reviewed and approved. This is an annual procedure. Furthermore, the procedure has encouraged the maximum utilization of local funds and maximum cooperation at the local level among governmental departments and agencies, among professionals and non-professionals, and between disciplines. All of this without loss of proper Federal evaluation and control. All of this would be true of the grant philosophy in the context of foreign aid. 

The grant device, in contrast to the contract method, encourages the setting of long-range goals and the planning of programs to accomplish them, with flexibility of programming in the light of day-to-day experience, evaluative judgments, and changes in conditions, without requiring the formalities of contract renegotiation with attendant delays, uncertainties and oftentimes frustration.

The grant device could be used to facilitate more complete involvement of total university expertness. Furthermore, it places much more heavily on the university the responsibility to decide what to do in programs which are essentially educational and institutional in character. 

But the grant method

Long-term funding is desirable using any device. coupled with long-term funding is the better combination. 

We find other possibilities in grant support which have been difficult, if not impossible, to realize within present concepts. One is the inclusion of research. 

Another is the very helpful use of graduate students, both domestic and foreign. 

We have long sought to include a research component in university foreign assistance contracts, with only limited success. 

Now what kind of research do we have in mind? Many kinds. (1) Research by U.S. scholars and foreign scholars on the economic development process itself, about which we know far too little, as it applies under many different conditions. (2) Research on relevant scientific problems of plants, animals, soil, breeding, teaching and learning, administration and institution building, technological transfer, etc., much of which may be mutually complementary with research in the United States.

An important advantage of being able to use grant moneys with considerable institutional discretion in research is that the U.S. career specialist-scholar can participate in the foreign assistance program with less risk that his typical two-year "leave of absence" will be a loss in his own professional development and advancement. We can train the budding overseas host country scientist by assignments on research problems in his native environment, assignments which not only contribute to solving local needs for knowledge and answers but effectively relate his scientific training to his career in his home country. Similarly, scholarly competence and graduate instruction in the local foreign university can be more soundly developed for the long pull, just as we have in the 

---

United States, by combining a vital, relevant research program with university teaching and in many cases extension. 

Our own graduate students are an overlooked potential for much useful work in technical assistance. And they represent a real source of career internationalists. But they are not too numerous. Competition for them is keen. They can never be attracted to or successfully brought into the technical assistance programs under the uncertain conditions of annually negotiated contracts. 

One last point, the grant type of funding can be useful for continuing a helpful, relatively inexpensive way for the U.S. university to continue an institutional relationship beyond the period of major input for development of the overseas institution, even beyond the weaning period in some cases. 

Mr. Chairman, I have used agricultural science for more than illustrative purposes in this testimony. The universities contain a wide range of competence in depth, extending over the full range of human knowledge, understanding and skills. We are available to help, therefore, in many areas requiring education, science and technology. But in no area is our competence more significant to the foreign assistance task than in agricultural science, pure and applied. We know that the Government places the highest priority on agriculture in the complex, multifaceted goal of helping the developing societies. We make this statement in order to place our brief statement to you in proper perspective. 

Mr. Chairman, under present contracts the universities are spending several millions of dollars annually. The money H.R. 12449 could provide to strengthen universities, we feel, represents a good start toward the accomplishment of longrange objectives under more stable financial conditions and institutional relationships. The Government and the universities will need some experience under the new provisions. We would expect to use wisely whatever is authorized and appropriated. Undoubtedly much, much greater funds could in time be effectively used. If this new and creative program is approved by the Congress, Mr. Chairman, the Agency for International Development will need to devise some new mechanisms for dealing with the universities, devices that will capitalize fully on the fresh opportunity for strengthening us to be used and then using us more fully. We look forward to working out with A.I.D. such new requirements.

I am supplementing this formal statement with direct quotations from a sampling of letters from other university presidents among our member institutions.

In conclusion, Mr. Chairman, let me state for our member institutions these convictions.

One, we believe our Government is properly and unavoidably engaged in its vast foreign assistance programs for some time to come. 

Two, assistance in the development of institutions, the development of knowledge, and the development of people all by nature take time, but also by nature are the most enduring of whatever we do to help the developing societies. 

Three, the university has special competence for the task. 

If H.R. 12449 is enacted, you can expect the universities to come forward with proposals which are sounder than before, with programs that can be carried out with continuity, breadth and effective interaction, with fuller utilization of the broad potential of our university competence.

H.R. 12449 would help the United States Government help put the underdeveloped world on its feet. And the universities are ready to dig in and do it. 

We thank you, Mr. Chairman, and members of the Committee.

APPENDIX A-SUPPLEMENT TO TESTIMONY OF DR. JOHN T. CALDWELL ILLUSTRATIVE STATEMENTS FROM UNIVERSITIES COOPERATING WITH THE U.S. 

AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT IN TECHNICAL ASSISTANCE (1) From President Earl Rudder, Texas A & M University (in support of long-term authority and fund commitment): "One of the most common fallacies in foreign assistance thinking is the assumption that host officials responsible for development in newly developing countries are already committed to the action required for progress with specific development projects. This fallacy, plus short-term aid commitments results in projects getting underway without sufficient commitment on the part of the host government to secure the minimum in local participation and support required for success. The result is hasty plan-

ning and inadequate educational groundwork required to build local interest, understanding and support." (2) From President Elvis Stahr, Indiana University (in support of the need for long-term commitment) : "Renegotiations of contracts annually or biennially have been known to take as much as eight months*** with corresponding uncertainty and loss of time. "*** ten years would be needed for a solid foundation to be laid on the basis of which a country would be able to operate any institution that was created under a contract. In Thailand it certainly turned out to be true, and it was not until after a period of ten years that Thailand was ready to embark upon the creation of the National Institute of Development Administration." 

(3) From Vice-President Allan F. Smith, The University of Michigan (in support of strengthening universities to develop needed resources): "There are many types of economists who are extremely useful in a Center such as ours but who do not fit easily into project budgets. For example, mathematical economists, statisticians, and growth theorists are all quite useful in assisting the study of the historical, institutional and policy aspects of economic development, but it is difficult to finance them through project grants." 

(4) From President John T. Fey, The University of Wyoming (in support of long-term funding): "In view of our activities and the need for long-range planning, the changes proposed in the Foreign Assistance Act would be very desirable. If these changes are enacted, the University of Wyoming would be able to expand its background resources for training and research related to our efforts, both present and future, to aid in our country's program of assisting less well-developed nations. Lack of predictable funding is a major deterrent for any university-related program." (5) From Purdue University, F. L. Hovde, President (in support of grants to universities and long-term commitments): "Such grants would be extremely useful to our Land Grant institutions. In fact, they are absolutely essential if the full potential of education and research is to be brought to bear on the serious problems of the world*** the training of U.S. and foreign technicians and scientists is a continuing endeavor that cannot be mounted effectively with shortrun fund authorization. ***" 

(6) From President Novice G. Fawcett, The Ohio State University (in support of strengthening university resources): "This is definitely needed for, as you can readily appreciate, candor requires me to note that both here and elsewhere participation in foreign operations-meritorius as they are has sometimes been at the expense of the quality of campus programs." 99 

(7) From President W. Robert Parks, Iowa State University (in support of program continuity): "Identification and selection of those to receive training, development of understanding and confidence on the part of those who will be working on the joint enterprise, and development of a first-hand knowledge of the country to be assisted all take time. These can only be accomplished effectively where it is known that the program will be carried forward without interruptions for renegotiation or periodic writing of new contracts and new cooperative agreements." 

(8) From President Oliver S. Willham, Oklahoma State University (in sup port of long-term authorization): "With a five-year commitment, a dean or department head can support a particular project and fill particular positions on international technical assistance contracts, and know exactly what adjustments he can and must make in his college or department to provide continuity and maintain competence on his own campus." 

(9) From President W. E. Morgan, Colorado State University (supporting as sistance to U.S. universities): "Our universities can contribute in the international sphere only to the extent they are enabled to develop and strengthen their capabilities through the Foreign Assistance Act and similar sources. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Chancellor Caldwell, for a fine statement. In your presentation, however, you indict to a degree the past contracting activities of AID when you say as you do in your summation that, if the 5-year program is authorized, the proposals would be sounder than before. This would indicate to me that the past programs that the universities have been engaged in were not sound. 

Mr. CALDWELL. Mr. Zablocki, I don't believe the language is intended to say that. We have many sound programs, but I proposed

in my oral statement that there are many types of programs which could be better financed or funded and better organized if there were longer term commitments. When we say "sounder," that is, of course, a comparative. We don't mean the programs were necessarily unsound in the past.

We know, however, in our own experience that even though we now may have a 3-year contract, the very fact that we have to review annually the budget means that sometimes we go right up to the very end and even spill over into a new contract year without being certain that we can fund the activities that we have, in my case, in Peru, this kind of uncertainty is really tough on good staffing. We have hundreds of real sound accomplishments under the contract method. As a matter of fact, I don't think that contracts ought to be abandoned. 

I merely think there ought to be an opportunity for this new type of funding for the institution building type of activity abroad. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. There have been criticisms leveled in the past about university contract funding, particularly the charge that many of these contracts were for programs purely of the theoretical or experimental type. It has been said that the universities would not spend their own money to experiment domestically but having a contract with AID in a foreign land, they try out various theories. Many of these experiments don't work out. Is there a possibility that if this requested authority is granted that it would bring about programs in these universities which have not really been tried before?

Mr. CALDWELL. Mr. Chairman, a good many of the things that we have been doing under contracts are things which have not been tried before. In every case I would say that whether a particular project would pay off in terms of a nation's development is always problematical. You just can't know. We have in every instance I am sure, every time a university has entered one of these programs there has been an inner belief in terms of what was known on the part of the local host country, plus our AID leaders in that country, plus the university expert who was brought in, that this project, whatever it was, was going to make a contribution to the development of that country.

I would say that many of them may have made very little impact on the country, sometimes because they were too short lived, sometimes because they were not long range enough, and sometimes because maybe there wasn't enough patience with making an error and maybe profiting from the error in choosing a broader, or maybe more realistic objective.

I would merely say there is no guarantee that any research that we pursue in this country in our U.S. agricultural experiment stations, will pay off. Sometimes we go down a completely blind alley and don't get anything out of it and have to start something else. 

Our objective may remain unchanged in terms of what we are trying to accomplish in a given plant or animal or piece of the economy. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Caldwell, I have two additional brief questions.

I have asked these of other witnesses who advocated a multiyear authorization. Does North Carolina State University have an annual budget, or a multiyear budget?

Mr. CALDWELL. As far as the general assembly is concerned, it has a biennial budget, but the system in North Carolina gives us a guarantee

that at least what we got in this biennium will be continued the next biennium with such additions as are essential for rising costs and rising enrollments.

So we do have a guaranteed base which is a continuing budget of the university.

AID." If you are now making 5-year contracts with AID, what is the authority that AID is doing it under, and why is it necessary that we now give AID a 5-year multiyear authorization?

Mr. CALDWELL. Five-year contracts and 3-year contracts. AID

you would have to ask these people of the AID organization. If they are violating the authorization, this is for them to answer.

It has been recognized that the 2and 3-year contracts were oftentimes inadequate and we had to have some assurance that the AID Agency would consider a program continuing beyond 2 years. They don't make a financial commitment for 5 years, they merely agree we are going to try to accomplish this job over 5 years. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. If AID can now enter into 5-year contracts with universities under annual authorizations, would there be a possibility that if they were granted authority of 5-year multiyear authorization that they may go into 10-year contracts with universities? 

Mr. CALDWELL. I can't answer that question, sir.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Gross.

Mr. GROSS. We have been and are spending millions of dollars in 

India on so-called education through universities, colleges, so on and so forth. I wish I had the figure to relate here this morning of the unbelievably small increase in agricultural production in India, despite the fact that we have ladled out, in one way or another, around $6 billion to India in recent years. 

How do you account for this, Mr. Caldwell, that we get so little that is acutely needed in India today?

Mr. CALDWELL. I would respond to that and am happy to in several ways. In the first place, I don't know that we have spent our $6 billion all wisely. We may have spent it as well as we knew how to meet both emergency and short-lived situations as well as the longrange situation. 

Mr. GROSS. We are meeting the emergency situation with respect to food in an additional way, as you well know. We passed a bill the other day calling for the expenditure of $700 million for food for India and unbelievably included was tobacco to be shipped over to starving people in India. I don't know how that was accomplished other than a spineless Congress approved it. 

But you go ahead.

Mr. CALDWELL. I don't feel called upon to defend that either, Mr. 

Gross. Nevertheless, let me say that you are illustrating with the 

India example the complex character of this problem of economic development. It has been only recently that any of the AID programs have been designed in India to develop agricultural education per se. I have been to India and in their universities and I despaired to see the number of people their universities were turning out with degrees and degrees in philosophy and how few degrees India was turning out in the field of engineering and in the field of agriculture. law

Mr. GROSS. Is this the fault of the Indian Government or is this the fault of the foreign handout program?

Mr. CALDWELL. I think part of it is the fault of their colonial heritage. The point is we take India as it is and start from now. What we have been doing, we have started a fairly sizable effort as I understand in India- 

Mr. GROSS. I don't mean to interrupt you, Mr. Caldwell, but I can't start from now because I have an obligation to the taxpayers of this country. This Government has already dumped around $6 billion into 

India and it is time that this $6 billion produced some real results. 

Mr. CALDWELL. Mr. Gross, I don't want to defend all the $6 billion because I don't know how it has all been spent. All I do want to say, though, is that developing the human competency to provide for a productive agriculture in India takes longer than 5 years or longer than 10 years or however long we have been spending some money over there.

We established the agricultural experiment stations in this country in 1887. The economy can demonstrate that it was not until the 1920's that production yields began to improve significantly in the United States as a result of that research. Some of them would say it has only been since 1940. This is precisely my point about developing human competency.

Furthermore, economic development is not just a matter of improving production on one plot of ground. It involves a great many other things like credit, like incentives. It involves storage facilities. It involves marketing outlets. It involves a thousand ingredients that go into making up the human animal. It doesn't happen overnight. 

It may be that we expected too much in too short a time. 

Mr. GROSS. Mr. Caldwell, I didn't know you were going to appear this morning or I would have gotten a copy of this contract a little earlier. But in a casual reading of it in the short time I have had here, the University of Kentucky holds two contracts in Indonesia, one for $62 million, and another for $52 million, dealing in approximate figures. How are we doing in Indonesia today?

Mr. CALDWELL. I think right now we are doing a whole lot less now in Indonesia. We cut off all our foreign aid programs for a while except in malaria control.

Mr. GROSS. For the money we have spent there, and these contracts apparently run over a period of years and go back several years, we haven't done very well in Indonesia have we?

Mr. CALDWELL. I think right now, Mr. Gross, we are doing very well in Indonesia at this moment. 

Mr. GROSS. Why do you say that?

Mr. CALDWELL. I think the Communists are not doing very well there. 

Mr. GROSS. Is there a stable government in Indonesia?

Mr. CALDWELL. No. 

Mr. GROSS. Do we recognize and give aid to this government today in Indonesia? There is something wrong with it or we would be in there still ladling out the money, wouldn't we?

Mr. CALDWELL. Mr. Gross, I really ought not to try to be the Secretary of State and respond Mr. GROSS. All I am trying to get at is, and I wish I could go deeper into this thing, what have we gotten for the hundreds of millions that

have gone out to the educational institutions of this country? What have they accomplished in all these foreign countries? 

I look, for instance, at Ethiopia where we have been with a foreign aid program, plus the Peace Corps. I think the literacy rate in 

Ethiopia is still about 2 percent of the total population, and we have been there for a long time Mr. CALDWELL. These things take a long time, this developing of human competency. It is the point I would like to make, that we can't expect programs to go on for 5 years and suddenly create a whole new race of literate people and competent people. It has taken us generations to develop it in this country ourselves. I just wouldn't want our expectations to outrun the practical facts of life. 

I would not like to say that any of this money that has been spent in Indonesia has necessarily been wasted. We don't know what the payoff is going to be. When the Dutch left Indonesia in 1945 there wasn't a single engineering school or university in that country, not a single one. The only piecemeal engineering school they had was at 

Bandung. They had 100 million people, 250 different dialects in that scattered empire. They had no trained civil servants. The Dutch had run every village right down to the base level. They had no trained civil service. I daresay that Indonesia has done extraordinarily well to operate at all.

Mr. GROSS. The British walked out and left a shell in India. 

Mr. CALDWELL. The British did not leave a shell in India- 

Mr. GROSS. Is it incumbent upon the people of the United States to attempt to police and educate the world? Is this our responsibility?

Mr. CALDWELL. I don't know whether it is our responsibility to them- Mr. GROSS. It may take 190 years or 200 years to bust this country, but apparently we are well on the way to doing it. 

Mr. CALDWELL. Mr. Gross, I think it is in the interest of the United 

States of America to have a prosperous India. It is certainly not in our interest to have a hungry and starving India.

Mr. GROSS. First of all, it is in our interest to have a prosperous 

United States.

Mr. CALDWELL. We universities believe in that, Mr. Gross. If the 

U.S. Government wishes to help, this is the decision of the Congress of the United States and the Executive. If they wish to help Indonesia or India or Peru or any other nation to develop human competency in that nation, my purpose here today is to say that the universities of this land want to help do it and we think we can. 

Mr. GROSS. I hope the taxpayers can afford it because they are paying the bills.

Mr. CALDWELL. We would like to do it as efficiently as possible. We don't want to waste any taxpayers' money, Mr. Gross.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Cameron. 

Mr. CAMERON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I am delighted to have you here, Chancellor Caldwell, and am deeply impressed with your knowledge of the foreign assistance program over the years. 

In your opening statement you mentioned that your organization represents 97 State and land-grant colleges and universities. I wonder

would it be possible for you to submit for the record a listing of all the schools that are members of your particular organization? 

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes; we will.

Mr. CAMERON. I would like to have a little more in-depth information with respect to the procedures whereby-which could also be submitted for the record-whereby you represent the organizations and how they formulated the opinions specifically in respect to a 5-year authorization. 

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes, sir; I could make that comment right now if you would like me to.

We have a committee on international affairs, of which I am chairman now. Our procedure 

Mr. CAMERON. This is one of the subcommittees of the 

Mr. CALDWELL. Of the association.

Our committee has about 30 members on it from the ranks of the deans of agriculture, the deans of international affairs, and the presidents.

We make an analysis of a piece of legislation such as this bill and its companion bill in the association senate. We take this bill to the senate, not necessarily this specific bill, but take our general position to the senate of the association, which consists of all the presidents of the association and certain other representatives from the institutions, and they debate and resolve on the general position of the association. 

Then our committee on the basis of that general guideline, we formulate the specific position of the association and we write to our members for reactions.

Mr. CAMERON. This committee subsequently writes for reactions. 

Is this done in an annual convention? 

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes, sir, in November.

Mr. CAMERON. The committee continues to work during the interim period? 

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes, sir.

Mr. CAMERON. I notice in your statement, a statement from Dr. W. 

Robert Parks of Iowa State University. Dr. Parks is in general agreement with the 5-year authorizations? 

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes.

Mr. CAMERON. Iowa State University has contracts that are presently operating? 

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes, sir.

Mr. CAMERON. What are the general areas? 

Mr. CALDWELL. They are operating in Peru, which has to do with the land economics of Peru. Their department of agricultural economics is an extraordinarily good one and the agency is using some of their people in the whole land program of Peru which their government is undertaking.

Mr. CAMERON. Iowa State University like most State universities is funded by the legislature and under the control of the people of the State of Iowa through the State legislature? 

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes, sir. 

Mr. CAMERON. They do support the program? 

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes, sir.

Mr. CAMERON. Thank you very much, sir.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Roybal. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Are the universities having difficulty recruiting capable people at the present time?

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes, sir, everybody is in short supply in all of the ranks of the trained professional people in the country. 

Mr. ROYBAL. This is due to the short supply of people, or is it due to the length of time which a prospective employee can be assured employment?

Mr. CALDWELL. I think it is a combination of two things. We don't have enough people trained in the United States to perform the tasks we have undertaken. What we have on hand we try to use to cover as much ground as possible. 

The staffing of any of these projects is an intricate procedure-how much time we want to assign to one plant pathologist to work on some problems in Brazil or wherever it might be and that he can take away from his own projects back home-how many graduate students he can have assisting him and who he will leave in charge if he goes down there for 1 year, and whether we have enough depth in the department to utilize the manpower. 

Mr. ROYBAL. The 5-year authorization helps, in your opinion?

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes. What we are having to do also, we want to attract even more graduate students into this kind of work. We feel that there is going to be a great deal of commercial, private as well as public, involvement in international business and so on. We are trying to develop more depth. This is one way to do it, attract more people into making themselves available for this kind of service. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Thank you. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Chancellor Caldwell. 

On behalf of the committee we want to express our appreciation for your excellent statement. You have been a very fine witness. We appreciate it very much. 

Mr. CALDWELL. Thank you.

Mr. FOUNTAIN. Dr. Caldwell just one more question before an observation. As I have said earlier I can readily understand why a longterm authorization or long-term preparation in those areas in which you are called upon to give assistance would be extremely beneficial. 

Has your committee or group given serious consideration to the other aspects of the foreign aid bill with a view of forming an opinion concerning the long-term authorization? 

Mr. CALDWELL. Do you mean development loans?

Mr. FOUNTAIN. Yes. 

Mr. CALDWELL. We have not.

Mr. FOUNTAIN. Your testimony is limited to the area in which you are called to give assistance?

Mr. CALDWELL. Yes, as we are concerned as institutions, not on the policies of the foreign aid bill. 

Mr. FOUNTAIN. I want to join the chairman in saying that you have not only shown yourself extremely knowledgeable but you have been a highly competent witness. I am proud of you as chancellor of the North Carolina State University. 

Mr. CALDWELL. Thank you.
