Mr. ZABLOCKI. Our next witness is Mr. John H. Eberly, Washing ton representative, Church of the Brethren General Brotherhood Board. 

You have a prepared statement, Mr. Eberly.

STATEMENT OF JOHN H. EBERLY, THE GENERAL BROTHERHOOD BOARD, CHURCH OF THE BRETHREN 

Mr. EBERLY. Yes; with your permission I would like to read it. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. If you will proceed, sir.

Mr. EBERLY. I do represent a church, and the statement I am reading is what I believe is appropriate for a church to present. Though 

I would not want you to think that we are limited only to religious

platitudes, and I would like to take this opportunity to compliment the two previous speakers for we ourselves have had some experience in agricultural exchanges and we place a very high estimate on what the agricultural colleges are doing. 

I am John H. Eberly, Washington representative for the Church of the Brethren, speaking in behalf of the Brethren Service Commission of the general brotherhood board of our denomination. 

The Church of the Brethren has, in the past, and continues in the present, to give emphasis to the belief that the Christian is one who demonstrates his faith primarily through service and through labors of good will and helpfulness. This position is taken from the Scripture which says that one cannot love God whom he has not seen without loving his brother whom he has seen.

The Church of the Brethren since World War II has participated in many programs expressing this Christian concern for the needy people of the world. Among these programs have been material aid, volunteer service workers, both among youth and adults, and several forms of exchange programs. These are the high school exchange, college exchange, and particularly one which we designate the Polish 

Agricultural Exchange. 

This latter program, at the present time, has in the United States 18 Polish agriculturalists and one from Bulgaria-men and women with graduate degrees, who are serving in practical research within our land-grant colleges or in actual work projects in agriculturally related businesses.

We have found the agricultural exchange a very excellent kind of technical sharing with the people of other nations and have been gratified to find it well supported by those in our own country whose assistance is needed to carry on the program. 

Many Brethren have had the opportunity of working directly in some assistance program abroad. We have had young people, and older specialists, serving in Europe, in the Middle and Far East, and in Africa. We have also had Brethren missionaries active in agricultural missions and in other aspects of village improvement carried on in our mission fields of India and Africa. From this background of experience and from the opportunity of observing the work of our own Government and others in aid, we wish to speak in strong support of the principle of economic assistance.

We are glad to see that the AID program now before the Congress has separated economic from military aid. While we have had difficulty in feeling that military aid qualifies to be aid, it is not our purpose primarily to speak against that, but rather to urge a greater emphasis on economic assistance to the needy people in the world. 

Material and physical need is on the increase in the world. This situation is compounded by the acceleration of population. We are aware of the threatened famine in India. Poverty, hunger, ignorance, and ill health can be found in every nation in the world and in some it dominates the social order. These are ills that can be cured and we would hope that the United States will see its role of leadership in the world increasingly in respect to these ills. 

The record that our international aid has made from the days of the Marshall plan until now contributes some of the thrilling stories

of history. We are not unaware of many of the justifiable criticisms that have been heaped on the AID program, but we believe in the greater validity of the success stories which are well known. 

Certainly we want to decrease the failures and the weaknesses of the program and increase the efficiency and the success of our foreign aid. 

The Brethren would like to join in all that the Government does to relieve the present suffering of the needy people of the world and in making long-range plans to help people to become self-sufficient and free from handouts. We are reminded of a statement in the Bible that should apply to the United States at this time in history which was spoken of an individual who had come "into the Kingdom for such a time as this."

We do not doubt the sensitivity of our Government for the role that the United States should take in a troubled world. We believe that the political and social instability which we would control primarily by military power, needs to be seen in the light of economic need. 

The policy of increasing our leadership in agricultural and industrial development for the needy countries of the world and at the same time decreasing our dependence on huge military reserves, needs to be more urgently considered and followed. There may have been a time when man engaged in political and military activity for its own sake, but these have now become subordinate to man's need for economic security.

We, therefore, urge that the Congress take steps to vigorously increase our understanding and our efforts to build up the institutions that will assure the nations of the world of better and more food, lodging, education, and medical care.

We wish to commend the Agency for International Development in its new initiatives in agriculture, education, and health. This begins to look like planned and controlled assistance as against a simple dole. 

It also indicates a kind of partnership between people who "have" with those who "have not."

The important thing in aid is the manner in which it is given and administered. Aid that is needed can never be wrong except in the way it is given. We urge our Congress and the administration to pay special attention to the necessity to be moral in giving. Aid must be given to the people who need help for the purpose of helping them. 

Any other reason will encourage misuse and corruption in an aid program. It could well be that the instances in which foreign aid has been corrupted can be blamed on a faulty method and motive in its giving. 

For this reason the United States needs to give serious attention to aid given unilaterally for the purpose of supporting its foreign policy. This runs the risk of moral violation and is self-defeating. 

You cannot "win friends and influence people" by exploiting need.

Assistance given for this purpose will fail, but given for the purpose of helping people, it will return manifold blessings to the giver. 

We should, therefore, consider channeling more of our assistance through such programs as the World Bank, U.N. agencies and private organizations. Our reluctance to do so is evidence of our insistence to make AID do something else. It is not the position of this paper that unilateral aid is of itself wrong. It is only that the temptation and risk of unilateral aid is more nearly avoided in multilateral programs.

In conclusion, we should remember that the real strength of a nation lies in its productive capacity. If this productive capacity is used to save lives and contribute to the welfare of mankind, it is a blessing, and history will record it with praise. If the productive capacity of a nation is used primarily for any other purpose except that which builds cities and institutions necessary for man's well-being, then history will record another failure in civilizations. We support the foreign aid program as aid to people in need and urge that it be expanded and as carefully administered as it possibly can be. 

We thank the committee for this opportunity to present testimony. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Eberly, for a fine statement. 

I believe you touch upon a very vital observation in your last sentence when you say "foreign aid programs should be carefully administered." In having multilateral programs is the administration of aid to a country apt to be more carefully administered or less carefully administered than a bilateral program? 

To restate the problem, don't you find it desirable on the part of the United States to have some supervision on the end use of our aid-to see to it, for example, that our aid and assistance is not used against other countries?

Mr. EBERLY. Mr. Chairman, I am thinking more in terms of motive and reason in the administration and the giving of aid rather than actually in the mechanics of it. I don't believe I would be qualified to say that multilaterally administered aid would be better administered than unilaterally as far as the mechanics are concerned. I think it would be accepted by those who receive it in a different light. 

This would not always be true, but I am speaking in generalities. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Then in general it is my understanding that many of the recipient countries would prefer to receive assistance not on a grant basis but on a long-term loan basis because they have an honor and respect for themselves and they find that they will be able to help themselves if they are not dependent upon the giver or the giving country for any length of time. 

Would you care to comment on that point?

Mr. EBERLY. We would agree with you very much there. In our work as one of the private agencies we make a great deal of the selfhelp principle.

We do believe, I think it is fair to say, that any of the faults that you find in any welfare program related to "charity" will apply in international aid just as well as it does to the individual. Countries and individuals both can be harmed more than helped by welfare and by aid unless it is properly and carefully done and administered. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Eberly. 

Mr. Whalley?

Mr. WALLEY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Eberly, are the churches and their people in Pennsylvania aware of your statement?

Mr. EBERLY. They are not in Pennsylvania but it is cleared with our headquarters at Elgin, Ill. It probably will be given some publicity, yes.

Mr. WHALLEY. In Pennsylvania? 

Mr. EBERLY. Yes; because we have a large membership in Pennsylvania.

Mr. WHALLEY. I say that for the reason that so many people in 

Pennsylvania, and I think many of them in your church, are apparently against the foreign aid program. It might be helpful for you to let them know your thinking. 

Thank you very much.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Cameron? 

Mr. CAMERON. Following up that point, Mr. Eberly, that Mr. Whalley just made, what is the specific auspices that you are speaking for as far as the Brethren Churches are concerned, what are the procedures taken that authorize you to make this statement on behalf of the entire membership?

Mr. EBERLY. I am representing the general brotherhood board this morning through the Brethren Service Commission. This statement has been cleared with the executive secretary of the Brethren Service Commission and the general secretary of the general brotherhood board. 

Mr. CAMERON. You are specifically authorized then on behalf of the hierarchy of the Brethren Church- 

Mr. EBERLY. No, I only speak for the leadership of the organization. 

Mr. CAMERON. This does not necessarily then reflect the view of each of the Brethren Churches?

Mr. EBERLY. Not necessarily, no. It reflects what is the leadership of the church. 

Mr. CAMERON. Thank you very much.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Gross. 

Mr. GROSS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

They may be divorced on paper but they are not really divorced because of the fact that economic aid, in the view of many members of this committee and the Congress, would not stand on its own feet. In other words economic aid has to be carried by the military assistance. Make no mistake about it, the two are joined. I don't believe you can take the satisfaction that your statement indicates Mr. EBERLY. I am aware of that, Mr. Gross. I have testified before the committee before and we faced this. I think perhaps I am taking some, having some confidence in the fact that they are printed on different booklets this year and different paper. 

Mr. GROSS. I think that is about the extent of it.

Mr. ROYBAL. No questions.

Mr. W. B. Hicks, Jr., executive secretary of the Liberty Lobby.

Mr. Hicks is accompanied by Mr. Clifford Sandahl, public relations director for the Liberty Lobby. 

You have a prepared statement, if you will proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF W. B. HICKS, JR., EXECUTIVE SECRETARY, LIBERTY LOBBY Mr. HICKS. Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I am W. B. Hicks, Jr., executive secretary of Liberty Lobby. I appear on behalf of the 185,000 subscribers to our legislative report, "Liberty Letter." 60-929-66-pt. 6- -3

Liberty Lobby is well aware of the tremendous demands that are made on the time and attention of this committee. As the role of the 

United States in world affairs has expanded, the role of this committee has grown in direct ratio.

Liberty Lobby is just one of the many citizen's groups and economic interests that come before you every year to present their view of the foreign aid program, although we are one of the few groups who do so with no thought of monetary gain for ourselves. Nevertheless, we recognize that your annual consideration of foreign aid is a trying task, and largely a thankless one. 

Under these conditions it is understandable that you might consider a bill such as H.R. 12449, which provides a 5-year authorization by which you can turn over your responsibilities in the troublesome area of foreign aid to the administration for a period of 5 years years, during which you can turn your attentions to other questions of wars and international organizations without concerning yourselves with foreign aid.

But Liberty Lobby asks: Why must this important issue of foreign aid be turned over to the nameless, nonelected officials of the administration? Are there no elected representatives of the people who are willing and have the time to take the responsibility for full and fair consideration of an expenditure of the American people's money that regularly exceeds $3,000 million every year? 

And what of the traditional congressional practice of voting publicly on the authorization of such expenditures? Are the people to be denied the right to measure the performance of their elected represenatives by the way they vote on this important issue?

A vote on annual appropriations alone is no substitute for a full and fair hearing and a full debate on the foreign aid policies of the Nation.

There are many questionable aspects of our foreign aid policy. 

Does foreign aid lead us into wars? This question should be answered.

There is evidence that it leads the recipient nations into wars, such as 

Pakistan and India. 

Has our military aid program been successful? Responsible experts such as those who form the Citizens' Foreign Aid Committee have concluded that our efforts to build a military deterrent have increased the danger of war, as in Turkey and Greece, while leaving our own strategic striking force as the only real deterrent to Soviet ambitions.

Has our economic aid helped to advance civilizations? The terribly slow progress of the backward areas seems to indicate otherwise. 

Indeed, our economic aid to such nations appears to be only a narcotic to which they become addicted, so that even a one-time exporting, food-rich nation like Indonesia cannot survive without it, once addicted. 

Have we authorized too much money for foreign aid? The fact that over $5 billion of funds appropriated in the past still remain unspent seems to indicate that past authorizations have been excessive. Two years ago, Liberty Lobby called for a study of these unspent funds before this committee, and we still think that such a study is needed. Until that study is made, there should be no new authorizations for foreign aid.

Should foreign aid be administered by international lending organizations, who will be neither responsive nor responsible to the American people? Liberty Lobby thinks not, for what we think is a very good reason, which applies to the entire concept of H.R. 12449. 

Foreign aid who gets it, and how much-is the people's business. 

The people pay for it. There are many questionable aspects to our foreign aid program, but there is no question at all about this undeniable fact: Congress is responsible for foreign aid, and if the Congress in its wisdom decides to put an end to foreign aid, it should do so; but the Congress should not and cannot solve the problem of foreign aid simply by concealing it for 5 years. 

In summary, Liberty Lobby recommends- 

(1) No new authorization for foreign aid until all previous appropriations are accounted for, (2) That this committee continue to authorize foreign aid only on an annual basis, and (3) That in the event this committee feels that it can no longer devote itself to a full and fair annual consideration of foreign aid, it should recommend to the House that a new committee be formed for that purpose. 

Thank you. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Hicks.

The Liberty Lobby is not opposed to foreign aid per se? 

Mr. Hicks. In general, sir, we are opposed to economic aid as it has been conducted in the past. Our board of policy has voted on this subject overwhelmingly to be opposed to the foreign aid program. 

In the past, however, we have not made specific opposition to the military aid program of the United States. This is the first year in which we have brought this into question. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Your recommendations are, however, that no new authorization for foreign aid be made until all previous appropriations are accounted for. You are aware that there is a pipeline? 

Mr. HICKS. Yes, sir. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. That there are funds obligated but unspent, obligated? 

Mr. HICKS. Yes, sir. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. We cannot use them for another purpose.

Mr. HICKS. We feel that this limitation is self-imposed on the 

Congress by itself. There is no reason why they cannot be redrawn into consideration and reconsidered. If their purpose no longer exists, surely there is no reason for leaving them in the pipeline. This is our feeling on it, sir. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Let me restate the question so as to be clear in my mind, at least, on the position of the Liberty Lobby I do agree with your point (2), that is, continue the annual authorization, but as to the desirability of continuing foreign aid, I differ with what appears to be your conclusion or your recommendation.

If all previously authorized funds which are in the pipeline can be accounted for as firmly obligated, would the Liberty Lobby be opposed to a further authorization for foreign aid?

Mr. HICKS. It having been discovered that the existing appropriations in the pipeline are still in the process of being used for the original purpose, that the purpose has not been eradicated by circumstances

or something like this, then I feel that Liberty Lobby would nevertheless still have to oppose on principle the foreign aid idea as it is currently being exercised by the Congress; yes, sir.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. May I ask a specific question: Is the Liberty Lobby opposed to our aid to feed India in its present state of starvation? 

Mr. HICKS. We have taken no vote of our board of policy on that, so we have no position. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Do you have a personal view? 

Mr. HICKS. Yes, sir. I feel like it is going to be largely wasted because it has come to my attention in the studies I have made of it, which have not been extensive I must comment, but on the surface it is apparent that India's problem is not one solely of a lack of food supply but a lack of a means of transporting food supply, a means of distributing food supply, and a means of deciding who shall get food supplies. So that just by providing a billion tons of grain for India, we don't necessarily prevent the specter of impending famine. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Very good. Granting that your observation is correct, that there is some waste, in the final analysis do you think it is desirable on the part of the United States to improve India's transportation system and its food distribution system? Should we or should we not assist India so that she can, in the future, feed her people, or does the Liberty Lobby-you are not prepared- 

Mr. HICKS. I cannot speak- 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Do you personally think we should allow starvation? 

Mr. HICKS. I think personally, sir, that we have no choice. It is not up to us to say whether we should permit starvation or not. 

Apparently all the indications are that starvation is going to occur. 

It is not a case of whether we would like to see it occur. Of course we wouldn't like to see it occur. There is no way we can prevent it as far as I can see from my limited knowledge of the situation. So 

I would not want to be put on record as saying that I thought we should prevent it when I know it is impossible to do. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Let me ask this question: In past years we have had our storage bins filled, Liberty ships filled with cereal, wheat, oats, becoming spoiled and vermin infested while there were people in need. Oftentimes, the very cost of storage exceeded the cost to distribute the food and allow people to avail themselves of our plenty. 

Could we in good conscience not heed the need of other people in the various areas of the world for food, under these circumstances? 

Mr. HICKS. Under the circumstances which you have just described, I would say if there is some means of alleviating suffering which can be done without the long-range commitment of the American people involving their own well-being, that there is no reason why we should not make the effort, that is, provided that there is the possibility that it works and the capability that we could do this without injuring our own well-being, which, after all, should be the first consideration of any peoples, then I would see, personally, no objection.

Again I say I cannot speak for Liberty Lobby because we have no position.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. I understand. 

I presume you include in this position not only our agricultural surpluses but also passing on our technical know-how to assist other people when it did not injure our own security and well-being?

Mr. HICKS. If the necessary prerequisite conditions did prevail that 

I just used in example with the food, I would say yes. However, I would like to point out that the testimony of a previous witness was to the effect that we are limited in our own technical know-how in this Nation. There is some question in my mind as to just how much technical know-how we have to export. 

I am just raising this as a question because I have no direct knowledge, but a previous witness did bring it up. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Everything on this God's earth is relative. What we have may be so much more than others have, but I do detect, if I may say so Mr. Hicks, you are not really completely opposed to foreign assistance?

Mr. HICKS. Sir, I would like to point out that the past history of the American people has been one of great generosity in the field of foreign aid. I call to the committee's attention the experience of former President Hoover, who, at a time when millions of people were starving in the Soviet Union, went to their assistance with the full backing of the American people, and as I recall without any extensive aid from the U.S. Government per se, managed to prevent. at least some 5 million persons from starving during a famine in the 

Soviet Union.

This is the type of foreign aid which I am sure would be undertaken by more individuals and groups in this Nation if the Federal Government would leave them the necessary excess in their pocketbooks after taxes to undertake such charitable projects. 

This comment may not have been necessarily called for but this is a fact, that the charitable instincts of the American people are being driven out by the high taxation and inflationary policies of the Government. We feel that under proper circumstances in this country the people would carry out these great charitable enterprises with a whole heart and good intentions were they given the chance, rather than to have charity legislated. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. If I may just comment on your last observation:

From testimony received by this committee even if foreign aid were completely discontinued, it wouldn't relieve much of the tax burden on the taxpayer. 

I do want to say that you have proposed some challenging questions in your statement. The one that I find most significant is whether this committee will abdicate its responsibilities to make annual reviews and authorizations. 

Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

I have enjoyed your testimony very much. I am particularly interested in your last question, No. 3, "in the event this committee feels that it can no longer devote itself to a full and fair annual consideration of foreign aid it should recommend to the House that a new committee be formed for that purpose." י.

I think all of the members of the committee, both the majority and the minority, have been very concerned because a superficial study is all we can possibly make. I know on our side of the aisle we are trying to figure out some ways by which these studies could be made more in depth, possibly members of the committee being sent out to

see for themselves what was going on in the different AID centers and so on, and evaluate it for ourselves. We have had some of the members go out and come back with some important material which has changed the viewpoint in some degree. 

I would like to ask you what sort of a committee do you visualize? 

You say a new committee be formed for that purpose. What kind of committee could it be?

Mr. HICKS. Mrs. Bolton, in the first place, I would like to say this was a rhetorical question because it never entered into my mind that the members of this committee would wish to abdicate their power to review annually the foreign aid authorizaion. 

Mrs. BOLTON. If you will yield for a moment, I am delighted in your choice of words "review annually." Some of us feel strongly about that, too.

Mr. HICKS. The question was posed, however, as the only alternative to an annual review by this committee, which would be acceptable to Liberty Lobby. It is not in my power to say what sort of committee this should be but I should think in this House of 435 Members who have such wide and ranging interests, that it should be possible to assemble a group of experts in economics and foreign trade who could take the foreign aid program of the United States on an annual basis and by using such techniques as foreign travel, to check up on the facts, come to conclusions, which would more than repay any efforts that they had to go to in terms of lessening the burden of foreign aid and achieving better results with it. 

Mrs. BOLTON. That is very interesting. Would you tell us a little. something more perhaps it has already been said, Mr. Chairman; if so I will read it-just what is the Liberty Lobby, where does it hold forth, and what is it? 

Mr. HICKS. Our address is 132 Third Street SE., and the Liberty Lobby is a rather unique organization in that we have no personification, there is no one individual who represents the Liberty Lobby. It was started by a group of people some 6 years ago, concerned, conservative Americans, who felt there should be a joint representative on Capitol Hill to speak for the conservative viewpoint. 

And to that end we have started publishing a newsletter which covers legislative topics. This newsletter today has a circulation of 185,000 persons who pay $1 a year subscription price.

For a long period of time the persons who formed the original board of policy of Liberty Lobby-there were some 47 of them--took votes every 2 or 3 years on specific issues on which Liberty Lobby took a stand and this year in January we announced the formation of a new board of policy which we opened up to all the readers of the Liberty Letter, that if they wished to become members of this board of policy, they were allowed to cast a ballot, which we printed up in full form, offering them some 36 issues to express their opinion on. 

Since that time we have added two new issues, constitutional issues, that came before the Congress on which we had to have a vote. So now we have I believe 38 issues on which we represent our readership. 

We are supported wholly by the subscription rates and contributions of the 185,000. 

Mrs. BOLTON. That is a very interesting group. I think I haven't had any real contacts with any of you. I would be very happy to have

some in the year ahead before we have to consider again the foreign aid programs.

The matter of the one year I have already expressed myself about, and I think I feel a little confused about your recommendation that all previous appropriations must be accounted for. We would like to see that, too, but there are so many sides of these matters, also there are great difficulties in carrying them out. I am sure you appreciate that. 

I want to thank you very much, Mr. Hicks. 

Mr. HICKS. Thank you, Mrs. Bolton. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Cameron?

Mr. CAMERON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Hicks, the organization you refer to on page 2, the Citizens' 

Foreign Aid Committee, that is the committee which Gen. Bonner 

Fellers is head of? 

Mr. HICKS. Yes.

Mr. CAMERON. Isn't the total title "The Citizens' Foreign Aid Com mittee Against Foreign Aid"? 

Mr. HICKS. Not to my knowledge, sir. 

Mr. CAMERON. Isn't that the way their letterhead reads in the small print underneath, "Against foreign aid"? 

Mr. HICKS. It may, but I can't answer the question. 

Mr. CAMERON. Have you ever known that organization to support foreign aid? 

Mr. HICKS. If I am not mistaken, their position in the past has been similar to ours in that they supported by acquiescence the military portion of the foreign aid bill, but opposed the economic. 

Mr. CAMERON. So the name is a bit misleading when it says "Citizens' Foreign Aid Committee," wouldn't you say? The implication would be that it is in support of foreign aid rather than in opposition to it. Is one of the basic principles of the Liberty Lobby, or one of the things upon which you have taken a vote, the repeal of the Federal income tax?

Mr. HICKS. At one time it was, sir. This year we broadened the phraseology so that our board this time voted on equality of taxation, leaving us more room to operate within the income tax area. 

We have in mind when possible a drive to eliminate some of the inequities that exist in the foreign aid program and of course- 

Mr. CAMERON. Foreign aid program? 

Mr. HICKS. Income tax; excuse me. In the future if the opportunity presents itself where it would appear clearly that to repeal the income tax would be one way of achieving tax equality amongst American citizens, then we might again appear to support Mr. CAMERON. Some 6 years ago, or whenever the organization was founded, this was the basic tenet upon which it was founded, the repealing of the Federal income tax.

Mr. HICKS. No; you are thinking of the Freedom Amendment Com mittee, I believe, whose major and only issue is the repeal of the in come tax-the Liberty Amendment Committee. 

Mr. CAMERON. I am thinking of a diatribe that you put out against 

President Johnson last year. This is one of the issues that was in there. On your third point with respect to this committee abandoning and a new committee being appointed, you say that in the event this

committee feels it can no longer devote itself to the full and fair annual consideration of the foreign aid, et cetera, "can no longer devote itself to full and fair," that would imply that we have in the past devoted ourselves to full and fair annual consideration; is that your position? 

Mr. HICKS. Yes, sir; it is. 

Mr. CAMERON, Then our consideration has been fair? 

Mr. HICKS. Yes, sir; absolutely. 

Mr. CAMERON. Thank you, Mr. Hicks. 

No more questions.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Gross? 

Mr. GROSS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Hicks, I want to commend you for your statement and say that the food proposition on which you were questioned rather closely a little while ago is a Public Law 480 matter which is not handled by this committee. However, since the subject was brought up, I wonder if you agree that it would be utterly impossible for the farmers of this Nation to feed all the hungry people of the world? Always

India is referred to as though it is the only country where there are hungry people. As a cold matter of fact, not everyone in this country gets all the food they want to eat at all times. 

Do you agree that it would be utterly impossible for the United 

States to attempt to feed the hungry people of the world?

Mr. HICKS. On the statements of responsible experts as represented in an article recently published in U.S. News & World Report, it is quite obvious that the United States, Canada, and Europe together could not feed all the hungry people outside the Iron Curtain countries much less those who were within the Iron Curtain countries. 

Certainly it would be true that if the United States, Canada, and Europe couldn't do it, then the United States could not do it alone. 

Mr. GROSS. With respect to the foreign aid pipeline, you were being conservative as to the amount of money in the pipeline. We had testimony a short while ago that it is $6 billion-plus in the pipeline. There isn't any reason why those who administer the program can't deobligate; isn't that true?

Mr. HICKS. As far as I can see, sir, the Congress could bring this about. That is the reason why we asked for it.

Mr. GROSS. Congress could do it. They already have the authority and do deobligate and reobligate in the program. There isn't any reason why they can't pull this pipeline down and should pull it down. 

Perhaps the best way would be to cut this authorization and see to it that they do pull down the pipeline to size. It is unconscionable that there be $6 to $7 billion in the pipeline. 

I commend you for your statement, particularly in that respect. 

That is all, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Hicks.

WEDNESDAY, MAY 11, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:20 a.m., in room 

2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

Chairman MORGAN. The committee will come to order. 

The committee meets this morning in open session on a continuation of the hearings on the Foreign Aid bills for the fiscal year 1967, H.R. 

12449 and H.R. 12450.

Our first witness this morning is the Honorable Charles E. Bennett, Member of Congress from the State of Florida. 

Charley, it is a real pleasure and honor to welcome you here. You have a prepared statement. You may proceed.

STATEMENT OF HON. CHARLES E. BENNETT, A REPRESENTATIVE IN CONGRESS FROM THE STATE OF FLORIDA 

Mr. BENNETT. I do, sir. Thank you very much.

Mr. Chairman, I appreciate this opportunity to appear on H.R. 

12449, H.R. 12450, and H.R. 53.

I am pleased to note that the economic and military assistance programs have been separated into two bills, a policy which I have supported for many years. I hope that this division of the two phases of our foreign aid program will lead to greater economy and efficiency in the operation of our assistance programs, bringing closer harmony between our Government and the governments and the peoples of the countries we help with our aid.

Over the last decade or more I have advocated that the military portion of the aid program be placed in the Department of Defense's annual request to Congress and that this request be worked upon by the appropriate authorization and appropriations committees in the defense field in the House and the Senate. 

It is my belief that all items in the two bills should be listed as line items, allowing a closer look at what the taxpayer's money is going for. 

I would also hope that there would be an expiration date placed on all authorized projects, just as there is in the military construction legislation which annually comes before the defense committees and the 

Congress for consideration. The best possible staff and committee specialists should evaluate each military item with the same critical scrutiny given such line items for our own armed services. 927

Mr. Chairman, I would like to recommend for consideration my bill, H.R. 53, either by amendment to the bill before you or otherwise, which provides that U.S. payments to the United Nations shall not be used for programs contrary to the policies of the United States. 

In the present year, and for the last several years, the United States has contributed over 40 percent of the cost of maintaining the United 

Nations Special Fund, now under the U.N. Development Program title. 

This program, as you know, is to help economic and social development in the less developed countries. 

The essence of my bill would be to cut off our aid to those nations who are hoping and planning to bury us. In January of this year, the United Nations Special Fund approved a project at the University of Havana to train engineers and other technical persons, costing $2,096,500. Castro's Communist teachers, many of whom are Russians, were handed this money to carry out a program certain to undermine our efforts to keep the Western Hemisphere free from Castro and 

Russian totalitarianism. Our representatives at the United Nations objected to this, and voted against it, but it did no good. Just 2 years ago, the U.N. Special Fund also approved a grant to help Castro's ailing economy, which cost U.S. taxpayers $500,000.

In the last 4 years, Cambodia has received almost $3 million from the U.N. Special Fund for various projects, which cost U.S. taxpayers $1.2 million. The most recent grant was for $611,900 in January 

1965. This is the same country which is led by Prince Sihanouk, who said on March 23, 1966, in announcing that Cambodia had given the 

Vietcong a shipment of rice- *** we have given this aid to them by closing our eyes because we and the Vietcong have a common enemy, which is U.S. imperialism. 

Foreign relations expert and columnist C. L. Sulzberger of the 

New York Times reported only last week that Cambodia is a sanctuary and supply source for the Vietcong on such a scale that the Pnompenh Government must know it. From the extreme south to Laos in the north, Cambodia is violating its proclaimed neutrality. 

I am hopeful the committee will seriously consider my bill as an amendment to the foreign aid authorization legislation. These are good examples of how our money is being used to perpetuate our enemies. 

Mr. Chairman, I am hopeful the committee will act favorably on my suggestions. The_foreign aid program in many respects has been good, but today, I see where there has been waste and misdirected funds to those countries who are plainly not our friends. We need to do everything we can to present a clear and concise foreign aid program to keep the peace and preserve freedom around the world. 

Thank you for the opportunity to appear before the committee. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Bennett.

Mr. Bennett, I believe you are a member of the Armed Forces Committee? 

Mr. BENNETT. Yes, sir. 

Chairman MORGAN. As you know, there has been discussion over a period of several years about the separation of economic aid and military aid. I see you recommend that all military assistance should be referred to your committee; is that correct?

Mr. BENNETT. I would prefer it. I didn't stress it very much in my statement to you. I would prefer it because their staff is a staff which is primarily designed to bring information to the committee from a background of military information. 

Chairman MORGAN. Of course, you know that there is no military assistance in this present bill for South Vietnam-that already has been transferred to the DOD budget. This present bill doesn't contain any military assistance for South Vietnam. 

Mr. BENNETT. It gives it to other countries.

Chairman MORGAN. Yes. 

Mr. BENNETT. What I have said is all the more important as to other countries. 

Chairman MORGAN. I see you make a recommendation that all military assistance should be under the DOD-the money for military aid to Vietnam for 1967 is in the Department of Defense budget. 

Mr. BENNETT. That may take part of the objection with regard to that one country. There are other countries receiving military aid. 

I do not think any country should receive military aid on any less stringent terms than our own forces receive military aid. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Bennett, I am very sympathetic to your questioning of the fact that some U.S. technical assistance money goes to help Communist Castro's Cuban government. As you know, this is part of a fund of over $100 million. We pay 40 percent as you said. 

But the other $60 million includes money paid in by some of the Communist countries. As you know, Russia makes a contribution. 

Mr. BENNETT. She makes pledges. 

Chairman MORGAN. She makes payments. If we were to go up there and say we don't want one dime of ours spent out of this fund for any Communist country, don't you think that would be a kiss of death for the United Nations technical assistance fund?

Mr. BENNETT. I think it might be an improvement if it were, if that is the way the fund is going to be run. I do not think the U.S. taxpayers ought to have to fund injurious to their own sons and their own country, whether in the United Nations organization or otherwise. 

I think under such circumstances we should remove ourselves from this fund. After all, we are not compelled to contribute to this fund. 

We could remove ourselves from this fund. 

Chairman MORGAN. We could. If we removed ourselves from the fund it would be quite likely that the United Nations technical assistance fund would no longer be in operation. 

Mr. BENNETT. I think that would be an advantage if they are going to give assistance to our enemies.

Chairman MORGAN. There frequently are times in the U.N. where we are outvoted. Would your suggestion be that we get out of the U.N. or do you support the U.N.?

Mr. BENNETT. I have always supported the existence of the U.N. 

I have sometimes questioned whether we should not bring Russia and China to trial for things that they have done to repudiate the Charter of the U.N. to see if we should oust Russia from the U.N. 

The United States is not required to contribute to all the funds of the U.N. There are many countries in the U.N. who do not contribute to the U.N. funds. As a matter of fact, many of these so-called con-

tributions are nothing but pledges anyway. They don't pay the money. 

We pay the money.

Chairman MORGAN. The U.S. delegates, I think it was during the 

1957 session of the U.N. General Assembly, were the ones that sparked the formation of the United Nations Special Fund. A very prominent former member of this committee, Dr. Judd, was one of the prime movers in establishing the United Nations Special Fund. 

Mr. BENNETT. I am sure Dr. Judd would take the same position that 

I do this morning. 

Chairman MORGAN. As I remember it, he always defended the few

American dollars, if any, that went to Communist countries as one of the liabilities inherent in belonging to an international organization. 

As I said, he was one of the prime movers in trying to get an international organization into operation where we could take some of the burden of foreign aid off the backs of the American taxpayers. This, I imagine, was one of the main reasons for establishing the technical assistance fund.

As you know, there are some strong winds blowing over on the other side of the Capitol in favor of spending more of our aid money through international organizations. I have always been strongly opposed to moving American taxpayers' dollars into international organizations where we have no control over them, just as we have no control in this case. 

I think the United Nations Development Program should be looked at very carefully. I have talked with Paul Hoffman, the Director, on a number of occasions. It hurts me the same as it hurts you if Castro is able to get one single dollar. Whether this comes out of the American dollars, the $40 million, or out of the other $60 million is not very significant. I notice that your figures refer to percentages. 

Mr. BENNETT. Actually, the United States is putting up most of the real money that comes in. 

Chairman MORGAN. How did you figure out that this cost the

Ameircan taxpayers $500,000, and how did you get the figure of $2,965,000? 

Mr. BENNETT. My information came from the Library of Congress under a document dated May 10, 1966, which I would be glad to furnish to the committee if you would like.

You probably wouldn't want to put it in the record. It is very statistical and detailed. If you want to put it in, it will be fine. I will be glad to put it in for information. They prepared the information for me and under no guidelines from me, but just to find out the facts.

Subject: United Nations Aid to Communist Countries. 

On November 22, 1965, the United Nations General Assembly created the U.N. 

Development Program, bringing together two of the United Nations' major sources of economic and social aid-the Expanded Program of Technical Assistance (EPTA), established in 1950, and the Special Fund, set up in 1959. 

From its inception in 1959 until October 31, 1964, the Special Fund provided the following funds for projects approved for Communist countries.¹ Cuba.. Poland.

Of 34 Special Fund projects completed by 1964, only one was in a Communist country. Other projects in Communist countries, however, are still underway.

In the 1965 publication by the Expanded Program of Technical Assistance, 15 Years and 150,000 Skills, Poland was given as an example of a relatively small country program of EPTA ($116,300 for 1965) and as the recipient of the kind of assistance which EPTA extends to a country in a relatively well advanced stage of development. The bulk of the Program in Poland is in fellowships; for example, in specialized aspects of metal, chemical, mineral and engineering industries, labor statistics, agriculture, health, telecommunications, meteorology, internal and international postal services, and the use of atomic energy in industry. Only three experts were provided, for very brief consultant missions in tuberculosis control, language teaching, and health statistics.*

In addition to aid from EPTA and the Special Fund, funds from the regular budgets of the United Nations and the Specialized Agencies are channeled into some Communist countries. (See the enclosed memorandum on U.N. aid to Cuba by Russell J. Leng of the Foreign Affairs Division.) A cooperative effort by the Special Fund, the International Labor Organiza tion, and the Polish Government led to the completion in late 1965 of a management program in Poland.5 (See the enclosed clipping for more details on the 

Polish project.)

A recent example of aid from the United Nations to a Communist country came in January 1966, when the UN Development Program announced that Cuba had been awarded a technological training grant for the University of Havana. 

The project is designed to "strengthen and expand facilities for training engineers and architects" at the University. (See the enclosed clippings from the 

Christian Science Monitor and the New York Times. In addition, several background articles on former U.N. projects to Cuba have been included.) 

ROSELYN R. WAHNER. 

Mr. BENNETT. My feeling about it is even if the United States did take a degree of leadership in establishing a fund, which is now resulting in the Federal taxpayers paying money to our enemies, this is not so sacrosanct that we could not now correct what is bad about it. me there is nothing so sacrosanct in that. To

What I have suggested would not remove us from the U.N. I have not suggested that we be removed from the U.N. Maybe we should. 

But I have no information indicating to me that this would be a constructive step at this juncture of history. I do think it places us in a rather awkward position for us to contribute our taxpayers' dollars to things against our interests which we can escape from paying.

I want to assure you that this has been bothering me personally for a good many years. I am very sympathetic to your views.

STATEMENT OF REX G. BAKER, JR., VICE PRESIDENT, NATIONAL LEAGUE OF INSURED SAVINGS ASSOCIATIONS 

Mr. BAKER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

My name is Rex G. Baker, Jr., and I am vice president of the National League of Insured Savings Associations, a nationwide trade association whose member institutions are insured by the Federal Savings and Loan Insurance Corporation. I am also president of the 

Southwestern Savings Association of Houston, Tex.

I am a part-time consultant to the Agency for International Development, dealing with the problem of home financing in the establishment and organization of savings and loan industries in part of Latin America, and I have just recently been reelected to a third term as first vice president of the Inter-American Savings and Loan Union, and as vice chairman of the Texas Partners in the Alliance with Peru. 

With me is William J. Kerwin, assistant executive director of the national league.

Mr. Chairman, we appreciate the opportunity to appear before this committee to discuss the growing role of thrift institutions in the economic, social, and political development of friendly nations in Latin America, Africa, and Asia. The national league in conjunction with the State Department's Agency for International Development for the past 10 years has made a special effort to promote the development of private thrift and homeownership in these areas and we are pleased to report to you this morning that these efforts are now beginning to return substantial dividends to the nations overseas and to the United States.

As you know, the development of thrift institutions under the Foreign Assistance Act was made a statutory objective with the adoption of the so-called Humphrey amendment in the Foreign Aid 

Act of 1961. You will recall that this provision stated that

The Congress of the United States recognizes the vital role of free enterprise in achieving rising levels of production and standards of living essential to economic progress and development. Accordingly it is declared to be the policy of the United States to encourage the efforts of other countries to increase the flow of international trade, to foster private initiative and competition, to encourage the development and use of cooperatives, credit unions and savings and loan associations * * *.

Enactment of the Humphrey amendment represented a milestone in the oversea thrift program because it reinforced the conviction of many savings and loan industry executives that establishment of private housing and home financing programs was an essential ingredient to economic development. Moreover, these men believed that U.S. investment of relatively small "seed" capital loans in these countries would expedite the development of thrift institutions and thereby assist private homebuilding. 

Mr. Chairman, I am pleased to report to you this morning that there are now 94 savings and loan associations operating in 9 nations of Latin America and in Ethiopia and eastern Nigeria in Africa. 

These associations represent 390,000 savers who have amassed $122 million in savings in a period of less than 5 years. Taken together with AID assistance, the total financial resources of these institu-

tions have been applied to the financing of almost 50,000 homes involving total mortgages of $226 million. 

We submit that this achievement provides convincing evidence that private thrift institutions are, in fact, the key to raising housing standards on a broad front in the underdeveloped part of the world. 

I might say, Mr. Chairman, that a dollar invested in a thrift institution such as a savings and loan association is a dollar that is constantly turned over time and again in the housing economy of a particular nation because as soon as the dollar is repaid, the money is reinvested in new housing. On the other hand, a dollar invested directly from government into a specific housing project is locked into that project or if repaid not necessarily available in the same manner for further home financing purposes.

At the present time the national league is actively working with AID to extend the free savings and loan concept to such countries as Jordan, Thailand, Argentina, Nicaragua, and Brazil and already a savings and loan cooperative association has been established in Pakistan as a result of guidance provided by AID and the national league.

This year the Foreign Assistance Act lays stress on agriculture, education, and public health, all sectors which properly require assistance. Think of the frustration of the young educated emerging middle-income professionals who can find no long-term home financing. We wish to point out that the well-being of an individual is conditioned by all factors affecting his environment, including decent housing.

Unless his shelter is undergoing improvement along with agriculture, health, and education, total development of the nation will be more difficult to achieve. In fact, as the people of the underdeveloped countries achieve better standards of health and education, there will be increasing dissatisfaction with their lack of adequate shelter.

Another factor which adds weight to the argument for greater emphasis on housing is the tremendous urban migration which is accelerating all over the world. It has been estimated that between 

1960 and 1970, 200 million people will move into the cities of Africa, Asia, and Latin America. This, in addition to the normal rates of population increases, will inundate the cities of the world in our generation.

For too long, housing construction for the masses has been left up to government in the underdeveloped countries and, unfortunately, these countries do not have the capital and resources to supply this need. But the U.S. Government through the foreign assistance program has shown a number of countries how to marshal local capital for long-term financing of housing. We members of the national league feel that efforts under the foreign assistance program to develop credit institutions have had a threefold result: 

1. The Government and private sector working together as a team has proven to be an effective vehicle to provide long-term credit at reasonable rates of interest for housing. 

2. The American Government through AID has laid the foundation for a sound, strong, credit system which has provided homes 60-929-66-pt. 64

for nearly 250,000 people, and will provide homes for millions of people in the future. 

3. The economic impact of a given amount of housing construction is not limited to the expenditure that directly results from this activity. The income that is received by individuals as a result of such expenditures, appears again and again in the economy in the form of income to others as each set of recipients pays it out in consumption expenditures or taxes. Income that is saved also reappears as capital investment expenditures. It is estimated that the building industry of the United States has an impact of approximately 12 percent on the gross national product and it is conceivable that a housing construction industry financed through savings and loan associations sponsored under the foreign assistance program could have a greater impact on the gross national product of underdeveloped countries than the construction industry has in this country. 

The proposed legislation provides for an extension of foreign aid authorization for a 5-year period. By providing a longer authorization period, the executive branch of the Government can more carefully and effectively program its resources while, in our view, the Congress retains its eminent right of review and authority through annual appropriations. We feel this will be helpful in planning the development of sound private home financing and housing programs in these areas. 

Last year, Mr. Chairman, Congress enacted the Smathers amendment to the Housing Act to permit insured savings associations to invest up to 1 percent of their assets in Latin American housing projects guaranteed by AID. We note that out of $400 million available for loan guarantees in Latin America, $100 million has been set aside for low-cost housing projects including the development of thrift institutions. Even so, this is a relatively small amount in relation to the estimated need for 16 million dwellings in Latin America alone. 

For a number of years, the national league has sponsored legislation to establish an international home loan bank which would channel funds up to $14 million from savings associations in the United States to new thrift institutions in underdeveloped areas of the world. 

It would take a number of years to reach this optimum, if ever, but this is the private enterprise potential which could replace Government financing.

This legislation has been pending for several years before the congressional banking committees. The most recent proposal introduced is H.R. 11764 by Chairman Patman of the House Banking Committee. 

Last month I received a letter from Assistant Secretary of State 

Lincoln Gordon who expressed the Department's continuing interest in the international bank because he said the development of thrift institutions "comprises one of the more important accomplishments of the Alliance for Progress."

It is our belief that the international bank holds the greatest promise of rapid development of housing and home ownership programs under the foreign aid program and we would hope that this committee would continue to emphasize ways in which private enterprise and investment can make more contributions to the success of our efforts overseas.

The development of a savings and loan system in any country means more than the financing of a large number of houses; it is the development of a financial system that will exist long after AID and other agencies cease to exist. It is this development of thrift institutions which will mobilize heretofore untapped capital resources that will someday allow these underdeveloped countries to become independent of the need for foreign assistance. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Baker.

Mr. Baker, since the incorporation of the Humphrey amendment in the Foreign Assistance Act in 1961 has the organization you represent here today, the National League of Insured Savings Associations, cooperated with the AID Agency in the operation of the Humphrey amendment? 

Mr. BAKER. Yes, Mr. Chairman, we have. 

Chairman MORGAN. Do most members of your organization endorse this approach to getting the underdeveloped countries off the back of the taxpayers?

Mr. BAKER. Yes, Mr. Chairman. Many of the national league executives contribute their time and effort by going to these foreign countries to advise government officials on the aspects of establishing savings and loan systems. They have actually established systems in a number of these countries which are operating today as we indicated. 

Chairman MORGAN. You say in your report that 94 savings and loan associations are operating in the nations of Latin America, Ethiopia, and eastern Nigeria. Are there any other countries other than Ethiopia and Nigeria in Africa where there is any hope that we can establish such institutions? 

Mr. BAKER. Yes; there are. We have experts from our industry who have gone over to Libya, Sierra Leone, Turkey, Iran, and Pakistan. We had a mission recently report back from Thailand. 

Mr. KERWIN. Mr. Chairman, I call your attention to this little pamphlet, which I believe you have a copy of, that describes some of the developments in this program over the last several years. We have been in Africa quite extensively I would say for the last 4 years. 

In fact, one individual, Mr. William K. Divers of the Savings and

Loan Foundation, made an extensive survey in 14 African countries several years ago in an effort to elicit support and interest in the development of private thrift institutions. 

The league is very much interested and aggressively attempting to promote this program overseas and we feel quite confident that we have the full support of the members of the league in this endeavor. 

Chairman MORGAN. I notice on the last page of this pamphlet in the list of industry executives who have contributed time and energy to this program that you are listed as president of the Southwestern Savings Association and have been in Nicaragua. What has been your success in that country?

Mr. BAKER. In that country they have two associations at this time. that have total resources in excess of $8 million. We are advising the country and prepared a model statute to the free savings system for them to adopt which I understand is before their congress right now,

Mr. Chairman, and which the savings and loan industry and the Minister of Finance has endorsed.

Chairman MORGAN. In some of the Latin American countries labor unions have established savings and loan institutions. Do you work closely with this type of organization?

Mr. BAKER. We have worked closely with the American labor union group which has been working in Latin America. There is one country, Peru, that I believe has this particular type of institution. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Baker. 

Mr. BAKER. Mr. Chairman, I would like to introduce the latest chart which shows by country the actual breakdown on investment as well as the number of associations which more or less categorize what I have mentioned in my statement. 

Chairman MORGAN. Do you want that in the record? 

Mr. BAKER. Yes. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection it is so ordered.
