Chairman MORGAN. Mrs. Bolton, do you have any questions? 

Mrs. BOLTON. Not at this time.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Farbstein. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Yes, thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Baker, I don't know if the thought has ever occurred to your organization. You know, we have counterpart funds, excess counterpart funds in a lot of these countries in which you are setting up these savings and loan institutions. You put in seed money; I presume it is American money. Has it ever occurred to you that you might take your American money and exchange it for counterpart funds and use that and we could use up some of that money we have got, and then also you could increase the capital in these various countries by using some of these counterpart funds? Has that ever occurred to your organization?

Mr. BAKER. We have thought of that. Some of our reports have requested that. Thus far most of the counterpart funds that we have requested were already designated for other uses. For instance, in 

Peru there was a request for counterpart funds existing there, but they had other plans for those funds at that particular time. To this date we have not used counterpart funds as a resource. We know they exist. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Have you ever gone to AID and asked them to use a little pressure on any of these countries where counterpart funds exist so that they can be utilized?

Mr. BAKER. We did in one case, but they were spoken for. I think this deserves some attention, Mr. Farbstein.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I know various of these countries do not want any of this counterpart fund used. They want it to remain there for time immemorial, so to speak. So whatever we sell to generate these funds will be considered as grants.

It seems to me that we should try to utilize that money, because it belongs to us. I think that you ought to pressure the Agency for International Development and raise a fuss about this so they in turn pressure these various countries and insist some of this money be used for purposes that will aid their own people.

Mr. BAKER. There is no question but if we can get the release of all or part of these funds it would be a tremendous boon to housing in these countries. We think it is a fine idea. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I would like to join with you in doing it. I would be glad to do whatever I can to exert any pressure I can on AID or the State Department in turn to put pressure on these various countries that have these counterpart funds. 

Mr. BAKER. We certainly appreciate that statement. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you. That is all.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Murphy. 

Mr. MURPHY. No questions. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Baker. 

Mr. BAKER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Our next witness is Mrs. Mortimer Jacobson, national president of Hadassah for the Hadassah Medical Relief Association, Inc. With Mrs. Jacobson is Miss Hannah Goldberg, director, Hadassah Medical Relief Association, Inc. Mrs. Jacobson is

accompanied by our good friend, I. L. Kenan, executive director,

American Israel Public Affairs Committee. 

You have a prepared statement and you may proceed, Mrs. Jacobson.

STATEMENT OF MRS. MORTIMER JACOBSON, NATIONAL PRESIDENT OF HADASSAH FOR THE HADASSAH MEDICAL RELIEF ASSOCIA- 

TION, INC. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman.

May I thank the members of the committee for the privilege of being here this morning. While I would like to feel every Member of the 

House and the Senate is familiar with the work of our organization, I know it is good from time to time to have the opportunity to review the record and the history of our organization. 

I am here specifically today to enlist the assistance, cooperation, support, and friendship of this committee to have Hadassah included in section 214 of the Foreign Assistance Act to enable Hadassah to receive a grant of $1 million in Israel currency, or I£3 million for the enhancement and the development of our medical program in Israel.

First, may I state for the record that we are an organization of over 300,000 women and that we have chapters in every one of the 50 States, and Puerto Rico. When I say the 50 States, I really mean we are organized in the large and the small cities. Our organization is in existence for some 54 years. We started as a medical organization, and we still are a medical organization. 

We were created in 1912 by an American woman, Henrietta Szold of Baltimore. She started our organization with 12 women and today we have grown to, I think, one of the largest voluntary women's organizations in the United States.

With our growth has come an additional acceptance of responsibility to the development of medical programs. Mr. Chairman, I have distributed to the committee additional documentation which gives a background of the program of our organization. I do want to briefly review for you that we have built and do maintain in Jerusalem, in Israel, a medical center comprising of a 600-bed hospital, a separate mother-child maternity building, a nursing school, dental school, medical school, postgraduate school, an Asian-African Institute for the training of doctors overseas from the Asian and African countries. 

We have an outpatient department to which 1,000 people come for treatment every single day. Arabs, as well as Jews, have a warm feeling toward our organization and its services, and medical work has by now become a part and parcel of the land. 

Why is it that we come to this committee for assistance? I think you will agree that we live today in a period where medicine is in one of its most dynamic stages of development. Every modern medical institution must keep up with the developments of today, with the new techniques for heart surgery, kidney transplant, child psychiatry, for the enhancement of nurses programs so that you can keep good people in the nursing profession. It is essential to give doctors today the opportunities to perform the kind of medical practice that their abilities enable them to produce.

Hadassah has brought to the United States during these past years some 200 of our doctors who have been trained in specialties in this country and who have gone back to be heads or key members of their department. Because of their special talents, we have been able to develop in Israel the kind of American institution which we think brings great credit to the American people as well as benefit to the people of Israel.

Therefore, when the American Government has a program for helping American institutions which are operating overseas, we would like to think of ourselves as one of the organizations worthy of being encouraged to continue the kind of work we do and which merits help in our development.

We face many special challenges in the future. We know that we have to enlarge the program in our nurses training school and also provide a residency for nurses. We regret that our magnificent facilities have not as yet been able to provide any residency for our nurses or for our doctors.

There is great pressure for facilities for child psychiatry in our institution; unfortunately, this is one of the great problems in Israel today. There is great need for research space. We have many, many urgent needs that we must fulfill if we are to keep pace with the high standards that our organization has achieved up to now. 

Mr. Chairman, we are a responsible organization. I think our record will show that whatever program we undertake we carry through, we maintain, we develop, and bring to it the very special quality and standards that are uniquely American. Last year we had made an application to receive a grant under section 214, but unfortunately we were too late in our application. 

So we come before you today to ask the cooperation and help of this committee to see to it that this year Hadassah is included for the 

3 million pounds that we are requesting. 

Thank you.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mrs. Jacobson. 

Mrs. Jacobson, last year you did not bring the Hadassah project to the attention of this committee. All the work was done as you know with the Senate. The first word that we had that your organization was interested in this matter was after we got to conference. Your amendment was put in on the floor in the Senate. The administration opposed it in conference and it fell by the wayside in the conference. 

I know several of us who served on the conference committee were very sympathetic to your position, but the House had taken no action. 

As you know, the administration bill does not include your request. 

The administration witnesses that have appeared so far in defense of this bill are trying to limit the program for American schools and hospitals abroad, to keep it from expanding. The administration is inclined to hold this program to the colleges and institutions that are already depending on this money for their support, such as Roberts College in Turkey and the American University in Beirut, and so forth. 

There is already in the record, not referring specifically to your request, testimony to the effect that the administration is opposed to any increase in this particular section of the bill. I, myself, know a good deal about the work you have done overseas. I know how active your organization is. I think you have a great organization.

In Could you give the committee any figures as to the amount of money your organization raises annually in this country?

Mrs. JACOBSON. We have filed all this information, and we will be glad to provide the documentation if you need it here. 

(The information follows:) 

For the fiscal year 1964/65 (ended June 30, 1965) Hadassah collected for its projects in Israel (which include Youth Rescue work, Land Reclamation Projects, Vocational Training Program, and Medical Program) $9,063,223-of which $6,625,067 was for its medical program. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. Mr. Chairman, may I point out that from last year to this year there has been a substantial increase in the amount requested to be appropriated for this type of work. It would hardly seem to me that it is consonant with the principles of American justice to leave out an organization that has not received any grant so far when the amount has been increased this year. 

On the contrary, it seems to me that perhaps there should be thought about spreading these grants to other worthy institutions, including our organization. 

Chairman MORGAN. My only point is that there are no additional hospitals or universities planned for 1967. There have been increases for those who took part in the program last year. They are reluctant to add any additional hospitals or schools to the program. 

I am sure your request will have a very sympathetic consideration from this committee. I personally, as I said, am acquainted with your organization. I am particularly pleased to hear your statement today that you are incorporating in the Hadassah Medical Center in Israel the same type of voluntary women's organizations that we have been working with in the American hospitals. Having been a member of a hospital staff over a good many years, I know how valuable these organizations can be to the efficient operation of any hospital.

The women in this country do a magnificent job. If this can be worked out I am sure the Hadassah Medical Center will benefit greatly by this type of voluntary work. I think your organization has done a good job.

I have talked with several members about an amendment along the lines you recommend. I can assure you that although as chairman of the committee, I am not going to sponsor the amendment, I know of one vote you have. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. Thank you. Welcome.

Mrs. BOLTON. The work of Hadassah is so well known. I have been over in Israel and seen some of the things there. Also, my son and I have sent over there a machine for the dental school-you probably know of it. The young woman who has been doing the work has done a splendid job. 

Could you tell me what other hospitals there are in Israel besides the Hadassah Hospital? 

Mrs. JACOBSON. There are many other hospitals in Israel but we are the only full-fledged teaching hospital in Israel. We are the only

university teaching hospital in Israel that has a dental and medical school associated with it which has the three-prong program of healing, teaching, and research. There are hospitals in the country maintained by the sick fund and also by the Government. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Your teaching is in the nursing school and the medical school? 

Mrs. JACOBSON. And in the dental school. We also have a postgraduate institute. In addition to that we have an occupational therapy school. 

Mrs. BOLTON. You are very thorough, as one would expect you to be. How many student nurses do you have? Where do they come from, Israel or elsewhere?

Mr. JACOBSON. We have at the present time approximately 160 students including a very wonderful group of girls that come from Malawi, who are taking the full 3-year course. We also have some nurses from Liberia. Our standards are extremely high and applicants have to take very special admission examinations. 

When these young girls graduate, they become first-rate nurses in the country. 

Mrs. BOLTON. That is good, because we certainly need them.

Mrs. JACOBSON. It is one of the greatest problems of all countries today. 

Mrs. BOLTON. What the situation is going to be here after July 1 when Medicare breaks is quite a story. I find the medical and nursing associations are a bit panicky about it. We are so short of bedside nurses, and we are not training them as we did. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. I think all countries will have to face the problem of making the nursing profession more attractive. We must not only find ways to attract girls to become nurses, but find the way to keep them in the profession.

One of the things Hadassah is considering is to make our program a 4-year program instead of only a 3-year program with the end result that the nurse will also have an academic degree as well as a nursing degree. We believe that this will help to bring a higher standard of student into the nursing profession and keep her there. 

Mrs. BOLTON. When we tried that in this country, it was not too successful although it is one of the ways to keep them and to give them the feeling that they are somebody. I think we haven't grown out of the old-fashioned training into an educational training, yet at the same time I think in some of the areas the nursing groups have gone quite beyond reason. We have forgotten that we have to take care of the sick, and we do too much to get degrees. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. That is true.

Mrs. BOLTON. A degree is a fine thing, but after all the sick people need care. Again, what you tell us is very interesting. We all have the greatest respect and admiration for your organization, as you well know. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. Thank you very much. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Farbstein. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mrs. Jacobson, I want to say at the outset that I shall consider it an honor to be permitted to offer the amendment to the foreign aid bill to

make this grant to Hadassah from Israeli counterpart funds, because I sincerely believe the organization is a very worthy one. As a matter of fact, it would appear to me that the work of your organization in effect is an extension of the foreign aid assistance program of the United States to underdeveloped countries. As I read your statement, it would appear that you bring in people from various of these other underdeveloped countries in order to teach them various skills so they can return to their own country, and in turn, teach their own people. 

I want to repeat that it seems to me that yours is a very worthy organization and certainly should be treated no worse than the other organizations in various parts of the country that are permitted to use counterpart funds. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. Thank you, Mr. Farbstein. We are most appreciative of your help and assistance. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. That is all, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Whalley. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mrs. Jacobson, has anything been said as to the amount of money that you are thinking about? 

Mrs. JACOBSON. Yes, sir. We have been asking for 3 million Israel pounds. 

Mrs. BOLTON. That is how many dollars?

Mr. WHALLEY. How many dollars? 

Mrs. JACOBSON. The equivalent of $1 million. But we are asking this in Israel currency, 3 million pounds. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Has this project ever been in the bill before?

Mrs. JACOBSON. Last year, sir, as Chairman Morgan was explaining, we were late in our application and it did not come before the House committee at all. We were told we were too late last year and this year to properly apply, and we are so doing. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Murphy. 

Mr. MURPHY. I want to commend your organization for the very fine work they are doing. May I ask, does the Israeli Government participate in your program? 

Mrs. JACOBSON. They certainly do. 

Mr. MURPHY. In what amount?

Mrs. JACOBSON. The Israel Government gives to every hospital bed in the country an annual grant of some 1,500 to 2,000 pounds. In addition to that we receive a special grant of some half million to 750,000 pounds for our hospital.

Mr. MURPHY. I understand your problem and the fine work you are doing. I am identified with the hospital in Chicago. Of course, we would like to receive a great deal of aid and improve our facilities. 

Like Mrs. Bolton, I am on the African Subcommittee and I am very interested in people from Africa participating in your program. I want to commend your organization. 

Mrs. JACOBSON. Thank you, Mr. Murphy.

You might be especially interested to know, last year at our Hadassah convention, the Government of Liberia gave Hadassah a special citation because our doctors went there to set up the first eye hospital in the country. Every year several of our doctors rotate service in the 

African countries, particularly in the field of eye diseases which are so prevalent there.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Will the gentleman yield? 

Mr. MURPHY. Yes.

(The material submitted by Mrs. Jacobson is as follows:) STATEMENT OF MRS. MORTIMER JACOBSON, NATIONAL PRESIDENT OF HADASSAH 

Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, it is indeed a privilege to be given the opportunity to testify on behalf of Hadassah, the organization of which I am privileged to be National President. I welcome this opportunity to set before this important Committee the record of development and achievements of Hadassah for truly believe the accomplishments of our organization reveal one of the greatest sagas of voluntary efforts on the part of women. 

First, let me state briefly that Hadassah is an organization of over 300,000 American women organized in all of the 50 States of the Union and in Puerto Rico. This means we have functioning chapters in the smallest, as well as the largest communities. Our organization was founded in 1912 by Henrietta Szold of Baltimore, a social worker, educator and humanitarian. She succeeded in convincing 12 Jewish women that it was urgent to send a medical mission to Palestine, then a land plagued by poverty and disease. 

These 12 women accepted the challenge and in 1913 sent two American trained nurses to do pioneering work. This, then, was the beginning of our organization. Since that time we have labored in war and in peace to develop modern 

American standards of medicine, and if Israel today is a country that is aware of the necessity to provide all its citizens with proper healing, teaching and research facilities, Hadassah can claim a large share of the credit. 

Indeed, if many developing nations of the world have become conscious of the primary need to provide good standards of health in order to develop responsible citizenship, it is because Israel has demonstrated that healthy bodies are essential to produce healthy minds and attitudes. 

I have distributed to the Committee data giving information on the program and services presently being performed by the Hadassah Medical Organization

in Israel. I think you will agree that it is a pretty impressive report. Our aims have remained the same throughout our long and distinguished history-to bring American standards and know-how to a nation struggling to absorb people who come from more than 60 lands, half of whom come from primitive backgrounds. 

Our goal is to provide the best possible care for the sick, both in-patient and outpatient; to sponsor undergraduate and post-graduate teaching and to encourage research in all fields. 

Because medicine knows no frontiers and every achievement benefits all mankind, Hadassah has played a noteworthy role in helping the medical programs of the developing countries in Asia and Africa. This is accomplished by Hadassah doctors being sent to these countries to help them start clinics for both treatment and preventive medicine. Last year, at our National Convention, Hadassah was privileged to receive a citation from the Government of Liberia for the brilliant work done by our doctors in that country. Two of our doctors established 

Liberia's first 22-bed Eye Hospital. Between 5 and 6 of the doctors from our 

Ophthalmology Department rotate service in African countries.

At our Nurses Training School, 13 girls from Malawi (Nyasaland) and 2 from 

Liberia are studying. Their studies are conducted in English and they learn sufficient Hebrew to communicate with the patients. 

MEDICAL SCHOOL 

Since its inception over 800 physicians have completed their training at the Medical School. 73 students from developing countries in Asia, Africa and South 

America are presently studying in the School. These students will eventually become leaders in the field of health in their respective countries. 

Last year, 105 medical students came from their respective medical schools to take summer courses with us.

Our out-patient departments are located on 4 floors serving 1,000 patients a day. A visit to these clinics will show a tremendous variety of ethnic groups waiting for attention. Services are given to all who apply regardless of race, color or creed. One sees Arabs, Christian and Moslem, people from countless lands-all feel at home at Hadassah. Here one can really marvel at the role medicine plays in helping to mold a nation.

I would also like to stress the unique volunteer aspects of Hadassah. The National Board controls the budget and the policy of our organization. Department by department; item by item, we analyze, approve, or reject the program that is submitted for consideration. Our medical program is an American program. It is based on U.S. methods. We have an Advisory Board of distinguished American doctors who assist us in making decisions. Our Israel doctors come to the United 

States on fellowship programs and there is a continuous flow of people and knowledge between Israel and the United States. 

To give a specific example of how Hadassah brought a uniquely American technique to Israel, let me briefly tell you about the voluntary service program we pioneered in that country. Over 500 Israel women have been taught how to make bandages, feed patients, bind books, read to children and assist the nurses and doctors in the countless services that can be done by such a group. This type of voluntary work was completely unknown in Israel and we are now teaching other hospitals to develop a similar program. Thus, we were able to transfer to 

Israel the American concept of women doing volunteer work in hospitals. 

Our great challenge today comes from the magnificent developments that are taking place in modern medicine. We live today in an era of great change in treatment; we live today in a period where science and research are providing new techniques almost daily. No medical institution can stand still. It must keep pace with modern developments or become second rate. This is especially true of a teaching hospital which has the responsibility of providing students with the opportunity to study new methods of treatment and diagnosis. Medical equipment is expensive and complex and becomes obsolete all too quickly. Healing is certainly a rewarding art, but it is also an expensive one. 

With all this, the medical and nursing professions all over the world are having difficulty in attracting the best students who today are seemingly more attracted to other sciences. In order to bring the best minds to medicine, it is essential to have facilities to enable gifted men to contribute the best they are capable of producing.

The special dimensions of Hadassah's work have established our Medical Center as a place of international renown which attracts a continuous stream of

professional and lay visitors. Many Members of Congress, and other representatives of the United States Government have visited our institutions and all have been enthusiastic about our program of work and generous in their praise of our accomplishments.

There is no doubt that. Hadassah's pioneering medical work in Israel has been decisive for the health of the country and its citizens. It established patterns of health care, preventive and curative, and its staff combines professional excellence with dedication. It is these contributions and these qualities which assure Hadassah its unique place in the history of medicine. 

While we have made an enormous expenditure in money and effort, our Medical Center has not reached its final stage. The great progress in medical science demands increases in expenditure, changes in equipment and new methods of treatment, and if we wish to survive as a great Medical Center we shall have to expand our physical facilities to meet the changing needs. 

It is to help reach these new horizons that Hadassah asks for financial assistance and we are confident that this Committee will recognize Hadassah's contribution in the field of health to the individual, to Israel, to newly emerging nations, and, indeed, to mankind. We therefore urge that we be included in 

Section 214 of the Foreign Assistance Act for one million dollars in Israel currency or (3 million Israel pounds).

DESCRIPTION OF THE MEDICAL PROGRAM OF HADASSAH MEDICAL RELIEF ASSOCIA- TION, INC., 65 EAST 52D STREET, NEW YORK, N.Y. 

INTRODUCTION 

Hadassah Medical Relief Association, Inc. is a New York membership corporation carrying on a charitable program as a voluntary, nonprofit organization. 

Hadassah is registered with the President's Advisory Committee on Voluntary Foreign Aid and enjoys tax exempt status under the United States Internal Revenue Code. Attached is a copy of the United States Treasury Department letter granting Hadassah tax exempt status. (See Exhibit “A”)

MEMBERSHIP AND PROGRAM 

Hadassah was organized in 1912 by Miss Henrietta Szold, who was born in Baltimore, Maryland, and was a social worker and educator. Hadassah has grown from its original 12 members in the United States to over 300,000 women members, organized in chapters in 50 states of the Union and in Puerto Rico. 

The membership of Hadassah participates in its program as volunteers. These programs are supported primarily by voluntary contributions from members and from the general American public.

Hadassah's medical and educational facilities in Israel and in America are nongovernmental, non-profit and are not church connected. 

Hadassah now has an internationally recognized program of healing, teaching and research in Israel, and also vocational education, child rehabilitation and land reclamation. In the United States it has a program of education, youth activities and American civic affairs.

HADASSAH MEDICAL ORGANIZATION Through HADASSAH MEDICAL ORGANIZATION, Hadassah carries on in 

Israel a comprehensive program of medical education; preventive, diagnostic and curative medicine; and an extensive program of medical and scientific research.

The major portion of Hadassah's medical work is centered in and fans out of the Hadassah-Hebrew University Medical Center, Jerusalem, Israel. The 

Medical Center complex includes the following:

Henrietta Szold School of Nursing. 

Hebrew University-Hadassah Medical School. 

Afro-Asian Institute. 

Hebrew University-Hadassah School of Dentistry (founded of Alpha Omega). 

School of Occupational Therapy. 

Department of Social Medicine and Public Health. 

Institute of Post-Graduate Medical Training.

Medical and scientific research laboratories. 

Exchange fellowship program.

Teaching hospital (containing about 600 beds). 

Mother and child pavilion.

Outpatient clinics. 

Hospital pharmacy (used by the students of the above-mentioned Medical School).

John F. Kennedy Memorial Building. 

Community health services for preventive medicine (at Kiryat Yovel, a village adjacent to the Medical Center).

HADASSAH POLICIES MADE BY UNITED STATES CITIZENS 

All of Hadassah's medical and teaching institutions in Israel were established by United States citizens. The National Board of Hadassah, consisting of representatives from all parts of the United States, is the sole authority making all policy decisions. Policy decisions relating to its Israel program are implemented by counterpart bodies in Israel. These counterpart bodies under the direction and control of the National Board of Hadassah are set up for the sole purpose of carrying out policies. American citizens as well as Israel nationals serve on these counterpart bodies.

STAFF INCLUDES UNITED STATES CITIZENS 

The Director-General of Hadassah Medical Organization and the principals of the various schools operated by Hadassah are Israelis. However, the teaching staffs and medical personnel include many United States citizens. Many of the Israelis working for Hadassah are American trained, and bring to their positions American standards and expert knowledge.

ADVISORY COMMITTEES COMPOSED OF UNITED STATES CITIZENS 

While Hadassah alone operates, manages, and controls its work in Israel, it has the advantage of volunteer technical advisory committees composed of well known, respected and highly qualified United States citizens serving as counsellors. The advisory committees help develop the standards used for Hadassah's program in Israel, which are based completely on practices and procedures used by similar facilities in the United States. Attached are listings of the members of the Medical Advisory Board and the Youth Reference Board. (See Exhibits 

"B" and "C.")

STUDENTS PRIMARILY ISRAEL NATIONALS 

The majority of students at Hadassah's schools are Israel nationals. In consonance with the United States program of international cooperation, a number of students are from developing nations in Africa and Asia. 

COURSES OFFERED 

The Hadassah schools offer technical training in the various disciplines of medicine. Courses in language, literature, geography and history of Israel are requirements for matriculation. A knowledge of the English language is necessary since some of the courses are given in English and many of the textbooks are in this language.

Since the 13 years of the Medical Residency Training Program 37 doctors formerly with Hadassah have become heads of Departments in other institutions and key hospitals in Israel.

HENRIETTA SZOLD-HADASSAH SCHOOL OF NURSING 

The Henrietta Szold-Hadassah School of Nursing, established in 1918, was the first nurses training school in what was then Palestine. The school has housing and teaching facilities for 150 student nurses. The standards set by this school have been followed and adopted by the Government of Israel as the minimum requirements for other nurses training schools in that country. Hadassah's school still ranks as the leader in the field of nursing and sets the standards for other similar institutions in Israel.

Under consideration for implementation is Hadassah's plan to add to the nursing curriculum which may entail additional plant facilities.

The additional period of training is in response to a pressing need in Israel for teachers, supervisors, and researchers in the nursing field. The expanded curriculum, producing more highly trained nurses, will add greatly to the economic development of Israel. The additional curriculum of training will enable the graduates of the school to receive a University Bachelor's Degree in nursing in addition to the RN.

Hadassah has the exclusive jurisdiction, control and responsibility for curriculum, teaching, administration and financing of the Henrietta Szold-Hadassah 

School of Nursing. 

HEBREW UNIVERSITY-HADASSAH MEDICAL SCHOOL 

The Hebrew University-Hadassah Medical School was the first medical school established in Israel. It was opened in 1949 with a student body of 50. Its outstanding faculty has helped produce a corps of expertly trained physicians in Israel. Of the 747 student body in its present enrollment, 520 are in the medical department, 72 in the pharmaceutical department and 128 in the micro-biology department. There are also 15 students enrolled for graduate courses in medical science and 12 in post-graduate work in public health. 

Hadassah has full and exclusive jurisdiction, control and responsibility for maintenance and administering of the clinical (bedside teaching) sections of the Medical School. The pre-clinical sections of the Medical School are under the joint control of Hadassah and the Hebrew University. 

AFRO-ASIAN INSTITUTE 

In 1961 the Afro-Asian Institute was established to train medical students from countries in Africa and Asia who later return to their native lands to practice and to teach medicine. 

Students came from Liberia, Ethiopia, Nigeria, Ghana, British Guinea, Cyprus, Kenya, Mauritius, Nepal, Tanzania, Uganda, and so forth. The Afro-Asian

Institute was established to assist the developing countries of Africa and Asia whose own educational facilities are very limited or non-existent. These students, on graduation, have the requisite training to educate a generation of young doctors in their own countries.

HEBREW UNIVERSITY-HADASSAH SCHOOL OF DENTISTRY (FOUNDED BY ALPHA OMEGA) 

The Dental School grew out of the small number of American dentists sent by Hadassah to Palestine in 1918. Since that time, the school has developed into a modern School of Dentistry with a network of dental services. It is the only 

Dental School in Israel. 

The present student body of the Dental School is made up of 120 students. 

Hadassah has full and exclusive jurisdiction, control and responsibility for the maintenance and administration of the clinical (chair teaching) section of this school.

INSTITUTE OF POSTGRADUATE MEDICAL TRAINING 

The Institute for Postgraduate Studies was founded in 1962. It provided for refresher courses for Israel physicians, courses for newly arrived general practitioners, and courses in specialized branches of surgery and medicine. These courses are given in Jerusalem, Tel Aviv, Beersheva and Afula so that local forces for teaching purposes can be utilized.

Postgraduate training is given to foreign physicians, some of whom have come from widely divergent areas such as Burma, Thailand, the African continent, England and Australia. Training is given in ophthalmology, dermatology, haematology, etc.

MEDICAL AND SCIENTIFIC RESEARCH 

Research work done by Hadassah doctors and scientists has attracted international attention; papers are published in outstanding scientific and medical journals; grants have been received from world famous institutes, funds and foundations, including the U.S. Government's National Institutes of Health, Ford Foundation, Rockefeller Foundation, Royal Society of London, and others.

Over 54% of the bio-medical research papers and 90% of the medical research papers published in Israel are by Hadassah doctors and scientists. The work

of these researchers is making a significant contribution to the world's store of medical knowledge. The attached list includes some significant published papers of Hadassah doctors and scientists (See Exhibit “D”). In order to meet Israel's requirements for more medical and scientific research, Hadassah has increased its laboratory facilities and has provided the necessary facilities for kidney transplant and open heart surgery.

EXCHANGE FELLOWSHIP PROGRAM Hadassah participates in the United States Exchange Fellowship Program under DSP-66 P 111-1411. Since 1946, through this comprehensive fellowship program, Hadassah has sent over 200 physicians, nurses and hospital administrators to receive additional specialized training in universities and hospitals in the United States and Europe. This specialized training has helped these fellows to set American standards in Israel.

FACILITIES AT THE HADASSAH-HEBREW UNIVERSITY MEDICAL CENTER 

Hospital: This 600 bed patient hospital has 28 Departments and 31 Institutes and Laboratories. Approximately 17,000 in-patients are treated annually. 

Mother and Child Pavilion: This facility has 50 obstetrical beds and 64 bassinets providing facilities for care of mother and new born infant. (These figures are included in the 600 mentioned above).

Out-patient Clinics: There are 32 out-patient clinics treating well over 200,000 patients annually, thus providing a direct link between the community and the hospital. Within the out-patient clinics there are sub-clinics manned by highly specialized personnel. The names of some of the clinics are: Ophthalmology, Internal Medicine, Pediatrics, Ear, Nose and Throat, Obstetrics, etc. 

Pharmacy Department: This department, serving the in-patient and out-patient clinics, fills approximately 500,000 prescriptions a year. This pharmacy is used as a teaching facility for the Department of Pharmacy which is part of the 

Medical School. 

New Nurses' Residence: A new residence for graduate, unmarried nurses is being constructed by Hadassah in close proximity to the hospital at the Medical Center. This facility is necessary to give nurses living quarters near the hospital as an inducement to their continuing in hospital service and to add to the efficiency of their work.

New Medical Residence: Hadassah is now in the process of planning a residence for its interns, residents and other medical personnel, such as visiting doctors from abroad who serve the hospital for a stated length of time. This residence is sorely needed, since the hospital has only minimum living accommodations. This new residence will increase the effectiveness of interns and residents who are always on call.

COMMUNITY HEALTH SERVICES-PREVENTIVE MEDICINE 

The Community Health Center at Kiryat Yovel, a village adjacent to the Medical Center, is a pilot plant which has attracted considerable attention from the international community. The Health Center serves a population of 20,000 families, mostly new residents in Israel, who originate from over 50 different countries.

The fundamental principle of operation of the Health Center is that the health of the individual is involved with that of his family and is influenced by emotional, social and economic conditions. Thus the whole family is treated by a team of physicians, nurses, psychologists and social workers. An average of 15,000 patients are seen annually by physicians and nurses. In addition, 45,000 prescriptions are filled and 12,000 laboratory examinations are made. 

The Health Center also serves as a teaching and research facility for students at the Hebrew University-Hadassah Medical School. As a pilot project it has been emulated by other agencies in Israel. Other nations, as well as the World 

Health Organizations of the United Nations, have sponsored the initiation of similar projects.

INTERNATIONAL COOPERATION 

In consonance with the United States program of international cooperation. 

Hadassah has made available some of its doctors and nurses, part of a corps sent to several Asian and African countries to set up health facilities and insti- 60-929-66-pt. 65

tutes for medical teaching in those countries where such facilities are sorely needed. Hadassah doctors and nurses were involved in the eye clinics established in Ethiopia and Liberia and other African countries. 

IMPROVEMENT OF MEDICAL TECHNIQUES AND RAISING OF STANDARDS 

Many United States medical authorities are invited to present to Hadassah's medical personnel papers on new developments in medicine and new surgical techniques. This exchange, in addition to the Exchange Fellowship Program, makes it possible to enhance the skills of many more of the medical personnel than are able to leave Israel under the Exchange Fellowship Program. In this way United States standards and techniques are available for inclusion into Hadassah's program. 

SUMMARY 

Hadassah's overall training program for all levels of medical education has influenced 1,357 graduates. In the 46 years of the existence of the Henrietta 

Szold-Hadassah School of Nursing well over 1,000 nurses have been graduated to serve the Israel community in hospitals, out-patient departments and in-patient medical service.

In the 18 years existence of the Hebrew University-Hadassah Medical School, over 800 physicians have been graduated to serve in all parts of Israel, in border settlements, in public health stations, in hospitals and in private medical service. 

In the 12 years since the establishment of the Department of Pharmacy, over 60 students have completed their courses of training and are serving in hospitals and out-patient and private work. 

Over the past 19 years since the Fellowship Program was initiated, more than 200 physicians have been outside of Israel for refresher courses (mostly in the United States) and observation to take back to Israel the latest scientific developments in their respective fields. 

In total, Hadassah has given to the State of Israel 2,231 highly trained personnel without which no modern state can carry on its vital functions. Medicine has no boundaries; Health has no frontiers. 

Respectfully submitted.

EXHIBIT A Mrs. MORTIMER JACOBSON, National President. 

TREASURY DEPARTMENT, OFFICE OF COMMISSIONER OF INTERNAL REVENUE, Washington, December 2, 1941. 

HADASSAH MEDICAL RELIEF ASSOCIATION, INC., 

New York, N.Y.

SIRS It is the opinion of this office, based upon the evidence presented, that you are exempt from Federal income tax under the provisions of section 101 (6) of the Internal Revenue Code and corresponding provisions of prior revenue acts. 

Accordingly, you will not be required to file returns of income unless you change the character of your organization, the purposes for which you were organized or your method of operation. Any such changes should be reported immediately to the collector of internal revenue for your district in order that their effect upon your exempt status may be determined. 

Since any organization which is exempt from Federal income tax under the provisions of section 101 of the Internal Revenue Code also is exempt from the capital stock tax pursuant to the express provisions of section 1201 (a)(1) of the Internal Revenue Code, you will not be required to file capital stock tax returns for future years so long as the exemption from income tax is effective. 

Furthermore, under substantially identical authority contained in sections 1426 and 1607 of the Code and/or corresponding provisions of the Social Security Act, the employment taxes imposed by such statutes are not applicable to remuneration for services performed in your employ so long as you meet the conditions prescribed above for retention of an exempt status for income tax purposes.

Contributions made to you are deductible by the donors in arriving at their taxable net income in the manner and to the extent provided by section 23(o)

of the Internal Revenue Code and corresponding provisions of prior revenue acts. 

Requests, legacies, devises or transfers, to or for your use are deductible in arriving at the value of the net estate of a decedent for estate tax purposes in the manner and to the extent provided by sections 812 (d) and 861 (a) (3) of the Code and/or corresponding provisions of prior revenue acts. Gifts of property to you are deductible in computing net gifts for gift tax purposes in the manner and to the extent provided in sections 1004 (a) (2) (B) and 1004 (b) (2) and (3) of the Code and/or corresponding provisions of prior revenue acts. 

The collector of internal revenue for your district is being advised of this action. 

By direction of the Commissioner: 

Respectfully,

T. MOONEY, Deputy Commissioner.

Chairman MORGAN. Our next witness is the Honorable Lester Wolff, 

Member of Congress from the State of New York. Congressman, it is a real honor and privilege to welcome you before this committee today. 

You have a prepared statement and you may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF HON. LESTER L. WOLFF, A REPRESENTATIVE IN CONGRESS FROM THE STATE OF NEW YORK 

Mr. WOLFF. I appreciate the opportunity to appear before you here. 

I cannot let the opportunity pass, however, before reading my prepared statement to comment on the previous witness. I should like to lend my support to the request that is being made by the Hadassah organization for their hospital in Israel. 

Mr. Chairman, once again this distinguished committee is being asked to authorize moneys for foreign assistance. Such aid has been a major feature of American foreign policy since, under the brilliant leadership of President Truman and Secretary of State Marshall, we helped raise the war-ravaged nations of Western Europe back to the affluence and prosperity they enjoy today.

Foreign aid has generally been a successful instrument of American foreign policy in building the economies of devastated nations and preserving them from the pressures of communism that feeds on such devastation.

I am in favor of judiciously administered foreign aid to those nations who appreciate it and who evidence that appreciation by not following courses opposed to our national interest. I have spoken out more than once on this subject in opposition to further aid to those nations who seek our help on the one hand, but vilify us and burn our libraries on the other. But today I would like to speak on another aspect of our foreign aid policies.

I believe that in many cases the effectiveness of the assistance we give other nations has been severely limited because of poorly conceived, poorly administered, even capricious projects that have helped no one. I am speaking about those programs we have all heard about which have turned out to be fiascoes and have even made the great United States a laughing stock in certain foreign capitals. 

I hesitate to consider an authorization for foreign aid or for any other expenditure of public funds unless I am satisfied that such assistance achieves the maximum return for the nation involved and for the vital interest of the United States.

I have grave doubts that many of our commercial assistance or investments abroad have always been based on commonsense business principles upon which this Nation has achieved its great preeminence. suspect that many instances where projects have failed have come about because the expertise of our private sector has not even been consulted. 

For this reason, Mr. Chairman, I should like to offer an amendment today to this foreign aid authorization bill to establish a business. advisory council, composed of American business specialists, to pass judgment, in the field, on each and every commercial loan or grant contemplated for a foreign nation under the AID program. 

I am not without experience in this field. I have seen AID missions in operation in dozens of countries especially in critical areas. 

I observed our programs in Vietnam last November, along with my colleague from New York State, Mr. Halpern.

It is my observation that the AID program has not attracted personnel from the private sector who have achievement in the practical survival school of experience that every successful businessman must graduate from if he is to survive. It is a school I attended for 25 years before coming to Congress.

Mr. Chairman, I believe our foreign aid program has been characterized by too many bureaucratic generalists. What the program needs is a few hard-working sergeants who have come up through the ranks of American business and whose experience is invaluable, even indispensable to our Nation's interest.

I am asking this distinguished committee to amend the legislation now before it to include the setting up within the AID program of a review system whereby a loan, say to establish a fertilizer plant in Vietnam, should be studied and reviewed by a panel of American businessmen with long experience in the fertilizer field, before such a loan is made. And such review should be conducted in the field to gain a maximum appreciation of all factors involved. 

I believe American industry and business, which has brought this Nation to the position where it is rich enough to consider such vast expenditures of our national wealth, will rise to this challenge and

provide the cadre of business leaders necessary to provide the judgment and experience required to make good, sound loans and grants when it is in our national interest. 

Mr. Chairman, my amendment would substitute expertise for innocence. It's a tough world, and we are involved in a tough scrap in 

Vietnam. And there will probably be others.

Let's apply the tough judgment of proven businessmen to our programs of assistance to friendly nations.

Should a nation grown strong because of the hard work of businessmen do less?

Foreign assistance should be a temporary program to assist nations establish a firm independent economic foundation so that a strong democratic nation can flourish and that the ultimate goal of establishing trading partners in the world community can become a reality. 

Trade is a two-way street. Our balance-of-payments problem and many political problems can easily be solved if these nations to which we render aid can ultimately stand on their own feet and buy and sell to us with their own money. I have never known anyone to kick a good customer in the teeth.

Our handling of AID financing under present circumstances and procedures, I am sorry to say, is so full of dirt it must constantly be swept under the rug.

Inept bureaucrats seeking to perpetrate their jobs act as judge and jury of the programs placed before them for implementation. Waste, gross waste, undermines what could become the salvation of the free world as we know it today.

The free enterprise private sector which has been generally critical of AID, I am certain, would step forward to assist the implementation of an effectual program if the "Ivory Tower" boys would for a moment accept ideas that were generated outside of AID or the State Department. In an attempt to protect this private domain, total disregard to experience prevails and this permeates loan and grant procedures in 

AID. 

I would substitute a reserve of business leaders expert in their fields who would serve 1 month a year to act as panels in all nations where aid is distributed as a review board to act upon the merits of projects up for consideration.

The Department of Commerce has a roster of hundreds of successful men who have agreed to serve without compensation on trade missions. Thousands of "business expert" not pseudo experts now employed as overall generalists, could be found who would serve our nation for a limited time without compensation and render to the overall problems the greatest assist we have ever achieved in making AID commercial loans and grants a working reality instead of a frightful fiasco.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Congressman Wolff. 

Congressman, will you expand a little bit more on your suggested amendment? Would you establish a different business council for each project or each country, or how would this amendment work? 

Mr. WOLFF. A council would be set up that would draw upon all varieties of industry within the United States. Then when specific loans were requested, people from this particular industry would be requested to serve on a review board in a particular area.

It would be a reserve force, a pool of qualified personnel that could be drawn upon as needed. For example, as I quoted in my statement, if a request came in for a particular loan in a particular country, you would draw upon this reserve for people in that industry or someone who had some connection with the industry involved. 

Chairman MORGAN. Congressman, you know we have one organization now functioning in the foreign aid field headed by Frank Pace. 

It is headed as a committee of business people, and AID is cooperating. 

They have been in operation about a year, I believe. Part of their funds are from business groups in this country and AID finances part of the operation. The volunteers are mostly retired business people or people who take leave from their business. They get per diem while overseas but no salaries.

AID testified here during these hearings that the per diem for sending these businessmen overseas, ranges anywhere from $800 to $1,000 a month. AID has been picking up part of this. Does this work in with the suggestion that you have?

Mr. WOLFF. It could with the exception, Mr. Chairman, that this would be more than having an advisory group in limbo. They would utilize the services of a group such as this to provide reserve cadre to pass upon the loans, similar to the way some banks operate. Banks use advisory groups to sit in judgment upon loans, giving them outside assistance in coming to a decision.

I have known a number of loans that have been made or not made by AID, based upon the judgment of an individual who sits either as a desk officer or as a project officer in a foreign land who has had no prior experience whatsoever with the type of industry or the field that he is supposed to pass judgment upon.

I think this group under Mr. Pace-and perhaps it can be added tocould provide a group of people who could sit with the project officer and advise and recommend on these particular loans. 

Chairman MORGAN. Are you familiar with the so-called Javits amendment that has been in the foreign aid bill? 

Mr. WOLFF. No, I am not, sir.

Chairman MORGAN. He has had ideas along this line for a good many years for increasing the participation of the business community foreign aid operations. This committee has been very sympathetic to views of this kind. We have over a period of years included language in the act to bring private enterprise and the business community into the foreign assistance program. 

As you know, there have been several committees. Are you familiar with the Watson committee? 

Mr. WOLFF. Yes. 

Chairman MORGAN. Is that committee's work along the line of your suggestions?

Mr. WOLFF. It is. The only point I am trying to make is the fact that I think we should utilize these people in addition to acting in the broad advisory sense, as well in the operational area, actually within the framework of the AID program, in the field and to assist with reconsiderations on the passing of individual loans and in the implementation of the programs after they have been passed. 

I am fairly familiar with the AID programs in southeast Asia, having served on two trade missions to southeast Asia. I was requested

to go into an area while on a trade mission to look over the idea of setting up a department store in Bangkok. After looking over the area, I decided this was an impossible task to set up a department store on the basis outlined.

Yet our local people in Bangkok, having no knowledge of retailing whatsoever, were promoting this project and were very disturbed over the fact that I felt there was no real raison d'etre for a department store in that area.

What I am getting at is the fact that I did come out of the private sector at that time and I was in the field on the site and as a result of which I think I was instrumental in stopping an investment on AID in something that could not possibly be successful. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Would the gentleman yield?

Chairman MORGAN. I yield. 

Mrs. BOLTON. What kind of an organization? What could have been done there?

Mr. WOLFF. I think there could have been some small stores set up. Actually the problem, Mrs. Bolton, was the fact that we were attempting to set up a showcase project, and the project itself really did not have merit because of the additional ramifications. They wanted people to invest in the physical building of a department store and then only wanted to give them a 10-year lease. Our economic officers at that time were strongly pursuing this idea and trying to get the private sector to come in on that basis, which was really a fallacious one.

Chairman MORGAN. Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON. I think you are perhaps unconsciously working with us in the idea that we are quite unable to get facts such as you bring us now as we are set up. If we had some way of sending out, say, two of our number and a staff member to really see these plans, and see them before they take place, and also afterward, we would have a more fitting and more accurate determination from it. 

We could really know what we are doing. It is very difficult to sit in Washington and know what is going on over in Mali or somewhere else. I think your opinion here is going to be very valuable to us. 

Thank you very much.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Farbstein.

We have in the AID program what is known as an Executive Service Corps. These people are called upon to give advice in particular instances, but we do not have a permanent business advisory council attached to either the AID program or any other organization that has to do with foreign aid. 

The Watson committee is occasionally called in-it is now the Watson committee, tomorrow it is another committee-but we do not have I repeat, any permanent organization of business people that are connected with the AID organization.

Mr. WOLFF. I would welcome it, sir.

Mr. CULVER. I say I also would like to join my colleagues, Congressman Wolff, in commending you on what I consider to be an extremely valuable suggestion. I would be interested in a possible elaboration by you as to how you feel this might be accomplished, or whether in fact you feel this would be adequate time to have a historical perspective and a general understanding of the unique environments that he would be called upon to lend his recommendations and suggestions to. 

Mr. WOLFF. Certainly I thank you for your comments, Mr. Culver.

I certainly don't believe that we can get all the answers within 30 days. However, I think with adequate briefing of the individuals-I want to comment at this point on the very excellent program that the Department of Commerce has in trade mission groups, whereby they take a 5-day or 3-day briefing, and with adequate source material they are able to provide the average businessman with a thorough

grounding in the local circumstances. By assimilating this material, and actually getting out into the field, I do believe that within a month's time, and this can be elastic, I do believe within a month's time a very serious and important contribution can be made by an individual or group to the judgment of a particular project. 

I think that in addition, these business people would be able to give some part-time assistance to this program, in addition to a month of full-time activity, and this would serve to fully answer the time limitations you so rightfully question.

Mr. WOLFF. I think that would be an excellent contribution.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Roybal. 

Mr. ROYBAL. May I also thank you for bringing this proposal before this committee.

Along the line of questioning that you just had, I would like to ask this question: Do you believe that the men passing judgment on a particular loan request should also have knowledge of the economics and of the people of the recipient country? 

Mr. WOLFF. Very definitely. We, through the Department of Commerce, today already conduct market studies in the particular area where the individual plant is to be located or the individual enterprise is to be set up. I do believe that the overall economic aspects of the picture are extremely important in assessing a particular project and I do believe the State Department can adequately provide the basic general economic data required.

I am sure too that a prudent businessman would very definitely require economic and market data in depth to fully assess any proposition.

Mr. ROYBAL. I ask that question because I have had the opportunity of visiting the Chile-California program. This is a situation where men from Chile and California get together to study the economics of Chile to make recommendations for joint ventures.

If a similar situation were to develop in other countries, I think it would be probably one of the best things that could happen. Your proposal, however, calls for a permanent committee composed of x number of people who would be experts, some in Latin America, some in Asia, some in Europe, who would then pass on the loans going to those particular areas of the world, is that correct? 

Mr. WOLFF. That is correct, except the fact that the committee itself would be fairly extensive and would actually be a reserve pool of people to be called upon. It would be a permanent body. I think you, Mr. Roybal, mentioned what I consider a magic word, and that is "joint venture." I have been exposed to a great number of joint ventures in foreign lands.

I think this is one of the most effective means that we have of establishing friendly relations with countries and also establishing them in a manner that gives the local national an opportunity of exercising his own judgment, his own abilities. We are pooling resources. 

I think this is the basis of foreign assistance, the idea of pooling re-

sources with other nations. I don't think that anyone likes to go around with a cup in his hand, especially the private sector. I think we cement our relations through this vehicle. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Thank you. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Wolff, I think your suggestion has a great deal of merit and I assure you that the committee will take it into consideration when we start the markup. Section 601 of the bill, as the act is now written, provides for encouragement of free enterprise and private business.

It includes provision for an advisory committee that is supposed to meet once a month, composed of nine people, and had an authorization of $50,000. It was to report by June 30, 1965, and was then given an extension to 1966. Is it your suggestion to go deeper in the study specific projects, or do you suggest an overall group of advisers primarily business people? This advisory committee is now made up of business, labor, and professional people. They have made a report just recently.

This advisory committee was the result of action by the other body, which after some modifications we accepted in conference. I am not sure personally that this advisory committee has been very valuable. 

They issue an elaborate report, Members of Congress read it, but I am not sure how much impact it has.

The work of this overall advisory committee for private enterprise has never convinced me that I should vote for continuing its existence. 

You have come today in front of the committee with an interesting proposal, and as Mr. Farbstein suggested, if you could expand it a little further in the form of a specific amendment and furnish it to the staff of the committee before the markup, together with a little explanation of your amendment, I am sure the committee would give serious consideration to your suggestion.

We all feel there is, as you pointed out, a great deal of delay on the part of the Government on these projects. I suppose we will never avoid criticisms of foreign aid operations. 

During my long tenure on this committee I have heard over the years people talk about foreign aid supplying striped trousers for Grecian undertakers, iceboxes for Eskimos, collapsible toothpaste tubes. 

Critics of foreign aid have been willing to go a long way to come up with minute details about the program. 

The extreme stories get the most attention from the press. Foreign aid over a period of years has been discussed in the book "The Ugly American," and other type publications. If there is any way that by following your suggestion we could stop waste in the foreign aid program, I would be glad to have your help. 

Mr. WOLFF. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I appreciate your comments. I want you to know that I am here not as a destructive critic of foreign aid but one who really appreciates this as a great weapon in the arsenal of democracy, and I am here for constructive criticism.
