Washington, D.O. 

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:40 a.m., in room 2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

Chairman MORGAN. The committee will come to order. 

The committee meets this morning in open session for a continuation of the hearings on the foreign aid bills for fiscal year 1967, H.R. 

12449 and H.R. 12450. 

Our first witness this morning is Hon. Birch Bayh, Member of the

U.S. Senate from Indiana. 

Senator, earlier this year during its consideration of the supplemental authorization, your amendment was adopted in the other body. 

It was eliminated in conference. I want to put in the record at this time the statement made by the House conferees. 

(The statement follows:) 

The managers on the part of the House pointed out that the amendment before the committee of conference dealt with matters which had not received consideration by the Committee on Foreign Affairs during its consideration of the bill nor had there been any discussion during debate in the House, but they recognized that this question deserved careful consideration. Because there has not been adequate opportunity to review in detail the full impact of the Senate provision, the managers on the part of the House were unwilling to accept the Senate language, but they agreed that the Committee on Foreign Affairs should look into the whole question during the consideration of the pending foreign aid bill. 

We will be glad to hear your views today. You have a prepared statement, and you may proceed.

STATEMENT OF HON. BIRCH BAYH, U.S. SENATOR FROM THE STATE OF INDIANA 

Senator BAYH. I do have a prepared statement. I am grateful to you as chairman, and members of the committee for granting me this opportunity. I know well the close study that you and your committee are making of this important legislation and thus am able to understand why you might be reluctant to consider at this late hour the amendment which was adopted by the Senate. 

With your permission I would like to read my prepared statement and then if you have any questions. I will be glad to answer them. 

Chairman MORGAN. You may proceed, sir. 969

Senator BAYH. Mr. Chairman, I am grateful for your letting me have a chance to appear here today to discuss the 1967 Foreign Eco nomic Assistance Authorization Act and to present an amendment for your consideration. To put my criticism of this problem in proper perspective, I would like to point out that since coming to the Senate, I have consistently voted for foreign assistance authorizations and appropriations. I have opposed certain individual programs such as assistance to Indonesia and certain military assistance programs. 

Generally, I have supported our AID program, although our foreign assistance program is not the most popular Federal program in my home State.

I believe that our foreign assistance program meets our responsibility to assist in the development of emerging and less developed nations, and at the same time to make a positive contribution to the 

U.S. foreign policy objectives.

In past weeks, I have been critical of the procurement policy of the 

Agency for International Development in the purchase of galvanized iron and steel products for South Vietnam. I have done so although our AID program is critically important to our total effort in southeast Asia.

The amendment which I propose refers to the purchase of iron and steel products for Vietnam under AID financing and is similar to one which I introduced in the Senate during its consideration of the 1966 supplemental foreign aid authorization bill. The Senate adopted the amendment without dissent, but it was removed later in a House- 

Senate conference.

At that time, representatives of the appropriate House and Senate committees pledged to give the proposal full consideration at hearings of this year's general foreign aid authorization bill. 

Although I am certain that the members of this committee are familiar with the background of this amendment, having dealt with it earlier in conference with the Senate, I will, with your indulgence, Mr. Chairman, rehearse the development of this situation for the benefit of the committee's records. 

In June 1963, the Agency for International Development adopted new regulations affecting the procurement of products under supporting assistance programs, "to lessen the adverse effects of the foreign economic assistance program on the U.S. balance of payments." The policy adopted at that time not only required that the item procured with AID financing come from an eligible source, but limited to 30 percent of the f.o.b. sales price the components of the item which could originate in advanced foreign countries. Under the regulation, Korea and Taiwan continued to import and galvanize Japanese black plate for sale to Vietnamese importers under AID financing.

The Korean and Taiwanese suppliers contended that less than 30 percent of the components by value, as measured against their final sales price, came from an ineligible source-Japan-particularly since the Japanese black sheet was made in large part from U.S. scrap. 

In January 1964, AID tightened the 70/30 componentry ratio to 

90/10—that is, no more than 10 percent of the f.o.b. sales price on each item could result from components originating in advanced foreign countries. Stated another way, 90 percent of the components

in each item had to originate either from the United States or from eligible developing nations, such as Taiwan or Korea.

Korea continued to import and galvanize Japanese black plate for export to Vietnam, rationalizing its actions by importing U.S. scrap for delivery to bonded warehouses in Japan for the purpose of making black plate to be shipped to Korea for further processing. 

In its own words, Mr. Chairman, and I quote, "AID found it increasingly difficult to police and enforce the 90/10 componentry ratio in connection with these Korean exports of galvanized steel plate and sheets to Vietnam."

So AID suspended shipments of galvanized products under AID 

financing from Korea to Vietnam. 

On January 31, 1966, AID issued a source ruling removing its 90-10 componentry ratio requirement. This ruling had the following 

1. All black plate, which ultimately would be shipped as galvanized sheets to Vietnam and paid for with AID funds, was purchased by Korea, Taiwan, or other eligible source countries from Japan providing an indirect subsidy to the steel industry of a nation in vigorous competition with our own steel industry. 

I would like to introduce into the record an exhibit showing the phenomenal increase since 1958 of U.S. imports of selected steel mill products from Japan. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. 

(The document referred to is as follows:) 

1959 

1961. 1962_ 1963_ 

EXHIBIT A 

U.S. imports of selected steel mill products from Japan, 1958-65 

Short tons 241, 641 623, 846 593, 098 594, 610 1,068, 005 

1, 802, 110 1964. 1965 2, 445, 267 4, 406, 298 Senator BAYH. As you can see from this chart, Mr. Chairman, U.S. imports of Japanese steel products have increased 18-fold in 7 yearsfrom 241,641 short tons in 1958 to 4,406,298 short tons in 1965. Present indications are that this rate will increase considerably during 1966. 

I can say without qualification, Mr. Chairman, that we are facing similar competition from the Japanese steel industry throughout the world. I have no basic objection to this, for I am a firm advocate of free enterprise system both for ourselves and for others. But I do submit, Mr. Chairman, that our AID program is dedicated to the fundamental principle that it will not affect adversely our balance-ofpayments problem. It seems clear to me that by indirectly supporting the Japanese steel industry, AID is doing injury to its own stated goals. 

2. The January 31 memorandum in no way affected the quality of the product which originated in Japan and was galvanized in Korea. 

I will submit, at the conclusion of my remarks, a report from an independent firm employed by AID which substantiates allegations I have

made that Japanese black plate is inferior and is galvanized in Korea with insufficient quantities of zinc. The result, as you will see in the report, is that these products purchased in the past must be replaced with greater frequency than higher quality products which adds to the final cost to the American Government. 

AID officials have assured me of their agreement with me on this point and are taking steps to assure better quality in the future. It is in their credit that they are cognizant that steps need to be taken to remedy the extremely inferior quality product that was being sold. 

This product not only wasted tax dollars, but also fanned inflation since more of it had to be purchased. 

(The report referred to is as follows:) REPORT OF INSPECTION, SAIGON, MARCH 15, 1966, UNITED STATES AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT TO VIETNAM (USAID)—SAIGON GALVANIZED FLAT AND CORRUGATED IRON SHEETS ORIGINATING FROM KOREA 

At your request and for your account, but being not provided with any specific name of ship, or Receiver, or particulars relating to any special consignment, we made inquiries in Saigon and Cholon vicinities, from 9th through 14th March 1966, with a view to ascertain the average quality of Korean Galvanized Iron Sheets, flat or corrugated, at the time of delivery after unloading from ships, and also their in-use durability.

According to information picked up on the market, we wish at first to point out that most of the Galvanized Iron sheets imported from Korea to Vietnam during the last twelve months or more are flat sheets. 

What is cost of corrugating

Once received here, they are corrugated locally by several Vietnamese enterprises equipped with machines able to corrugate heaps of ten sheets in one time. whatever their thickness may be. 

It is of common knowledge that Korean factories are using raw Iron originating from Japan, where same product is processed chiefly from salvaged scrap, and therefore gives a lower quality of milled products. 

We have had good opportunities, through previous inspections on some merchandise, to ascertain that Korean Galvanized Iron Sheets were showing two main defects: (1) Iron processed with a high percentage of scrap. (2) Defectuous coating which is quickly weather-worn.

On the other hand, it must be pointed out that Vietnamese wholesalers (most of them being of Chinese origin) are mainly interested in importing Iron Sheets of small thicknesses, since retail prices on such commodity are based on the unit and not on the weight. U.S. gauges most often specified are ranging from 20 to 34, with a higher percentage of 24, 27, 28, 29, 30. As a matter of fact, the thinner the sheets are, the poorer the quality and in-use results. 

For the present inspection, we selected three of the corrugating enterprises located as follows: 

In Saigon : n° 60/62, Ky Con St. 

In Cholon: n° 33, Van Kien St., n° 44, Mac Cuu St. and investigated among various consignments of Korean Galvanized Iron flat sheets during the course of their corrugation.

The names of Korean Suppliers were given by the marks of each consignment, i.e. 

1°) XXX—USO #34-1m x 2n supplied by KUY DONG STEEL CO., LTD., 

Pusan, Korea 2°) XD-USO #34-1m x 2n coating 0.6 oz/n-Made in Korea supplied by 

SHIN HWA SILUP CO., LTD., Seoul, Korea. 

3°) Crown-USO #33—1m x 2n coating 0.6 oz/Ft2 supplied by ILSSIN IN 

DUSTRIAL CO., LTD., Seoul, Korea. 

Our findings 

The sizes of sheets were comformable to specifications 1 x 2 meters. 

The thickness was rather irregular from one sheet to another, but final average was corresponding to specified U.S. gauge, although individual weight per sheet appeared slightly lower.

As a general remark available on both consignments, galvanization was irregular. All Korean factories are known to use spray-processing for Iron galvanization and we ascertained that such processing was not actually satisfactory on the inspected Iron Sheeting. Certain sheets where galvanization is defectuous were showing brightless spots which surface is slightly rough and blistered, where rust will soon appear (see attached samples and pictures). 

However, still according to importers' opinion and since they are made to import same in a highest proportion, Korean Iron sheeting is considered one grade above iron sheeting from Taiwan, which is said to be of the lowest quality. 

Among various Korean Galvanized Iron sheetings, the importers' choice is mostly fixed on "DRAGON" brand.

There is little to say about local corrugation, which is not better but not worse than any other, excepted that all defects shown on a flat sheet are stressed out once same sheet is corrugated. 

Cost of the operation is of about 1 to 1.50 piasters per sheet. Sizes after corrugation are 0.9 x 2 meters.

Then we investigated among consumers here and there in Saigon/Cholon, and requested them to show us in-use Korean Galvanized corrugated Iron sheets, and to give us their opinion about each item after a few months use. 

Most of them were complaining about short life of the sheets they have bought, chiefly during the rainy season. Owing to a rather low quality of material and often defectuous coating, all sheets used as roofs or fences are quickly losing their brightness, thus affected by rust and must be replaced within a shorter period than with Iron sheets of Western origin.

We were not in a position to check how long a Korean Galvanized Iron Sheet in use resists as compared with same item of other origin (Taiwan excepted), but all consumers we approached were unanimous to agree upon the fact that the price they pay for Korean sheeting is less than ever justified by the latter's quality and durability. (See attached samples and pictures.) 

All our inspections are carried out to the best of our knowledge and ability, but without any responsibility neither for us nor for our organization. 

M. O'CONNELL, Manager. 

Senator BAYH. I would simply reiterate that for a long period of time, Korea violated previous policy with the rationale that the Japanese-made steel contained large quantities of U.S. scrap. The Japanese black plate had a large scrap content and was considerably thinner than the thinnest stock item manufactured in the United States. 

It was galvanized in Korea with less than half the amount of zinc that would meet minimum specifications of the U.S. Bureau of Standards. Thus, on three counts, the steel sheet sold to Vietnam under 

AID financing did not meet any reasonable test of quality. 

3. As the result of the impact of the memo, the prices paid by Vietnam with AID financing for inferior products were outrageously exorbitant. Although AID had sought to avert effects on our balance of payments, using tied letters of credit to make payment to eligible countries, profits were so high that they permitted the recipients of the tied letters of credit to subvert easily the intent of these letters to the detriment of the American economy. (The prices referred to are as follows:) 

EXHIBIT "B"

Prices paid by AID for 32 gauge corrugated steel sheets: 

1963-$179 plus $16.50 freight (Korea). 

1964 $270 plus $16.50 freight (Korea). 

1964 $260 plus $16.50 freight (Taiwan). 

1965-$259.50 plus $16.50 freight (Korea).

Senator BAYH. In 1963, the price of a galvanized ton of steel to 

Vietnam, f.o.b. Korea, was $170. In 1964, this price boomed to $270,

and in 1965 it was lowered to $260. The year that the price rocketed upward, the cost of Japanese black plate decreased. Some of the upward cost may be attributed to monetary changes in Korea, but surely the great increase demands investigation and, in my estimation, a change in policy.

While Korea was selling a galvanized ton of 32-gage steel sheet to Vietnam for $260 plus shipping and handling, it was buying black plate from Japan for $140 a ton. Thus, Korea was adding about $120 a ton for galvanizing and handling. Now, most American companies using twice the amount of zinc per square foot and paying their labor many times the amount Korean laborers are paid-charge approximately $60 to $70 a ton for galvanizing sheet steel. In point of fact, Mr. Chairman, a galvanized ton of sheet steel from the United States, shipped directly to Saigon would cost no more-and in some cases less than the Vietnamese were paying for Japanese steel poorly galvanized in Korea and shipped to Saigon under AID financing. 

The reason underlying this odd circumstance is ensconced in documents currently classified as secret. Suffice it to say, however, that a system of kickbacks, collusion, and corruption are at the root of the problem. I would not suggest that members of the AID staff, top or bottom, are involved in this particular type of activity. The procurement system that has been followed makes it almost impossible to avoid practices which none of us want to tolerate. 

Responsible AID officials, in whom I have the greatest confidence, are making every effort to contain this situation, but I see no way in which they can succeed short of imposing artificial price ceilings on these products. Such a method is not only contrived but also does not alleviate the problem of American aid dollars flowing out of the United 

States with no residual benefit. 

4. The problems and cost of effectively policing the quality and price of Japanese-made, Korean-galvanized sheet steel, plus the problems and cost of effectively policing proper use of the tied letters of credit are so enormous as to render them impractical-if not impossible. 

This point not only is relevant to past procedures but as a useful measurement of certain proposed formulas which have come or will come to the attention of this committee.

I have already quoted an official AID report on the problems of effectively policing the 90/10 componentry ratio when the galvanizing is done outside the United States. However, it seems to me more realistic to expect effective policing of the 90/10 ratio than policing the quality of products, prices, and use of letters of credit by non-American firms.

As I have tried to demonstrate regarding inflated profits, we are not dealing with businessmen who play by the rules of Marquis de Queensbury. Letters of credit can be circumvented by those who seriously seek to do so.

The most obvious means is to buy eligible American products and resell them at less than cost. This can be done because the recipients of the letters of credit already have made a killing-profits at about 60 percent higher than normal-in selling steel sheet to Vietnam. 

Thus, to turn these letters of credit into cold, hard cash, they can afford to take a small loss on the products they buy with letters of

credit and still come out profitably in the deal. In the meantime, by offering these products at prices cheaper than those quoted by American companies, they can be assured of quick sales that, in turn, close off potential markets to American firms. In this way, letters of credit do not achieve their purpose of increasing potential American iron and steel sales abroad.

The idea of tying the use of American aid dollars for purchases of American steel by use of letters of credit is a good effort made by some of our top officials in AID. I admire and respect these persons for what they are trying to accomplish. However, it seems to me if policing the previous 90/10 ratio is futile then there is small chance of policing these letters of credit.

The letter of credit, as you know, is designed to insure that any dollars received for black plate or for galvanized products in turn be used to purchase perhaps more sophisticated steel products that are produced in the United States, so that the final business comes from the United States. I have yet to see how it is going to be possible to prevent purchases of these products by holders of these letters of credit for resale to other markets. For example, Korea could purchase a steel lathe, and after it is in Korea's possession, she could ship it practically anywhere in the world and sell it in a market that had existed. for an American lathe.

Again, it seems to me, this whole problem could be prevented-but only by checking every invoice, every bill of lading, every transaction involving the use of the letter of credit. There simply is no other effective way of policing the prohibition against reexport of products purchased with the letters of credit.

The proposal I am offering, Mr. Chairman, would place the purchase of iron and steel products for Vietnam back on the 90/10 componentry ratio.

In effect, it would mean that black plate or finished galvanized products would be bought from American firms-thus being assured of good quality at prices free from corruption and kickbacks. Such a policy would in no way prevent the continuation of the practice of having black plate galvanized in Korea-providing that the Koreans met improved standards for galvanization and charged reasonable prices for their services.

It is important to recognize significant contributions being made by the Korean Government to our war effort in Vietnam. Thousands of Korean soldiers are fighting alongside American troops and Vietnamese soldiers. We must continue to support efforts of the Korean 

Government to improve the economy and welfare of its people. Reports of the fine conduct and high caliber of the Korean troops in the field are noteworthy.

It should be made clear that since 1949, the United States has provided economic assistance to Korea in excess of $2.5 billion. We should certainly continue to contribute generously to the welfare of the Korean people. In the present bill there is a significant amount contained for such purposes in the future. I don't need to go into detail as to the sacrifice made by American families to make it possible for the Koreans to enjoy freedom.

Mr. Chairman, I have supported our foreign aid program with only a few exceptions, primarily in the area of military aid to certain nations.

It has always been my belief that foreign aid, properly administered, is in the best interests of the people of the United States as well as the people who benefit directly. 

In regard to Vietnam, I am doubly sympathetic with our aid program, for I agree with the President of the United States, that the Vietnam question will never be solved through military action alone, but with a determined effort to oppose all the forces and conditions suitable for communism-whether the form of Vietcong terrorists, North Vietnamese regulars, or hunger, poverty, and disease among the people of South Vietnam.

Let us not overlook the fact that the latter, traditional enemies of mankind, are equally a threat to peace and freedom as an armed aggressor.

The United States, in its supporting assistance program, seeks to bolster the free economy of South Vietnam. It does not seek to manage the economy nor does it seek to dictate economic goals or economic policy. Our commercial import program has not been geared to provide what Americans thought necessary for the economy of 

South Vietnam, but to provide what the Vietnamese market demands.

This philosophy must be maintained. It is important, however, that careful scrutiny be given to the type and quantity of commodities being licensed under the commercial import program. If we are not careful, products entering Vietnam with our support may reflect the desires of those Vietnamese motivated by quick, high profits rather than the needs of the people.

For example, when the Vietnamese were using very thin corrugated roofing which deteriorated very rapidly, it would have to be replaced at least twice as fast as thicker-grade roofing. In Vietnam they buy the product by the ton, fabricate the roofing and sell it by the sheet. 

The thinner the sheet the greater the profit per ton. Importers and others who sell this roofing have a vested interest in a thin product. 

I don't think the U.S. Government should contribute to such projects. 

Much more attention must be paid to the priorities given to goods flowing into Vietnam under the commercial import program to insure that the limited financial resources of Vietnam and its overtaxed transportation system will be devoted only to those goods which will be important to the welfare and progress of the Vietnamese people. 

As our military effort has increased in South Vietnam, so has our economic assistance increased. The bill now being considered by this committee would authorize economic assistance for Vietnam far greater than our efforts in any other part of the world. Supporting assistance for South Vietnam is requested in an amount even greater than fiscal year 1966.

The supporting assistance has grown from $216 million in fiscal 1965 to $532 million in fiscal 1966, and is now projected in excess of $550 million for fiscal 1967. As U.S. involvement has grown militarily, it is understandable that our economic involvement also would grow. 

We in Congress recognize the administrative problems that must inevitably result from this rapid increase in AID spending. The AID staff in Saigon has been taxed to its limits and has been unable to oversee properly the level of AID financing. Until recently, just one man staffed the commercial import program. Now fewer than 10-

I think the last report indicated only 6-are engaged in overseeing a commercial import program of $300 million. 

I have tried to demonstrate the problems which have resulted relating to a single commodity representing less than 10 percent of the commercial import program. There are other examples and other problems, Mr. Chairman, that the committee might care to look into at a later date.

Secretary Rusk disclosed recently that certain chemicals imported into Vietnam under AID financing were possibly falling into the hands of the Vietcong for use as explosives.

I learned recently that a Vietnamese businessman was using a highgrade tinplate imported under special priorities for specific use in canning food to manufacture footlockers and trunks for sale to American servicemen. Specifications for products for which licenses are requested under the commercial import program are required to be filed for review by the mission in Saigon prior to issuance of licenses, but it is virtually impossible to make any systematic, thorough review. 

Several men without proper data processing facilities cannot establish and maintain quality and price standards for $300 million worth of diverse commodities. We must recognize that our operation in 

Vietnam today is of such scope that the most sophisticated management techniques, procedures, and equipment are required to insure proper administration.

I know that the Agency is making a concerted effort to add staff to the Saigon mission, but today there are still less than 10 men attempting to control a $300 million program. I recommend that the committee provide authorization for whatever staff and equipment are necessary for the proper administration of our foreign assistance program in Vietnam. The manpower and equipment required to manage effectively the foreign assistance program must be provided. 

Mr. Chairman, as I have advocated an amendment to assure that 

American dollars expended for steel products in our AID program for Vietnam will flow back into the American economy, I have also advocated that steps be taken to spend slightly more money to insure that our huge assistance program is effectively supervised. This small investment will pay for itself many times over. 

I am old-fashioned enough to believe that there is little to be gained from being penny-wise, yet pound-foolish. Just as each of us believes that no expense should be spared so that our fighting men in Vietnam have the best possible equipment, we should see to it that our people there who are fighting the quiet war against want are adequately equipped. 

We must win our objectives on both fronts, Mr. Chairman, if we are to bring about a peace that will bring our boys home to us soon. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Senator Bayh. 

Senator, after listening to your statement, is it your position that the steel importers in Vietnam, for the prices they now pay, should be able to get U.S. steel rather than Japanese steel?

Senator BAYH. Under the prices that have been paid in the past, they can purchase the same product laid down in Saigon within five or ten dollars, some say five below, or five or ten above. I would like to emphasize that the important part is quality.

If a better quality product is purchased, even if it costs slightly more, but lasts four times as long, a lot of money is saved. By buying poorly made products, and especially thin items, it will not stand up well.

Chairman MORGAN. Senator, if galvanized sheets made out of U.S. 

steel would cost more than the present prices in Vietnam, do you feel the price of sheets delivered in Vietnam should be increased to permit sheets made in the United States to be imported?

Senator BAYH. Here again I think you have to take into consideration the quality of the product involved. When we are talking about price, Mr. Chairman, it seems to me we in Congress have to take into consideration the impact on the economy, the taxes that are paid by the corporations as well as the people who work there. 

Chairman MORGAN. Senator, do you believe the United States should insist on procurement of sheets rolled in the United States even though this would end the galvanizing of sheets in Korea? 

Senator BAYH. As I said in my statement, I think if we buy the basic black plate, I have no hesitancy in permitting the Koreans to galvanize it, if they can do it at a competitive price. However, I have two basic concerns. One, I am opposed philosophically to spending foreign aid dollars in a program that permits another nation's industry to have an unfair competitive advantage over industry in this country. Secondly, I am concerned that we get a high-quality product. 

The Bureau of Standards and the American Iron and Steel Institute have conducted durability studies over the past year. An American product will meet these important standards. 

Chairman MORGAN. Senator, I know you have devoted a great amount of study on this particular issue. Am I correct in understanding that the executive branch stated to you that the opportunities for Korea to bid on purchase of galvanized plate is an integral part of the military effort in Vietnam? 

Senator BAYH. Would you repeat the question? 

Chairman MORGAN. Am I correct in my understanding that the executive branch has stated more than once that the opportunities for 

Korean bids to be considered for purchase of galvanized plate for Vietnam are an integral part of the Korean military effort in Vietnam? 

Senator BAYH. I have been told that; yes, sir. 

Chairman MORGAN. Do you believe that is so?

Senator BAYH. I am willing to accept as a good faith effort that such an arrangment has been considered with the Koreans. 

Mr. Chairman, if this type of support must be bought I think we better reexamine our policy. If the numerous aid programs have not indicated a determination to continue to assist the Korean Government, then we better look very closely. I have no compunction whatsoever about letting the Koreans continue to galvanize this product if it is competively priced. 

Chairman MORGAN. In reference to the situation where the black plate might be bought in the United States, transported to Korea, and galvanized there, have you gone into the cost structure in order to determine whether the Koreans would make a profit on a transaction of this kind? 

Senator BAYH. Yes, sir. 

Chairman MORGAN. Is this profit_comparable to what they would make in buying the black plate from Japan?

Senator BAYH. Right now we are furnishing the Korean Government the zinc that is used. This is part of our desire to help the 

Korean Government. The charge made for the galvanizing process is $40. We have been working with the American Iron & Steel Institute, trying to determine what reasonable costs should be. According to our calculations, $21.87 is the reasonable cost of galvanization, leaving $18.13, for a profit of almost 50 percent. I think that is a very reasonable profit. 

Chairman MORGAN. Senator, it is my understanding that over a period of time since the supplemental foreign aid authorization was passed, and your original amendment was removed from the bill, that you have been in close contact with the Executive on this matter and that the Executive has been willing to make some concessions. 

I have here a copy of a letter they sent you yesterday authorizing new procedures to correct the evils you complain of. Have you analyzed that letter and arrived at any conclusions as to the adequacy of these new procedures? 

Senator BAYH. Mr. Chairman, I received that letter about 5 o'clock last night and gave some thought to it overnight. So I am continuing to study it. I do not feel, despite the fact that the executive branch has made this effort in good faith, that even they feel it will do the job. I certainly am not convinced it will, Mr. Chairman. 

First of all, it makes a concession that 50 percent of the commodities would be purchased in the United States. Fifty percent would still be purchased by another means. Half of a rotten apple is still a rotten apple.

What concerns me, Mr. Chairman, is that the AID officials set up standards for policing the corruption which has existed, and that they enforce the standards which will improve the quality of steel in Vietnam.

Mr. Chairman, we are setting up a real octopus here in trying to police not only the purchasing procedure and the bidding procedure, in which there has been obvious collusion. We are also trying to police standards which up to now have been totally inferior; moreover, we have to police the letter of credit, which means we are going to have to police every slip, every bill of lading, every commodity that is purchased through these letters of credit.

We are going to make certain that every lathe that is purchased by a letter of credit in the United States is put in the plant. I can see no way to prevent the purchase and transshipment of goods anywhere in the world in competition with American exports. 

Chairman MORGAN. Senator, do I remember correctly that you say that 10 men supervise the import of $300 million of these commodities that are purchased by AID for Vietnam? 

Senator BAYH. I think only six now. 

Chairman MORGAN. Why are you singling out steel? Out of that $300 million, how much galvanized steel is purchased? 

Senator BAYH. About $50 million. 

Chairman MORGAN. That is $250 million of commodities other than galvanized steel that goes into there under the AID program. Have you done any investigating of the situation with respect to commodities that are purchased besides galvanized steel?

Senator BAYH. In my estimation, there is a need to do this very thing. I mentioned the footlocker episode in which an entirely dif-

ferent commodity, tin plate, was being used under priority for food supplies but is now used instead by profiteers to make footlockers for 

American troops.

We are aware of the commodity that the Secretary of State admitted was reaching the Vietcong and being used for explosives. I am not suggesting that these practices are premeditated and willful. 

The program is so large that upon close examination we could find similar practices in cement, in rubber, as well as other commodities. 

The only commodity that I have gathered all the facts on is galvanized steel. Although the facts that I listed, such as inferior quality, poor business practices, and others, were denied when first made, we have a Government Accounting Office report that I would like for you to inspect, and possibly include in the record if you desire. A part of the report is a classified document that will have to be handled in an appropriate way.

You might also include in the record for your perusal the report made by a French firm which AID hired to conduct an analysis study. 

This independent study substantiates all the claims we originally made. I wish I had all the facts on the other commodities, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. Senator, in your study of the additional cost of buying black plate in the United States and having it galvanized in Korea, have you compared the price before the operation was suspended in November of 1965 with current prices? Have you arrived at any figure of what the additional cost of this particular operation would be today?

Senator BAYH. As I mentioned, it is very close to the same. Some say $5 less, others say- 

Chairman MORGAN. $5 a ton? 

Senator BAYH. Yes.

Chairman MORGAN. Short ton? 

Senator BAYH. Metric ton, which is the unit used to purchase all commodities. Are you familiar, Mr. Chairman, with the report that was compiled by Mr. Spreyer of AID?

Chairman MORGAN. I have read the report. 

Senator BAYH. On page 4 the questionnaire contains the documentation for this statement. I will be glad to put it in the record. It is rather voluminous. 

Chairman MORGAN. You feel then that the additional cost would be about $5 a ton?

Senator BAYH. It could be a little less. It could be $5 or $10 more. 

At the same time, I am concerned about the quality of the product which will make it more durable and probably cheaper in the long run. 

Chairman MORGAN. Senator, would you say that if Korea were required to process U.S. steelplate rather than Japanese, it would increase the cost of the Vietnamese war?

Senator BAYH. I don't think it would cost more in the long run. 

The Iron & Steel Institute, plus the studies we have had by the Bureau of Standards, indicate that a galvanized sheet of U.S. plate lasts about 

12 years. The Korean product lasts about 1 year or less. 

Chairman MORGAN. Most of this is used for roofs, to replace the thatched roofs in the villages damaged in the fighting, isn't it? 

Senator BAYH. Roofs, fencing, and sometimes corrugated piping.

Let it be clear that I am anxious for us to show the Koreans that we

are as compassionate about the freedom in Asia as they are and are willing to assist them in their efforts to help us combat communism in that area.

I think we are being a bit shortsighted, Mr. Chairman, if we in good faith allow a procedure to be established that is so frought with misgivings and opportunities for corruption and impossible to inspect. 

I don't think we can afford to buy corruption as a price for this help. 

Here again, Mr. Chairman, I am not directing any accusations toward any of the governments involved. Unscrupulous men are all over the world, in the United States, as well as other countries. 

Chairman MORGAN. Of course, Senator, I do not like to be too critical of any nation which has furnished 22,000 troops for South Vietnam, as South Korea has. We have to pay part of the cost for these troops and we should not deny the Koreans an opportunity to make a profit. A subcommittee of this committee after a visit to 

Korea last year recommended that Korean exports should be increased, and testimony before this committee indicates that South 

Korea has been making a real comeback economically.

As I said, Korea is the only nation in the world, other than the United States, that is willing to furnish 22,000 troops for the war in Vietnam where they are badly needed. I wonder how much we are hurting a friendly nation that is really doing us a great service. 

Senator BAYH. Mr. Chairman, to my knowledge the Japanese don't have any troops in South Vietnam.

Chairman MORGAN. I am speaking of South Korea. 

Senator BAYH. In my estimation, if we would buy the American black plate and let it be purchased by the Koreans and they galvanize it at a reasonable cost, they would be doing the same thing as they are doing now. 

Chairman MORGAN. Do you feel that if the black plate was purchased in the United States, sent to Korea for galvanizing and then on to South Vietnam you would have no objection to that kind of operation? 

Senator BAYH. No, sir, I would not.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Senator. 

Senator BAYH. Mr. Chairman, we have some cost data that I don't think it is necessary to burden the committee with, but it shows the cost factor conclusively. Should you and the other members want to have this information included in the record please feel free to do so. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, sir.

Mr. ADAIR. I would like to have that in the record. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. 

(The information follows:) 

In a questionnaire to Korean Steel producers AID asked: "What would be your f.o.b. price a metric ton of galvanized iron and/or steel sheet, U.S. gage 32, if you used U.S. black plate laid down in Korea at $190-$220 a metric ton?" 

The responses were as follow: 

1. $283.77 to $313.77. 

2. $283.77 to $313.77. 

3. $280.44 to $310.44. 

4. $306.50 to $336.50. 

5. $300 to $330.00. 

6. $309 to $339.50. 

7. No response. 

The last U.S. sale of comparable steel sheet was on April 5, 1965, and the price of that order was $281 per ton.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. Senator, I am not entirely clear, is it your position as a result of your studies that Japanese sheet or plate could be purchased and sent to Korea for galvanizing and could then be raised to a proper quality level? 

Senator BAYH. I think so; yes, sir.

Mr. ADAIR. If I understand you correctly then it is not a matter really of quality of Japanese plate or of U.S. plate?

Senator BAYH. There is some discussion on this subject in which our iron and steel people are concerned about the quality of Japanese black plate. That does not concern me as much as the existence of a policy which permits extremely thin plate to be imported, whether it is American black plate or Japanese, and then an inferior amount of zinc put on them. This report that I have asked to be put in the record says some of these sheets have rusted before they have left the ship. The chairman is accurate in _stating that we have been trying to increase exports from Korea. To this end the Defense De partment, as you know very well, has been doing a lot of purchasing of commodities in Korea. However, the Defense Department has not purchased any of this thin galvanized material. They won't buy it. 

Mr. ADAIR. Just for purposes of comparison, if plate should be purchased in this country and then sent to Korea for proper galvanizing, I mean up to American standards, would it in your opinion compare in cost with plate bought in Japan and sent to Korea and then treated with the same qualitative type of galvanizing?

Senator BAYH. I think the cost would be slightly more to do it that way instead of buying the galvanized directly from the United States.

When I look at this as a Member of Congress and review the purposes of the foreign aid program, I think we have to take costs into consideration in analyzing the benefits to our economy, the taxes that are paid by the corporations, and the number of workers employed and other national expenses.

Mr. ADAIR. I certainly agree with you on that point and was a little concerned at a point raised by the chairman. I think it certainly is true as both you and the chairman have said, that we have a feeling of the greatest friendliness for the Korean people and appreciation for what their fine troops are doing in Vietnam.

But it certainly seems to me that if we are going to help Korea, if she needs military assistance to carry on her effort in Vietnam and her effort at home, then we ought to do it forthrightly and directly and not through this means, as you have pointed out. 

This is no way to strengthen the Korean economy if it results in an improper product being delivered or inadequate product being delivered to Vietnam.

A final line of questioning, and I suspect Mr. Gaud will enlarge upon this later: Is it necessary in your opinion to have legislation or could

we as a committee feel that the efforts which you have delineated would be adequate without legislation?

Senator BAYH. Mr. Chairman, I feel very strongly that legislation should be preferred. I do not know what commitments have been made by those at the State Department. Although hints have been made, I don't know the exact details. I think it is important for the 

Congress of the United States to show the executive branch as well as other governments what the policy of our country is. If AID has made certain commitments that they feel should be made, I believe these commitments could be turned into another area. 

Mr. ADAIR. Might legislation not be a weapon in the hands of AID to assure that a proper product at a proper price is put down in Vietnam?

Senator BAYH. I think legislation might relieve AID of its present commitments.

Mr. ADAIR. In fact, we might be doing them a kindness. 

Senator BAYH. If I might insert one other factor here relative to the question that the chairman raised about prices, and in reviewing some of the material that we have assembled, it has been pointed out that we delivered this material in 1965 f.o.b. Saigon for $281. At the same time, Saigon officials were buying it for $290 from Korea. I think 

American industry can be competitive. If we pull the black plate out, it seems to me according to the estimates we have that it is going to cost slightly more.

I would be willing to pay that extra amount, one, to help the 

Koreans; two, to retain the benefits for our economy.

Mr. ADAIR. If the Koreans need assistance for their military efforts as I said a little while ago, in some respect why not approach that directly and not by means of giving assistance in this way? 

Senator BAYH. I agree. 

Mr. ADAIR. Are these profits going to Korean businessmen or to the 

Korean Government? I am not clear on that point.

Senator BAYH. In my estimation some of the profits are going to the Korean businessmen, but the unexplainable and inexcusable profits are going to some shysters in Vietnam. The information is contained in the secret document that I will be glad to let the committee study. 

Mr. ADAIR. Is the amendment which you are proposing now precisely the same amendment which was removed from the supplemental bill in conference?

Senator BAYH. We took out one factor, the cost factor, believing that that would be too complicated. It is almost identical, and Congressman Adair, Mr. Chairman, and members of the committee, I am not wed to the wording of this amendment. We used the lan guage from the Small Business Administration memo which AID 

used when it first established this 70/30 and then 90/10 ruling, believing it would pin the formula down, so that the intent of Congress could not be misunderstood.

We wanted to make it abundantly clear in the light of past practices of what was wanted.

Mr. ADAIR. I share your views that there is a good reason to have congressional intent spelled out. 60-929-66-pt. 6- -7

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Farbstein. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Senator, I welcome you. Frankly I feel basically the same way that you do, that we have to protect our interests. Let's see if I understand this correctly. Do you want to stop imports of Japanese steel into Korea for the galvanizing of that steel and plate? 

Senator BAYH. Yes, sir; if we pay for it. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. I have that clear now. What is the import of your amendment? Is it that anything that is paid for by the Agency for 

International Development consist of at least 90 percent from the United States, emanating from the United States, and only 10 percent from other areas?

Senator BAYH. From the United States or from some underdeveloped area, such as Korea or Taiwan. In the original measure we could buy commodities from any place in the world. 

Then we found that some of the industry that we had set up after the war in Japan and in West Germany particularly, had used our Marshall plan aid and other funds to construct industry which was able to compete with our own industry. 

First we established the 70/30 and then the 90/10 ruling, believing that we would be acting within the purposes of our foreign policy by allowing these purchases to be made in underdeveloped countries but not the developed countries. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Senator, don't you believe as a result of the war that is presently existing in southeast Asia that this situation has arisen? 

That this is a special situation rather than a general situation and that if it were not for the existence of the war in Vietnam, this objection that you make wouldn't exist?

Senator BAYH. That is right. There is no question. I think a special situation does exist and our responsibility in Congress is to try to find means to prevent corruption and to achieve high standards of quality for products.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. This will not only affect this particular area but this will affect the entire world; will it not?

Senator BAYH. This is the only place in the world where this exception is made now. that 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. You are trying to create a principle, I assume, from here on in-unless it is in the law today I am not awareany money expended for foreign countries under our aid program must consist of American export merchandise to the extent of 90 percent of the money expended. 

Senator BAYH. That is exactly what I am trying to do. I would like to point out this ruling is in effect for the rest of the world; a special exception has been made for South Vietnam. I am trying to bring the same ruling into effect in South Vietnam that exists for other areas of the world.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Do you feel that the existence of the war should make for any deviation of this principle or not? 

Senator BAYH. No, sir; I do not. I think that the existence of the war should certainly make us face the situation and take the necessary steps. One of the steps that is necessary is to provide corrugated roofing for our pacification program down there. I have no objection to that. I have supported the appropriations.

If we could administer this program so we are not paying exorbitant prices in kickbacks to profiteers and getting an inferior product- Mr. FARBSTEIN. Someone once said that confusion is par for the course in wars. It would seem to me that any deviation from the rule that is followed in connection with the 90/10 might perhaps be excused because of this special situation in Vietnam. Basically I go along with you. I am just wondering whether in view of this situation we shouldn't be a bit more lenient, not a black or white field, but a gray field and when this situation is over, perhaps we could go back to the norm.

Senator BAYH. I cannot go to my constituents supporting a policy which permits cartels in a country to get together and determine prices so that the bids are rigged. I can't permit the existence of a policy that will let individual black marketeers or importers in a country, to blackmail those that have goods to sell for kickbacks. Because the situation is so complicated it seems to me that we have a greater responsibility to adhere to a policy that will minimize the confusion. 

The formula which has been followed in the document that the chairman referred to-the letter which I am certain you will hear more about this morning-is so clear about additional needs for policemen that it is not going to be possible to police it in any way, shape, or form. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. The understanding I have as a result of the testimony that I have heard here this morning is, the question of price doesn't enter quite so much. The question of cartels doesn't enter because in the final analysis the steel that will come from this country will cost more than the steel that will come from any other part of the world. 

How can we say the situation will be affected by a cartel?

Senator BAYH. The steel that will come from this country will not cost more than the price that has been paid in the past. That price has been manipulated in any number of different ways, to the point that exorbitant profits have been made. All I am asking, sir, is that we, first, let American businessmen have the chance to make these goods if they are going to be given away in the first place, and secondly, that we demand a quality that will be durable. In the present situation we are not helping the Vietnamese or anyone else.

I am not so much concerned about helping the Vietnamese importer as I am the person who lives out in the rice paddy or hamlet. If he is buying a product that deteriorates at the end of 1 year, and must go back to buy another piece of steel, then inflation is being encouraged. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. As I say, I go along with you, basically. What I am trying to find is a basis for objection at this time. You talk about corruption and cartels and everything that is evil, and yet we charge, we get more for our steel than they get for their steel with all this evil. 

How does the question of cartel enter into it? How does the question of corruption enter into it when our price is still above theirs? 

This is the point I am trying to clarify in my mind. 

Senator BAYH. There is not a substantial difference in price as far as the laid down price in Saigon and the price that was being charged. 

In my estimation the facts will substantiate that the price being charged was higher than we can eventually purchase the product for

if we could eliminate some of the hanky-panky that is going on behind the scene.

I am willing to pay a little more for an American product, give the business to our country, and get a quality item, to assure myself that the standard of that product will hold up. 

Chairman MORGAN. The gentleman's time has expired. 

Mr. Whalley. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Senator, thank you for the statement which certainly shows a discrepancy in prices. You say Korea charges $120 a ton for galvanizing and handling. American companies using twice the amount of zinc per square foot charge $60 to $70 a ton. What do the specifications call for? Is it twice the amount or is Korea using a light amount?

Senator BAYH. There have been no specifications until recently. 

When we brought this matter to the attention of AID, which first denied such activities and later ordered a study to be made, it was obvious that an inferior quality product was being purchased. AID has taken steps now to try to increase the quality. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Are they using the same amount of zinc today that we are?

Senator BAYH. Right now it is a moot question because we are uncertain whether the purchases have been suspended. The future program, proposed by AID, would initiate an inspection procedure which would make the Korean product adhere to the standards of the U.S. product. The U.S. product is the standard that we are trying to follow. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Would the price of $120 in Korea stay the same even though the quality is increasing? 

Mr. BAYH. I don't know.

Mr. WHALLEY. Thank you, sir. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Murphy. 

Mr. MURPHY. No questions. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. McDowell. 

Mr. MCDOWELL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Senator, I am sorry that we in this committee don't provide very much glamour for you when you come over here. 

Senator BAYH. I am not after glamour, Mr. McDowell, thank you.

We would like to have this committee room over in the Senate, by the way. 

Mr. MCDOWELL. No, thank you. I think we prefer to stay here. It is quieter.

Senator, do I understand that it is your feeling-first of all, of course, we all agree with you that we want to see this done on a businesslike basis and want to cut out these kickbacks and any corruption that is involved in the transactions—but do you base any of your premise upon the fact that this isn't, as you said, an inferior quality now, that the U.S. steel industry can furnish a better quality product not necessarily at a lower price; is that correct? 

Senator BAYH. That is one of my conclusions.

Mr. McDOWELL. Do you have any indication from the U.S. steel industry operating practically at maximum production today, that

they would be able to absorb this increased business if it came to them? 

Senator BAYH. Yes, sir; I have gone to some great length discussing this with a dozen or more companies. I think the commodity will be available.

Mr. McDOWELL. Had the U.S. steel industry been supplying this market heretofore?

Senator BAYH. They were prior to the practice which started back in 1963 or thereabouts to purchase the commodity from Japan, galvanize it in Korea and ship it on to Saigon. The French supplied most commodities when Vietnam was a colony. Because the French demanded a high quality product, they would not let thin, poorly galvanized material into the country.

Mr. McDOWELL. If your proposed amendment were written into the aid program, or into the law, would this then have the effect of fully correcting this situation permanently? 

Senator BAYH. I think 

Mr. MCDOWELL. Would it apply to other materials other than this particular type of steel product?

Senator BAYH. This proposed amendment would apply only to steel. 

In my estimation it would go as far as we can reasonably expect. 

Some policing will still be needed. I agree with Mr. Farbstein who said that it is extremely confusing down there. For that reason it is in our best interest to have the simplest procedure we possibly can, buy a good quality product in the United States. My proposal would permit us to police and regulate sales, ease the bidding procedure, and eliminate the need to police letters of credit. The plan that will be proposed by AID will be (1) to police the quality, (2) to establish an entirely different control for bidding procedures which have been plagued with kickbacks in the past, (3) and the most difficult task to police these letters of credit.

If we give them money to buy or if we extend letters of credit to buy galvanized steel, these letters of credit are tied to the purchase of iron and steel products in the United States.

In other words, they could use these letters of credit to buy lathes, say, or steel bars, but there is still no way to prevent reexportation. 

How in the world are we going to tell where the Koreans transship these steel bars or lathes? We would have to go through every bill of lading and police every ship when it comes into port. Today, for example, they could buy a steel lathe with one of these letters of credit; it could go through the subterfuge of a port, and end up in Australia, India or any other place in the world.

I know that AID believes this proposal is a good idea but it is humanly impossible for them to police it. I think right now they have a lot of other areas that they ought to worry about policing rather than setting up some sort of ingenious scheme to add to their burden instead of easing it. 

Mr. McDOWELL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Thomson. 

Mr. THOMSON. Senator, through all of your testimony seems to be a thread of concern for the American steel industry. 

Senator BAYH. Yes, sir. 

Mr. THOMSON. You have said that we must take into consideration the costs and the taxes paid by our corporations and the effect on our

workers. Could you tell us for the record what the rate of production of the American steel industry is at the present time? 

Senator BAYH. I have not been able to find that. 

Mr. THOMSON. Isn't it in excess, perhaps, of 95 percent?

Senator BAYH. All I have been able to get from the American steel industry from investigation and correspondence is that we could meet this additional demand.

Mr. THOMSON. There is probably no doubt about that. But I am wondering what the rate of productivity is here now. Couldn't we get that from the Commerce Department? 

Senator BAYH. Perhaps we could. 

Mr. THOMSON. Perhaps then you could tell me what the rate of unemployment is today in the steel industry in America? 

Senator BAYH. It is not very great. 

Mr. THOMSON. Practically no unemployment? 

Senator BAYH. That is right. 

Mr. THOMSON. Could you tell me what the present rate of profits is today in the U.S. steel industry? 

Senator BAYH. No, sir; I cannot.

Mr. THOMSON. Aren't they running at the highest level of profits in the history of the country?

Senator BAYH. I am certain the steel industry as well as all other industries are running very well-that is no reason for us to be giving some of it away if we can produce it here under sound businesslike procedures. I don't care if our steel workers in Gary or Youngstown or wherever it might be throughout the world are working 8 hours a day 6 days a week. If he can work 9 hours and make extra money, I am for that and I am for the stockholders getting a little extra money.

I think it is a dangerous precedent to set that after profits get so high and unemployment rate goes so low, we are going to put a lid on profits or find some way to expend them in some other part of the world.

To expand on this question, because this is a very realistic line of questioning, I am concerned about the precedent that might be set here. We are talking about not over $50 million now, as the chairman pointed out, of the $500 million AID program to be spent in Vietnam this next fiscal year.

I should hope that we do not establish a precedent that will permit us to use foreign aid dollars to purchase products from other countries that can be purchased as well as or better in this country. In my estimation, the study that we did in cooperation with the steel industry showed that between two-thirds and three-fourths of all the exports of the steel industry are purchased by AID. I would hate to establish a precedent in a $50 million program that would permit us to lower the bars to erode the export position of our steel industry. 

Mr. THOMSON. Senator, if we added $5 a ton to add additional galvanizing to Korean steel, we would still be buying it for the same price, would we obtain a better quality if we did that? 

Senator BAYH. I am not certain. Do you mean Japanese steel?

Mr. THOMSON. Buying it from Korea and asking them to galvanize it to a little greater degree. You say the difference in their price and the American price is only about $5 a ton.

Senator BAYH. It would be more than that, I think, if we went that route rather than buying our galvanizing—it would cost a bit more. 

We would get better quality because we would demand greater thickness. Here again the issue that concerns me more than anything else is that the standards that we have adhered to in the past, and that we may adhere to in the future, will still permit the use of thinner material that will not last as long as the product that we demand. I think this is very shortsighted.

Mr. THOMSON. Have there been complaints from Vietnam about the poor quality of steel they are purchasing?

Senator BAYH. In the French report that will be a part of the record, there is an indication this report refers to Koreans, who complain about the short life of the sheets they bought, chiefly during the rainy season. The low quality material and defective coating no doubt account for their poor experience with this steel. 

All sheets used on roofs and fences are quickly losing their brightness and then streaked by rust and must be replaced in a shorter period than with iron sheets of Western origin.

What we have differed with AID about is that they feel that policing the galvanizing process will solve the problem. They admit that our original charges about inferior galvanizing are true; they now say this can be done solely by increasing the thickness of the galvanized coat.

From the material we have been able to gather from the tests that are made in this country, I have to take the word of experts who know this business because I am a relative newcomer to the galvanized field, the total life of a galvanized sheet is dependent on two things (1) the coating and (2) the thickness of the base plate. The thinner the base plate the more likely the galvanized coating is to wear away and deteriorate under the weather. 

Mr. THOMSON. Then the ultimate solution is to buy it all in America? 

That is it; isn't it? 

Senator BAYH. That is the way I look at it.

Mr. THOMSON. You mentioned besides quality and cost the matter of profits, and you said the profits were going to shysters in Vietnam. 

That is a term commonly applied to certain classes of attorneys. 

You didn't want to imply that, did you? 

Senator BAYH. As a member of the bar, no, sir, I did not. 

Mr. THOMSON. I am glad to have that 

Senator BAYH. If I might, Mr. Thomson, I think it is important that I make abundantly clear to the committee and to some of our friends from the press that my remarks are directed at a few unscrupulous individuals in the countries involved, not at the great bulk of the people, many of whom are at the mercy of these unscrupulous ones. My criticisms are not directed at governments involved; we consider these governments to be our very good friends. 

Mr. THOMSON. Senator, have you given any thought to beefing up the staff of the Inspector General and maybe putting the end use investigators in those foreign countries under his supervision so that we will have a more adequate staff to run down this type of illegal conduct that you complain of?

Senator BAYH. I think that is one possibility. The issue that concerns me more is the fact that AID seems to be attempting to do an

impossible job now. I don't know how they are doing as well as they are in administering such a large program with only a few staff members. I think it would be a good investment in our foreign aid dollars to provide for a few more staff. They want to do a good job, but it is very difficult now. Additional personnel that want to do the job well could help relieve this problem. 

I seriously question the advisability of a new program which will require more inspection, more testing and treating than the present program does. 

Mr. THOMSON. Have you considered whether AID can recruit people in competition with the salaries that the poverty program is paying? 

Senator BAYH. No, sir; I have not. I do know they are having trouble getting people of high quality as in the case in many fields of government. While I sympathize with this problem it only substantiates my argument. Why give overburdened staff a more difficult formula to police before recruiting more qualified people? 

Mrs. BOLTON. Mr. Chairman, may I take a little of my time for just a moment? I think that we are all hearing a great deal about another metal. Why don't we put everything into aluminum?

Senator BAYH. I am certain that some of my constituents, Mrs. Bolton, would be very happy to hear you say that. 

Mrs. BOLTON. You spoke of having galvanized things on your farm.

I have a son who has a farm. He is slowly getting it into aluminum and the difference is amazing. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Senator BAYH. I also use some of that aluminum.

Mr. HAMILTON. Mr. Chairman, I have no questions. I want to commend Senator Bayh for an excellent job. I think the Senator is to be highly congratulated for his investigation and for coming before this committee with that amendment.
