COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C., March 22, 1966.

Hon. DAVID E. BELL, 

Administrator, Agency for International Development, Washington, D.C. 

DEAR MR. BELL: The discussion of the Bayh amendment to H.R. 12169, supplemental foreign assistance authorization for fiscal year 1966, and its consideration by the Committee of Conference indicate the desirability of a comprehensive review of the procurement of iron and steel products for Vietnam and of the supplying of such products to Vietnam by Korean manufacturers.

It is essential that the Committee on Foreign Affairs have a more complete report of the facts with respect to these matters, and I believe that the Agency for International Development is in a better position to collect this information in a short time than anyone else.

Would you undertake to submit to us everything you regard as significant, including analyses of the following issues: 

1. Could galvanized sheets of satisfactory quality financed by the United States be obtained more cheaply from any other source or under any other arrangement? 

2. Is the galvanizing industry a more effective vehicle for stimulating the 

Korean economy in terms of creating employment or developing manufacturing facilities, or for political impact, than other industries or operations to which the 

United States might give preferred treatment? 

3. Is the United States steel industry getting a significant volume of business from Korea as a result of the restricted letters of credit now being issued? 

4. What would be the impact on the United States steel industry in terms of production and of jobs if means could be found to arrange for procurement in the United States of the galvanized sheets required for Vietnam or of the steel for galvanizing in Korea or elsewhere in the Far East? 

5. Under the regulations now in effect, is competition with Korea for the Vietnamese market for galvanized sheets developing? Is it likely that such competition may have an adverse economic or political effect on Korea? If Korea loses its present "monopoly," will it be necessary for the United States to work out some other special arrangement to attain our political or economic objectives for Korea?

Your analysis and recommendations on this entire subject will be appreciated. 

Sincerely yours,

THOMAS E. MORGAN, Chairman. 

DEPARTMENT OF STATE, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT, OFFICE OF THE ADMINISTRATOR, 

Washington, D.C., May 12, 1966.

Hon. THOMAS E. MORGAN, Chairman, Committee on Foreign Affairs, 

House of Representatives, Washington, D.C.

DEAR MR. CHAIRMAN: We have examined the matters raised in your letter of March 22, 1966 concerning the Bayh Amendment on procurement of galvanized iron products for Vietnam while working on a resolution of the problems in this element of our program. 

Historically, sources of galvanized iron products under the A.I.D. commercial import program for Vietnam have been in the Far East, because the suppliers are nearby and the cost of transportation is low. A.I.D. has, however, been progressively restricting sources for supply. At first, any free world country was eligible, then the industrial countries (such as Japan) were eliminated. 

Very recently the list was further restricted to only nine less-developed countrie's which have agreed to accept payment by tied letters of credit. Under this arrangement, all dollars earned by the supplier country must be spent in the United States. In addition to the above, A.I.D. also sought to enforce a requirement that 70 percent-later changed to 90 percent of the value of an item sold by eligible countries must have originated therein or in the United States. 

It was this last requirement which posed a problem in the sale of galvanized sheets to Vietnamese importers by Korea and the Republic of China. The 

Koreans argued that they were complying with the purposes of the 90 percent rule by buying iron scrap in the United States to be used in making iron sheet in Japan, and additional U.S. scrap to pay much of the Japanese fabricating cost. 

The arrangement was not acceptable to A.I.D., however, because: (1) it was not clear that the additional scrap was additive to normal U.S. exports: (2) it was extremely difficult to police; and (3) Korea was the only eligible Far 

Eastern country to devise this means of claiming compliance with the 90 percent rule and had, therefore, no Far Eastern competition on galvanized products to keep its prices down.

We suspended further A.I.D. financing of Korean galvanized products in 

November 1965 until we could work out what we considered a more satisfactory arrangement. In January, we devised and issued a new procurement regulation on this commodity for Vietnam only. Under the new policy, we limited the sources for galvanized sheets sold to Vietnam under A.I.D. commercial

import financing to (1) the United States, which is always eligible; and (2) to those Far Eastern countries which accept letters of credit tied to purchase of iron and steel machine tools, metal-working machines and a limited amount of steel scrap from the United States.

Under the new A.I.D. procedure, Korea could purchase its iron sheet for galvanizing from Japan, but every A.I.D. dollar earned on sale's to Vietnam of galvanized products must be spent by Korea in the United States for iron and steel products, including tools with high iron and steel content or a small amount of iron and steel scrap.

In devising the new procedure, which was on a trial basis, A.I.D. had in mind five principal considerations: (1) the need for nearby sources of roofing and other products at delivered prices that would not feed inflation in Vietnam; (2) the desire to permit Korea, which is making a major contribution to our military effort in Vietnam, to at least have a chance to continue sales to that country; (3) protection of the U.S. balance of payments; (4) promotion of U.S. steel exports; and (5) competitive, fair prices.

We considered and rejected the alternative of insisting that Korean or other Far East suppliers use only U.S. blackplate in the galvanized products, because (a) this would substantially raise galvanized product prices they could quoteprobably above the high U.S. prices on galvanized products-and, consequently, (b) might cause the Vietnamese Government to finance these imports with its own foreign exchange, with all this business going to the lowest price source, Japan. In either event, this would effectively deny Korea continued sales to 

Vietnam at a time it was considering doubling its 20,000 man troop commitment to Vietnam.

As a result of intensive investigations made by A.I.D. in the Far East, we have concluded that prior actions taken by this Agency needed to be supplemented by even more restrictive measures. We have recently instituted the following measures to complement and supplement the procedure established in January, 1966: (1) galvanized iron and steel products now must meet U.S. commercial standards of 1.25 oz. of zinc coating per sq. foot; (2) the items must be inspected by an independent testing and inspection agency in the source country; (3) the bidding and award system is to be so arranged to preclude collusion between suppliers or suppliers and importers; (4) fifty percent value must be from the 

United States; (5) price must be reasonable and honestly competitive; (6) Far

East suppliers must have an opportunity to make offers, but if successful will receive payment under A.I.D.'s restricted tide letter of credit procedure, with scrap, however, eliminated. A copy of our new procedure is enclosed. 

We believe the revised procedure is the best means of serving several United States interests in this matter. It will take some time before the tied letters of credit are translated into orders for U.S. iron and steel products. When these orders do start flowing, they will inevitably increase our iron and steel and tool exports to countries which have been only minor customers of these U.S. industries. Since it is estimated that A.I.D.-financed commercial imports of galvanized sheet by Vietnam will total $25 million, in calendar 1966, a substantial boost will be given U.S. iron and steel and tool exports when the tied A.I.D. payments to Far Eastern suppliers are spent in the United States. 

The following are answers to your specific questions: 

1. "Could galvanized sheets of satisfactory quality financed by the United

States be obtained more cheaply from any other source or under any other arrangement?”

No, unless we are willing to finance procurement from Japan. With respect to the 50 percent of the business allocated to the United States alone under our revised rules, we could obtain sheet more cheaply from Far Eastern sources, if they were permitted to use Japanese blackplate. 

2. "Is the galvanizing industry a more effective vehicle for stimulating the 

Korean economy in terms of creating employment or developing manufacturing facilities, or for political impact, than other industries or operations to which the United States might give preferred treatment?"

In connection with question 2, I invite your attention to the "Report of the 

Special Study Mission to the Far East, Southeast Asia, India, and Pakistan, No vember 7-December 12, 1965," under the Chairmanship of Congressman Clement

J. Zablocki. This report to the House Foreign Affairs Committee recommends 

(at page 13): "The United States should do everything possible to develop and expand Korea's export market, including items produced in Korea which might be valuable to the U.S. military and economic effort in Vietnam."

After listing illustrative items which might be furnished by Korea, the Report concludes, "Some [items] might successfully find commercial markets in Vietnam or be purchased through U.S. aid programs in Vietnam. Responsible U.S. officials should thoroughly explore these possibilities.”

A cessation of A.I.D.-financed purchases would deprive Korea of all exports of galvanized products. The Korean industry has no export possibilities except 

A.I.D.-financed purchases because the highly efficient Japanese industry can undersell any competitor in the Far East. The point which distinguishes the galvanizing trade from others in Korea is that it has had the Vietnam business; 

Korea would be deprived of a chance to continue to bid for its continuance in the absence of the new A.I.D. procedure. 

3. "Is the United States steel industry getting a significant volume of business from Korea as a result of the restricted letters of credit now being issued?" 

The U.S. steel industry will get the benefit of whatever galvanized products sales are made to Vietnam by Far East countries. All dollars these countries earn will return to the U.S. to buy U.S. iron and steel, machine tools and tools with a high steel component. No effect can be yet demonstrated because no transactions have been executed under this system. 

4. "What would be the impact of the United States steel industry in terms of production and of jobs if means could be found to arrange for procurement in the United States of the galvanized sheets required for Vietnam or of the steel for galvanizing in Korea or elsewhere in the Far East?"

If all the procurement of either black sheet or the finished galvanized sheet for Vietnam were directed to the United States it would certainly require some additional man-hours of U.S. labor but we cannot quantify the number of additional jobs. U.S. sheet production (and galvanizing) is highly automated. It is our belief, however, that the manufactured iron and steel products and tools which must be purchased in the United States, with the dollar earnings of Far East suppliers of galvanized products to Vietnam, will involve considerably more labor and have greater impact in terms of production. 

5. "Under the regulation now in effect, is competition with Korea for the Vietnamese market for galvanized sheets developing? Is it likely that such competition may have an adverse economic or political effect on Korea? If Korea loses its present 'monopoly,' will it be necessary for the United States to work out some other special arrangement to attain our political or economic objectives for Korea?" 

Korea had competition on tenders received in Saigon on March 31 and April 4, from Taiwan, Thailand, Singapore, and the United States. (No business has been awarded under these tenders, which will have to be rebid under the new procedure.)

Although Korea will lose its recent Far East monopoly advantage, we doubt seriously that it will lose a sufficient amount of the market to require the United States to work out a compensating special arrangement to attain our political or economic objectives in Korea. We have responded to Korea's requests to be an eligible source of supply, and have permitted it to bid for sales under its existing pattern of trade, rather than to requests for an exclusive position in the market. In fact, the new system is designed to provide Far Eastern competition for Korean galvanized products, in order to assure reasonable prices. 

Provision for Korean opportunities to bid under the circumstances described, even with U.S. and Far Eastern competition, will be a sufficient response to reasonable Korean export promotion ambitions as far as galvanized product sales to Vietnam are concerned. 

If you desire further information or clarification, please let me know. 

Sincerely yours,

GALVANIZED SHEET PROCEDURE DAVID E. BELL. 

The following procedure has been devised to meet the criteria for supply of galvanized iron and steel sheet: 

1. Quality must be acceptable to U.S. standards. 

2. Price must be reasonable and honestly competitive. 

3. Fifty percent of the dollar value must be from U.S. sources. 

4. Far East suppliers must have an opportunity to make offers but if successful will receive payment in letters of credit requiring all dollar earnings be spent in the U.S. for iron and steel products and machine tools.

It is proposed that: 

1. All Vietnam commercial import requirements of galvanized iron and steel sheet shall comply to ASTM Specification A-525-64T under coating class desig nated as 1.25 oz. commercial, subject to inspection at source by an independent inspection company. 

2. No sheet thinner than gauge 31 shall be eligible for A.I.D. financing. 

3. Procurement of galvanized iron and steel sheet shall be under formal bidding, the sealed bids to be opened in the presence of an A.I.D. observer, but otherwise privately. 

4. The Director of Commercial Aid will aggregate requests for quotations received from Vietnamese importers and divide them into two gauge groups as nearly as possible equal in dollar value. He will then issue two invitations for bid: (a) U.S. only for the thicker (lower numbered) gauges, and (b) U.S. and eligible Far East sources for the thinner group. All Far East suppliers would be paid on basis of Small Business Memo 64-4A-26, March 29, 1966, including the restricted tied letter of credit except that scrap would be eliminated as eligible commodity under the tied letter of credit and from the blacksheet requirement. 

5. Invitations for bids will require that each bidder forward: (a) One set of sealed bids to DCA. (b) One duplicate set of bids to A.I.D., Washington, D.C. 

6. The DCA will receive offers, open bids in private, but with USAID/Vietnam present, tabulate and evaluate prices and quantities. 

7. When the bids are received A.I.D. will furnish to DCA a confidential ceiling price established for each gauge on the date bids are due or thereafter. Any 

Far East bidder's price quotation in excess of this pre-established ceiling will be automatically rejected. 

8. The DCA will quote prices by gauge and secure confirmation and earnest money from each Vietnamese importer of the total tonnage of each gauge he will accept. The DCA will not then disclose suppliers or source countries. 

9. On basis of these confirmed tonnages, the Director of Commercial Aid will: (a) Determine lowest cost offers. (b) Allocate not less than fifty percent of the dollar value of the total business to low bidder among the U.S. bidders. (c) Allocate balance to low bidders among the Far East suppliers. In the event that there are insufficient U.S. bids to total half the business, the DCA shall award to Far East bidders an amount not exceeding the amount awarded to the U.S. A second bid invitation, again split 50-50, maybe required. In order to balance U.S. and Far East awards, the DCA may split a gauge between sources. If so, he will average or blend the prices and split the sheet supplied each importer. (d) Open letters of credit in favor of successful bidders, including among provisions of the letters of credit: (1) Instructions to suppliers to consign shipments to DCA. (2) Requirement for an inspection certificate from an independent inspection company if merchandise originates in a non-U.S. source country. 

10. After contractual arrangements have been completed, including issuance of letters of credit to successful bidders, all participating bidders will be sent copies of notices of awards. Such notices will be publicly displayed by the DCA and will be furnished to USAID/Vietnam in sufficient copies for transmittal of one set to A.I.D., Washington, D.C. 

11. When shipments have been made by the supplier, DCA will notify participating importers in a timely manner of the anticipated arrival of the merchandise, will arrange for deposit of balance of counterpart to the extent this deposit has not already been covered by payment of earnest money, and license assignment of the merchandise to the importers following clearance through customs. 

12. USAID/Vietnam would advise GVN and importers that if irregularities are found in this procedure, we reserve the right to insist on U.S. procurement authorities handling entire import of galvanized sheet.

Chairman MORGAN. I know you and I are interested in getting business for U.S. companies. Is it correct that under the procedure proposed by the administration the U.S. steel producers would get 50 percent of the business? 

Senator BAYH. That is the implication of the letter; yes, sir.

Chairman MORGAN. Also, isn't it correct that they would get an equal amount under a tied letter of credit requiring Korea to spend the other 50 percent of these dollars for U.S. finished steel products? 

Senator BAYH. They could compete for the other 50 percent.

Chairman MORGAN. I don't think we are too far apart here in arriving at some satisfactory solution, Senator. I think this testimony today has been very valuable. It has been particularly valuable to me because when I went to conference to consider your amendment I did a certain amount of soul-searching before we removed it from the authorization. I am for selling as much American steel around the world as we can sell. 

I wondered if you and your staff would give the procedures AID proposes some serious thought before we start marking up this bill which will be in another week or 10 days and see if we cannot come up with some compromise arrangement that both AID and the steel companies could live with? Do you think that is possible?

Senator BAYH. I will be very glad to study the letter. As I say, received it last evening. It has obviously received a lot of thought and attention. Again the issue that concerns me is whether we might still have a bad program. Remedying one half of the program, does not make that other remaining half any better. Despite the rather detailed proposals that are made in the letter, each one will require more policing, not less. Right now AID has its hands full getting programs under control in Saigon without establishing an intricate procedure that is going to require additional policing. 

Chairman MORGAN. I am very much impressed with your statement that we should have additional end-use supervisors to ride herd on the imports that are going in there- 

Senator BAYH. Primarily South Vietnam. 

Chairman MORGAN. Yes, South Vietnam. We have a very friendly relationship with South Korea. As you pointed out we have spent two and a half billion dollars to help South Korea and our relationships are good. As I said before, the South Korean economy apparently is on the way back and we want it to continue to improve. If we get American black plate and send it to our friendly neighbors in South Korea, even if it cost us a few dollars more and if we can get a good quality product for use in South Vietnam, I think maybe it could be worked out. I see no reason why we should deny the economic benefit to South Korea which they get from galvanizing the steel, and as I read your remarks in the record at the time you offered your amendment in the Senate I think that was your line of thinking. 

Senator BAYH. I have said that from the beginning. I have offered such a proposal in each conversation with AID officials. With all due respect, I think that would be a solution that we could all accept. 

Chairman MORGAN. I note that you stated, "I would not want my amendment to be interpreted in any way as interfering with our friendly relations with South Korea." I want to make clear that I, as one Member of the Congress, greatly appreciate the help that South 

Korea is giving us in South Vietnam.

If it can be worked out so that black plate can be bought in this country and sold to South Korea and they can galvanize it and if the inspector says it is up to standard it can be shipped to South Vietnam, I am all for that.

Senator BAYH. I see the way you do on that particular subject.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. I am not clear upon one point. Is it your understanding, and the gentleman from AID can enlighten us on this later, that AÏD has or has not now established quality standards for this product in Vietnam ! 

Senator BAYH. They have proposed these standards. 

Mr. ADAIR. What does that mean?

Senator BAYH. This means they are going to do a better job of checking, taking samples, analyzing the products. I think that with additional effort they can police the galvanizing process. 

Mr. ADAIR. Have these standards been circulated to your knowledge so that concerned people know about them?

Senator BAYH. In my estimation they probably have not been circulated to the industry. I learned of them last evening. We will check this out. Congressman Adair, we would not require such a thick base plate as is required in this country for normal use. 

Mr. ADAIR. Of the galvanizing material? 

Senator BAYH. Yes, sir. It is important for us to understand that conditions in the field in South Vietnam are considerably more difficult as far as the corrosive effect on galvanized plate is concerned than they would be on the north 40 here in the United States.

Mr. ADAIR. To come back to this matter of specifications again, if I understand you correctly it is your present feeling that these are now more in the nature of guidelines than in the nature of absolute requirements in Vietnam? 

Senator BAYH. That is my understanding, although the gentleman from AID can speak more directly to that. 

Mr. ADAIR. We will no doubt make inquiry on that.

Chairman MORGAN. Any further questions? 

Mr. MCDOWELL. Mr. Chairman, for the record, my State doesn't produce any steel, but the Du Pont Co. produces some pretty good paint. Regardless of how we are successful in increasing the quality of this galvanized metal that is going to be used over there in Vietnam, I think we might introduce them to the use of a little paint on it in time. 

Senator BAYH. May I say, Mr. McDowell, it might be in the best interest of Du Pont and others in the paint industry to vote against my amendment. Perhaps paint could be used to prevent rusting of the steel. 

Mr. MCDOWELL. Do you-I am sure you know, Senator, from your experience as you have stated you don't put the paint on until it begins to rust anyway.

Chairman MORGAN. Any further questions? 

Thank you, Senator Bayh, for your appearance today. 

Senator BAYH. Mr. Chairman, you have been more than kind, as busy as you are.

Chairman MORGAN. We had Mr. Gaud and Mr. Poats scheduled for this morning, but we don't have time and we are going to reschedule them for Tuesday morning so that we can hear the executive side of this question. 

The committee stands adjourned until 10:30 Tuesday morning. (Whereupon, at 12:30 p.m., the committee adjourned, to reconvene at 10:30 a.m., Tuesday, May 17, 1966.)

TUESDAY, MAY 17, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C. 

The committee met at 10:19 a.m. in room 2172, Rayburn Building, the Honorable Clement J. Zablocki presiding. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The committee will please come to order. We meet this morning in continuation of the hearings on H.R. 12449 and H.R. 

12450. We are indeed privileged to have Honorable Edward J. 

Gurney, distinguished Member of Congress from Florida. Mr. Gurney, I understand you have a prepared statement.

Mr. GURNEY. Yes, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. If you will proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF HON. EDWARD J. GURNEY, A REPRESENTATIVE IN CONGRESS FROM THE STATE OF FLORIDA 

Mr. GURNEY. Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I appreciate this opportunity to appear and testify on the subject of the 

U.S. foreign aid program.

In the consideration of our foreign air program this year- 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Will you try one of the other mikes? That doesn't seem to be operating. 

Mr. GURNEY. I would like to ask the committee to consider an amendment to the legislation which would cut off aid from the United States to Communist countries through the United Nations. Americans were startled and shocked earlier this year when they learned that $1.2 million of their tax dollars were being spent by the United 

Nations to help the University of Havana train young Communists and Cuban regulars in electronics, radar, and military communications. We know this because $3.1 million, of which we contribute 40 percent, an amount equal to $1.2 million, was given from the United 

Nations Special Fund to the University of Havana. One half of this gift was earmarked for the technological branch.

The university's technological branch is headed by Russian and 

Cuban military personnel. Only Communist Party and military members are permitted to attend. Cuban technology is specifically oriented toward training in computers, electronics, and other areas which Mr. Castro feels necessary to strengthen Cuba militarily, especially radar and military communications.

As soon as I heard about this, I introduced H.R. 12835 on February 

16, 1966, to stop this gift and prevent further moves like it. 997

In the area of foreign affairs, there has not been a single issue that has provided greater controversy in my own office than this indirect use of $1.2 million of the taxpayers dollars to promote communism. 

I have received hundreds of letters indicating to me that Americans are extremely and justifiably indignant to see their tax money going to aid and abet forces which seek to destroy us. 

For the past several years now, the United States has contributed over 40 percent of the cost of maintaining the U.N. Special Fund. 

The purpose of this program is to help less developed countries in the area of economic and social development. 

I cannot believe that any of us are so short of memory as to have forgotten the meeting earlier this year in Havana of representatives of 82 Communist Parties in three continents. Their purpose was to organize a more unified effort to subvert Asia, Africa, and the Americas.

And yet our money is being spent to train young men to assist Castro's Cuba in this conspiracy against America and every other free nation of the world.

My bill, H.R. 12835, would write into the language of the foreign aid law that the United States shall make no loan, payment or contribution, voluntary or assessed, to the United Nations or any agency or program thereof to furnish financial or other assistance to any Communist country. Now the committee well knows that we already have such language written into the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961 prohibiting assistance to Communist countries. Now it has become apparent that we must further spell out our meaning and intent to leave no room for mistake, clearly stating that that law cannot be bypassed by giving money through the United Nations. 

As I have mentioned, I have received hundreds of letters from across the Nation concerning this bill. They have been unanimous in approval and in surprise that such a provision did not exist already within the law. 

Mr. Chairman, I urge the committee to adopt the language of my bill, or an amendment of similar intent as a part of the Foreign Assistance Act of this year, insofar as moneys channeled through the United Nations are concerned, in order to prevent American tax dollars from assisting Communist nations. 

Thank you. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you. 

May I commend you for your interest. I am in sympathy with what you desire to do. As I understand H.R. 12835, if any part of the

U.S. contribution to the Special United Nations Fund were used for grant or loan purposes to a Communist country, our participation in the Special Fund would have to stop immediately?

Mr. GURNEY. Well, let's put it this way, Mr. Chairman. The purpose of the bill, and I would hope the language of an amendment which would go into this year's foreign assistance bill, would provide the means of setting up a procedure whereby our moneys going into the United Nations Special Fund could not be used for purposes like this gift to Cuba, which we made earlier this year. I don't pretend to set out what the mechanics of the law would be, but I certainly think that the staff of our Foreign Affairs Committee would be able

to work out the mechanics in order to prevent the use of this money for these purposes.

To put it another way, the purpose of my bill, and I would hope an amendment to the act, would be to set up procedures in advance which would prevent the use of our foreign aid moneys to go to the assistance of Communist countries, and specifically gifts like this one to Cuba earlier in the year. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Yes, I understand. You maintain that the $1.2 million of the $3.1 million that was given by the United Nations 

Special Fund to the University of Havana was part of our contribution since the United States contributes 40 percent of the total budget? 

Mr. GURNEY. That is right.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The argument to the contrary says that it is very difficult to ascertain what, if any, portion of our contribution to the United Nations Special Fund is actually used for Communist countries. Now 40 percent of $3.12 million would be approximately $1.2 million. Therefore, you maintain that actually 40 percent of this amount would represent a U.S. contribution for the University of Havana? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, that is right. Of course, I realize the counter argument that others who might be opposed to this procedure would make, that you can't determine actually whether U.S. dollars went to Cuba and the University of Havana, but I still maintain that if we are contributing 40 percent of this particular fund and the fund is being used to assist Communist countries, then obviously our money is being used to help out in this matter. 

Again I say that I don't pretend to suggest how the mechanics can be worked out, but I am sure we have ingenius enough minds to figure a way to prevent this indirect use of our tax dollars if we want to put a stop to this. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. I am sure that you know that the Communist countries contribute more to the Special Fund than they receive from it, and, therefore, it is contended by some the moneys from the United Nations Special Fund that does go to Communist countries is not actually part of our contribution? Then a following question: Is it better to stop all Communist participation in these types of programs? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, I certainly think, Mr. Chairman, that that would be a part of the answer and solution. Put it this way: Looking at the broad overall purposes of our foreign assistance program and, of course, we spend many billions of dollars each year in this program, we all know that we have spent considerably in excess of $100 billion since the end of World War II, the main purpose of the program, the underlying basis of it, actually, is to fight Communist aggression throughout the world, to strengthen free nations, those that we feel are friendly to us and on our side, certainly have future potential in this direction, to strengthen these nations, to prevent them perhaps, from being subject to Communist subversion and takeover. 

It seems to me it is inconsistent and grievously ridiculous if we enter into a foreign aid program through the United Nations, part of which is directed to strengthen Communist countries that indeed have as their goal the subversion and destruction of our own Nation and of other nations. So I simply say that whatever the mechanics are in 60-929-66-pt. 6-8

working this out, we should go about it in order to prevent the use of our tax dollars to directly work in opposition to our basic foreign aid program.

Now perhaps it would be the drastic thing we must do stop the contributions to the Special Fund altogether-if this is necessary in order to prevent this sort of thing happening. I don't really think the argument is very persuasive that Communist countries are contributing to the Fund also, because I think, unfortunately, the way the propaganda business works in the world and part of this whole business in the cold war fight against communism and against the free world on this side is propaganda, I think the Communists manage to get a good deal of propaganda out of any moneys that they might funnel into free world nations, direct or through the United Nations. 

Contrariwise, I don't think the United States gets much advantage or benefit out of any funds that may go into a place like Cuba. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. But you are not opposed to our participation in the 

United Nations Special Fund, per se, are you? 

Mr. GURNEY. Not at all. As long as we are able to direct these funds in the way that we, all of us I know, feel that we want them to be used. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you very much. 

Mr. Adair.

Mr. ADAIR. No questions. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Fraser. 

Mr. FRASER. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

I want to congratulate you for appearing before the Committee this morning. When Members will take the time to present their views on a matter of this kind it is helpful to the committee and I think it is a good practice that all of us look on with favor. I wish more Members would take the time as you have this morning.

I think the questions that the Chairman has asked do pose the problem that confronts us as a practical matter. If we are going to be part of an organization which includes Communist countries, we are faced with the prospect that, from time to time, decisions taken by majority vote which we may individually oppose might grant assistance to one of these countries. 

You have pointed out one example. I think the Children's Fund might be another. There might be help, under the Children's Fund, to the children in a Communist country. There might be a program under the World Health Organization, for example, to combat malaria that might involve a Communist country.

We are confronted with these decisions which are made by the body and which we may or may not be in sympathy with. We have really two values here at stake, one is our desire not to see in any way the Communist regime strengthened, but the other is our interest in continuing to be an active participant in an active world organization which has some positive values for us in the world. 

I think your suggestion that the staff is ingenious enough to devise the proper amendment certainly shows respect for our staff, but it is a very knotty problem that you have presented.

Just what are the mechanics so that we can continue to preserve the things that are important about international cooperation and

yet work out the kind of problem that you are calling attention to here? Do you have any further thoughts on how that could be done?

Mr. GURNEY. Well, I don't know that I have any further thoughts on the exact procedures. I can think of a very obvious one, that certainly some committee in the United Nations sits down each year and decides how they are going to spend this money, there is no question about that. Obviously they pick specific projects to direct this money to and certainly I think as far as the United States is concerned, it could just say "No," as simple as that, to anything that is as obviously wrong in the use of this particular fund as this money that went to Cuba and the University of Havana. 

Now I realize as a practical person that there are many gray areas where your argument obtains. But I think that there are also many areas where it would be entirely obvious not only to the United States and its friends in the United Nations, but the Communists, also, that the use of funds like going to the University of Havana, and further use in the technological branch of the university to train people in military technology and expertise, is not the right way to use this money.

Now the Communists might put up a big squawk if we said, "No," but they are practical people, too, and I think they would realize that after they aired their squawks, our position would prevail in this particular case.

So I don't really think that this is a matter of impossibility of handling and working out if we are determined to do it. 

Mr. FRASER. I recognize that our representative could vote "No" and could make the arguments that you are making and I think with some force. I don't think there is any question but what we ought to be concerned about the prospect of strengthening the system which is not only Communist but which is embarking on an attempt to export its activities. But if we make a commitment, for example, at the beginning of the year for our 40 percent share and then along the way a vote is taken on an individual project, and our position does not prevail, then we are confronted with the question of what do we do? Do we then say we are going to walk out next year? It is a problem and I recognize that there is no simple answer and that is what you are suggesting, too. Let 

I am just saying that I think the difficulty is rather profound. me just ask, if I may, on something like malaria control or the children's program, would your views be the same, or would your feeling be there should be more latitude there? 

Mr. GURNEY. No, I certainly think there ought to be more latitude in something like that, that isn't directly associated with the strengthening of Communist forces or training people to subvert the free world countries as this gift to Cuba was used. 

Mr. FRASER. So there would have to be some modification under those circumstances, I assume? As I read it here it would be blanket provision, no matter what the program. 

Mr. GURNEY. Let me say this, that I doubt if any bill or amendment or law introduced by a Member of Congress isn't usually revised considerably before it finds way into law, sometimes beyond recognition as we all know.

Mr. FRASER. Let me reiterate my appreciation. I think this makes a constructive contribution. 

Mr. GURNEY. Thank you.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you. Mr. Gurney, I would like to thank you for appearing today, and ask what you are trying to accomplish by your proposed amendment. Would it be enough if the 

United States received assurance that no money which it provided to an international organization were used to aid any Communist country? Is it your proposal that if that assurance were given, that we would be willing to pay the same dollar amount of money to the international organization? Should we seek to have our assessment made available only to the non-Communist countries? Is it simply a question of readjusting the program so we would finance perhaps 50 percent instead of 40 percent of some program to a Western country, but nothing would be actually earmarked in the way of dollars for a 

Communist country?

In other words, is it simply a question of juggling the books that you are advocating? Or is it a question of simply turning our back on the United Nations if they ever give any aid to a Communist country. 

The latter course, I think you can realize, is an impossible condition to impose on an international agency, even though we do contribute very substantial amounts. 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, no, Mr. Frelinghuysen, I don't think it is really either, turning your back on the United Nations or juggling books, as you put it. I really think it is a matter of hard business practical sense to get across the message to the United Nations and nations that participate in it, that we won't stand for our money being used for projects like this particular one to Cuba, and again I say that while there may be some difficulty in working out the procedures, that is true of life everywhere.

One thing I think that is apparent to us all is that the Communists understand good, hard practical talk and I think if we make our position clear that we will not tolerate the use of this fund for projects like this, we can get that message across. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I don't think I understand your "good, hard practical talk." Are you suggesting that we would be satisfied if our money went only to aid non-Communist countries, and that Communist contributions should make up the short fall that would develop in aid to Communist countries?

In other words, is it a question of simply receiving some assurance that no money would be earmarked, no U.S. dollars would be earmarked, for aid in any form to a Communist country, but that we would allow more than 40 percent in American dollars to go to aid to non-Communist countries? I call that juggling the books. Perhaps this would not satisfy you, but it would be a change in the present procedure. It would seem to overcome the objection which you now have to aid being distributed both to Communist and non-Communist countries. 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, I am not sure that I understand exactly what your proposal would be. Is it your thought that there would be two

Special Funds set up within the United Nations, call one a free world fund and one a Communist fund?

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. We would take what I would assume would be a peculiar position. We would be the biggest contributor, but we also would put strings on our aid and say that any money we give to this Fund can only be used to aid non-Communist countries. If we continued to give the same dollar amount as we have been giving, we would not create any short fall except with respect to Communist countries. However, we would be taking up the tab in the event that the Communist did not supply aid to Western countries. Presumably they would be supplying aid to the Communist countries to make up for what we are not supplying.

Mr. GURNEY. Well, it seems to me that the problem is a little broader than that. It probably has to do with the projects themselves. Let me pose this as a question and perhaps put the shoe on the other foot. Would it be the sense of the Foreign Affairs Committee to continue to support the United Nations Special Fund if this Special Fund is being used for projects like Cuba and the University of Havana, where you have an obvious use of the money to build up the resources of a Communist nation in order to continue its subversive type of work and activities throughout the world? 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Well, if you are saying, "Should we repudiate the U.N. and refuse to aid it if they ever support a Communist country?" I should say the answer is obvious. Of course, we are not going to be able to dominate the United Nations to the extent that they will never pay any attention to the needs of Communist countries. Mr. Fraser pointed out the very simple example of malaria eradication. Suppose these were agricultural specialists, suppose your assistance was in that area, not for subversion or for this particular project in Cuba? I am not sure what the military implications are of this particular Cuban project. I am not saying that this is a good way for the U.N. to spend money. However, I think it is impossible for us to be arguing, yet maybe this is what you are arguing, that our financial veto should be so complete that the U.N. would be obliged only to give aid to those countries that we wanted to help. 

Surely you are not suggesting that we should do that? Should we refuse to support any program that gives any aid to any countries that we disapprove of? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, if I may repeat what I said before, the question

I posed was this: Is the position of this committee and this Congress that we use these moneys to aid a specific project like the Cuban project?

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Well, if it were- 

Mr. GURNEY. And the answer to my question you said-in answer to my question you said would I advocate cutting off the use of funds for something like malaria. If I may submit I don't think my question was answered.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. If it were within the power of this committee, I am certain we would not pay the Castro government in any way. 

Certainly I wouldn't want to see him aided. But it isn't within the power of this committee, it isn't within the power of Congress to prevent a United Nations agency from making some such decision which would be opposed by us as an individual country. 

Mr. GURNEY. Well-

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Are you saying if they take such an action, that we should cut off all aid for all programs, most of which we do approve of, in areas where the need is very great? Should we do this because in one instance they go against what we consider desirable? 

I am suggesting that that isn't really a realistic approach, unless you are willing to abandon the international organization because it flouts our will in particular instances.

Mr. GURNEY. Well, my suggestion is that as a practical matter I think it is possible to devise means of communicating to the United Nations that we will not participate in projects like the one in Cuba. 

It seems to me it is begging the question to say that this Fund is a fine fund and simply because it is misused we shall not participate in it, which is really, I think, the position the gentleman is making and I say that is not practical.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Well, if we adopted your amendment there would be no choice but to pull back altogether from this particular program. I don't know how much money is involved, it may involve a great deal, none of which could be used, because of their unwillingness to go along with our disapproval of this project. I am not trying to beg anyone's argument, but it seems to me you have begged my question regarding an earmarking of our aid just to the non-Communist countries even though this might require us to put up more than 40 percent, with the Communist countries taking care of projects in their own countries.

This might be an undesirable approach, but the net result might be what you want. It certainly would prevent us from supporting in any way, or being open to the charge that we were supporting in any way, a project in any Communist country, no matter how worthwhile the project was, even if it was not directed at a military end. 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, I would say to the gentleman that that probably would be at least some improvement over the present state of affairs. Again I say I have no great pride of authorship as far as the particular language of this amendment is concerned, and yet I am sure that the language of the amendment does suggest to the committee the problem and hopefully we might resolve it in some way. 

We certainly have resolved it in our own Foreign Assistance Act as far as our own direct aid to other nations is concerned. We have earmarked and actually cut off or prohibited aid to Communist countries, naming them, those that we think are unfriendly to us. And I certainly think that some similar kind of language can be devised and strings attached to our assistance to the United Nations Special Fund to accomplish the same purpose. 

This is what I am saying. I don't think that we should throw up our hands and simply say that because most of this money may be used for good and useful purposes throughout the world, therefore, we should go along with what we know in our own minds and hearts is a deliberate misuse of our moneys. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. You have pointed out a problem, but your resolution, Mr. Gurney, your proposed solution to this problem would, it seems to me, result in the complete termination of U.S. aid to very worthwhile United Nations programs. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman. I realize my time has elapsed.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Hamilton. 

Mr. HAMILTON. No questions. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Gross. 

Mr. GROSS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I join my colleagues in expressing appreciation for your appearance before the committee this morning, Mr. Gurney. I have been an interested spectator to the discourse that has taken place. 

Yes; I would support an amendment that cut off all U.S. contributions to the United Nations Special Fund. This points up, this discussion, what has been implicit in the United Nations since its origin, the fantasy of the organization. It points up what we can't do by way of control of our own money in the organization. would cut it all off every single dollar to the United Nations Special Fund, if it is going to be used to support a character such as 

Fidel Castro. 

The Inter-American Subcommittee holds hearings by the day and comes up with reports denouncing Castro and all his works in Cuba, and yet we turn around in a bill of this kind and help make it possible to continue Cuba as a spawning ground for subversives through out all of Central and South America and the Caribbean area. How incongruous can we get, we the Members of Congress?

There is no reason you could not specify Cuba in a resolution or amendment and provide that none of the funds contributed by this Government be allocated to Cuba. There is no reason why we should crawl or grovel to the United Nations or any other international organization. Cambodia told us to take our aid and go to hell, to get out of the country, and we got out. 

I suspect they have mosquitoes and malaria in Cambodia as well as in Indonesia. We get out of those countries when they tell us to get out, but we haven't the courage to get out when we know damage is being done to us. I don't understand this Government's operation at all. For my money we could get out of the United Nations altogether, and recognize it for the debating society that is. 

That is all I have to say unless the gentleman wants to comment.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Murphy. 

Mr. MURPHY. No questions. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Berry. 

Mr. BERRY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

I want to commend our colleague for the position he has taken here. 

I would simply ask this, Congressman Gurney: Can there ever be a solution to this problem worked out so long as communism has islands of communism within the free world? Unless we do as was suggested, I think, by Mr. Frelinghuysen, let the Communist money take care of this assistance back of the Communist wall, the Iron Curtain or the Bamboo Curtain, or whichever curtain it is, let their money take care of this kind of work back of that curtain, let our money take care of this kind of work outside of the Iron Curtain? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, as I replied to Mr. Frelinghuysen, I said I think that is a possible solution. I myself think that language could be devised specifically prohibiting the use of this money perhaps to a certain country.

What is wrong-what is wrong with our taking the position that there are countries that are known troublemakers in the world, every-

body knows they are, and Cuba certainly is a shining example, and simply denying the use of a special fund of this sort in the United Nations to a country that is bound and determined to make trouble in the world. I see nothing hard about that, difficult about it, or wrong with it.

As we have done in the Foreign Assistance Act in section 620, we have spelled it out in voluminous language, about not giving any assistance to Cuba. We know what our national policy is there, and 

I don't see why we can't spell out this same intent and language in the foreign assistance as far as the one before us is concerned, and I don't think it means you have to destroy the Special Fund and its use in proper areas or the United Nations, itself. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Well the gentleman yield? 

Mr. BERRY. Just a minute. I would like to ask the gentleman one more question. Do you think, Congressman Gurney, that we should be increasing trade or should we stop trade with the Communist countries so long as they are killing American boys? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, I feel very strongly about that; I don't think we should be increasing trade with Communist countries. My feeling there is that we are strengthening them and we are permitting them to wax stronger with our help to make further trouble in the world and that has been my position always.

Mr. BERRY. And do you feel as I do, that we should stop building up, helping to build up Communists so long as they are killing us? 

Mr. GURNEY. That is right. 

Mr. BERRY. I yield to the gentleman.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I thank the gentleman. I simply wanted the record to indicate that what you referred to as my suggestion about Communist countries aiding Communist countries, and non-Communist countries aiding non-Communist countries, was not a suggestion I was making. I was seeking a clarification of Mr. Gurney's position. 

I myself think this would be a retrogressive step. If we are willing to support the United Nations, we should not try to divide up responsibility, and qualify the way we should support it. Of course, we should try to influence the result, but it would be a mistake-for a variety of reasons-to try to use the U.N. to develop a Western aid program and a Communist aid program. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Hays. 

Mr. HAYS. Mr. Chairman, I am sorry I was late. 

Mr. Gurney, I have read your statement. The question occurs to me, how do you-I don't like this business of giving our money to Cuba, but as long as you are going to belong to the United Nations, in which a nation with a million inhabitants has as much vote as we do with approximately 200 million, how are you going to stop it? If they out vote you, what do you do? I don't think your proposal would be effective, because you either belong and subscribe to majority rule or you don't, one or the other. How do you do it? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, we had discussed that, Mr. Hays, previous to your appearing, and we have discussed just now one possible solution, that this is to earmark our funds, if that is the way of putting it, or simply state that they shall not be used in respect to certain countries. 

I don't think that is impossible. I realize there is the argument that if you are going to belong to a team, you have to play on the

team, but I think you can belong to a team and play under certain rules if you want to, and this is what I am suggesting, that as far as this Special Fund is concerned, we clearly set out some rules under which we will participate.

Mr. HAYS. Suppose we pay our assessment and they go ahead and give this anyway, what do you do then?

Mr. GURNEY. Well, I think that would be simply resolved by putting up the money and then allotting it for programs that are not in contravention to the Foreign Assistance Act, as to how our moneys will be used in the Special Fund. I don't see any difficulty there.

Mr. HAYS. You think maybe we ought to do the way the French and 

Russians do, whenever we don't like a program, just don't pay our assessment.

Mr. GURNEY. Well, I don't know that we have to make a statement that we do as the French and Russians do. It seems to me this is a simple statement that really doesn't have much bearing on what we are talking about here. I think we can set our own rules that further the broad purposes of this country which, as far as the United Nations are concerned, and the foreign assistance program is concerned, is to try to help the world and particularly to strengthen free nations so that they in turn will become independent and help us all make a better world. 

Now I don't see why we can't use our money to further those aims and when our money is being used in contravention of these purposes, then I think we should set out rules which will see that they are not so used. 

Mr. HAYS. Well, Mr. Gurney, let's not talk in circles. I don't think we are in a position to set out rules, and I don't see anything wrong with comparing not paying our assessment to the French and Russians. I didn't approve of them not paying at the particular time because I happen to approve of the operation, but I think there is an old adage around somewhere that real power is the power of the purse, and I don't see how you are going to make any rules for the United

Nations unless you just say, if you use this money in a way we don't like, we won't pay.

That seems to me the only way we can get at it. Can you succinctly tell me any other way to do it? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, you are saying the same thing. You are saying if we earmark a certain amount of money, and I think the sum is around $150 million, as I recall, which we contribute to the Special 

Fund, I don't know the mechanics of putting it up. Actually, if we put it up at the beginning of the year and give it to them, obviously they can spend it the way they want to. We can decide in this committee and in turn in the House and Senate that we are going to put up this money, and then I think we can point guidelines as to how it will not be used-and this Cuban incident I think is a good example-and if it is misused, then yes, halt the flow of the money for a particular project. 

What is wrong with that?

Mr. HAYS. Well, there isn't anything wrong with it as far as I am concerned, except you don't recall it after you have once given it to them, unless next year you deduct the amount that you think they mis-

used. I am just trying to find out a practical way of doing this and short of not paying, I don't see any.

Mr. GURNEY. Well- 

Mr. HAYS. I don't think you can put up $150 million, or any other part, and then dole it out on a weekly basis according to what you think they are doing right or wrong with it. I think you have to put up the money and if they have done wrong with it, it seems to me the only solution would be to not give them that amount next year. 

Mr. GURNEY. I don't think you have to do it on a weekly basis. I would imagine there are procedures where projects are approved in the United Nations, and I see no reason why if you run across a project which is in obvious contravention to the policy of this country, at that point you can say we are not going along with this.

Mr. HAYS. But if you have already paid in your money, it wouldn't do you much good. 

Mr. GURNEY. The point I made is you don't pay your money in until you find out what they are doing with it. 

Mr. HAYS. Well, I don't know, it seems to me you are making a pretty good case, from a political standpoint, but from a practical standpoint of how you pay or don't pay your dues to a lodge, I don't think you are making much of a case.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

I feel very grateful to you, Mr. Gurney, for bringing this whole matter up because it hits so many areas of our giving. What do we do when England continues to deal with Red China in Vietnam, what do we do in all instances of countries we have been helping? Aren't they in the same general category or are they, because they are not in the United Nations? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, if the gentlelady is asking my own opinion on this whole broad concept of foreign affairs, I would say that when we run into affairs like what England is doing, we try to devise means to persuade them not to. I don't know that I am prepared to suggest to the committee this morning all the specifics of how we persuade every nation in the world not to do the things that we don't want them to do. 

If the gentlelady is asking me whether I approve of the actions of

England in continuing to trade with our enemies, I certainly don't. 

Mrs. BOLTON. I agree with you heartily.

Mr. GURNEY. I could say this, and again I think we are getting into a field where we could argue all morning here, but I can think of one practical solution, where nations, supposedly our allies, are trading with a foreign country-that we are at war with like North Vietnam-I suppose one practical solution might be to deny the use of the U.S. ports to these allies or these shipping countries that are engaged in such trade. Now here is a practical thing that I think of at once. I am not sure whether it would work or not, but it is something that might be a solution, and I might point out that even some of our trade unions have come up with that idea. I remember a strike here, recently, a refusal to unload some ships a short time ago at one of our ports, where the union people, who apparently had a very strong belief in not aiding Communist countries or nations that were aiding

and abetting Communist countries, took it into their own hands to do something about it and frankly I admired their action. 

We as a government, I think, might devise means of effective persuasion along these lines.

Mrs. BOLTON. It would have been my understanding that when this money was given to Cuba from the Special Fund for this university, that we objected very strongly, and we were told by the Chairman that none of the money that we had given would go to the university. 

Do you know anything about that? 

Mr. GURNEY. Well, one, I know we objected strongly, yes, but I really think the fact that none of our money is going to the university is probably an exercise in semantics. The point of the matter is that we put up the major share of this particular United Nations Special Fund, and if it is being used for purposes of this sort, then certainly our money is being used indirectly, at least. 

Mrs. BOLTON. I do agree with you. I admire you for it. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Rosenthal?

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Fraser? 

Mr. FRASER. I just wanted to add one comment to follow up some earlier comments. It is true that the Soviet Union and France failed to pay assessments for something they didn't like in the U.N. You are familiar with that? 

Mr. GURNEY. Yes.

Mr. FRASER. If in effect we adopt their position, then it seems to me we are suggesting that others can object to actions they don't support. As a result of this concept, Greece would object to programs for Turkey and vice versa because of their dispute over Cyprus-India and Pakistan in the same vein-because to these countries those hostilities and those tensions are as great and significant as the ones that we see in the cold war confrontation. And I say this without attempting to downgrade the problem that you raise but I am just trying to point out what I think are the dangers, dangers which may be even greater than this amount of money which has gone to Havana, which isn't a great amount of money compared to what the Soviet Union is unilaterally supplying to Cuba. 

I think it is about a million dollars a day.
