Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Gaud? 

The committee will next hear the Honorable William S. Gaud, Deputy Administrator, AID. You have a prepared statement, Mr. Gaud?

STATEMENT OF HON. WILLIAM S. GAUD, DEPUTY ADMINISTRATOR, AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, I have, Mr. Chairman. 

Mrs. BOLTON. Will the gentleman yield, Mr. Chairman?

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mrs. Bolton? 

Mrs. BOLTON. I just want to say publicly that I regret very much that you have been scheduled a second time at the latter part of the morning. I would hope if we don't finish today, we give you a really better place to present things we do want from you and that we need very much. 

Mr. GAUD. Thank you, Mrs. Bolton. 

Mrs. BOLTON. You don't have to comment on that. 

Mr. GAUD. Shall I go ahead with my prepared statement, Mr.

Chairman ? 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. If you will, sir. 

Mr. GAUD. Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, as you know, over 90 percent of all the commodities financed by the Agency for International Development are now bought in the United States. 

This is in sharp contrast to the situation which existed in 1960, when only 49 percent were bought here.

The problem which is now before you arises out of the less than 10 percent of our commodity purchases which are made overseas. More specifically, it arises from our commercial import program in Vietnam, which accounts for the bulk of our offshore procurement. 

The commercial import program provides Vietnam with dollars to finance the importation of essential goods required by the Vietnamese in excess of their own foreign exchange capabilities. These imports absorb the greatly expanded market demand resulting from the war effort which, if not met, would create dangerous inflation. The imported goods are paid for by Vietnamese businessmen, and then sold in local currency.

Galvanized sheets are one of the principal items financed by the Vietnam commercial import program-approximately $20 million is the estimate for fiscal year 1966. 

If I may depart from my prepared statement for a moment, when

Senator Bayh was testifying here last Thursday, he estimated the business in galvanized sheets in Vietnam at $50 million for this coming year. Frankly, we can't fit that figure into the facts. We see licenses for $20 million for fiscal year 1966, rising perhaps as high as $25 million for calendar year 1966, and neither in terms of past business nor what we see to be the future business do we think it will grow to anything like as much as $50 million this year. 

These sheets are used by the Vietnamese primarily for roofing. Beginning in 1963, Korean galvanizers developed a substantial market for these sheets in Vietnam, using Japanese blackplate as a basic component. In June 1963, for balance-of-payments reasons, AID adopted a "componentry rule" restricting to 30 percent the material in finished products which could come from a source other than the supplier country or the United States. In January 1964, again for balance-of-payments reasons, AID adopted a 90/10 componentry rule which restricted to 10 percent the material which could come from a source other than the supplier country or the United States. 

This ruled out the use of Japanese blackplate. A Korean galvanizer who attempted to use U.S. blackplate found he could only sell his galvanized sheets at a loss.

In May 1965, the Koreans worked out a new procedure which they asserted complied with the AID componentry rule. Under this procedure, they bought U.S. iron and steel scrap and in effect bartered it to Japan to pay for most of the cost of Japanese blackplate which they then galvanized and sold to Vietnam. After extended discussions, we told the Koreans that this did not meet the 90/10 rule. 

We therefore ordered suspension of procurement of galvanized sheet from Korea on November 1, 1965.

This suspension came just at the time that the Korean Government sent its Tiger Division to fight in Vietnam. In February 1966, the 

Vietnamese Government asked for a second Korean division, plus a combat brigade. Shortly thereafter the Korean Government agreed to send these additional troops to Vietnam.

The Koreans have for several years been mounting an intense export drive, which is reducing their need for U.S. aid. They are naturally eager to maintain and expand their sales to Vietnam. 

We believe-and we acknowledged to the Korean Governmentthat it is reasonable for Korea, a developing country and an ally which will soon have 40,000 troops in Vietnam, to have an opportunity to compete for some of the U.S.-financed procurement supporting the war effort. Galvanized products represent at least threefourths of Korea's present total exports to Vietnam. This is business that Korea has had. Enforcement of AID's 90/10 componentry rule would have cut off this business at a most inopportune time. We therefore decided to adopt a new procedure for the procurement of galvanized sheets which would permit Korea to bid for the businessand if they got it-to be paid in dollars which could only be spent in the United States for iron and steel products or a limited amount of steel scrap. To assure competition, we offered the same opportunity to several other Far East countries as well as the U.S. industry.

When we announced this new procurement policy, Senator Bayh raised several objections. He cited information indicating that the quality of galvanized sheet from Far Eastern sources had been poor, and that prices paid were higher in some instances than appeared reasonable in the light of the suppliers' costs. He also urged us to reinstate the 90/10 componentry rule which would have prohibited the use of Japanese blackplate and instead to require the use of U.S. blackplate by Far Eastern galvanizers.

We undertook investigations on these three issues and other aspects of the problem. During these investigations and up to the present time we have not financed any galvanized sheets under the procedure challenged by Senator Bayh. In other words, the matter has been in suspension since November 1, 1965. Our investigation confirmed that many of the sheets had been of poor quality. This resulted primarily from inadequate specifications provided by Vietnamese importers. We now intend to deal with this deficiency by eliminating thinner gage sheets and by requiring zinc coating meeting U.S. commercial standards as defined by the American Society for Testing Materials. We will also require that an independent testing agent certify compliance with these standards at the source prior to shipment to Vietnam.

At the last hearing on Thursday, a number of the members of this committee asked Senator Bayh if he thought that we had dealt satisfactorily with this question of quality. This is obviously an important question. We are grateful to Senator Bayh for having raised it. 

We feel that we have gone 100 percent of the way to meet his objections, and I would like to call the committee's attention to the exhibit annexed to my statement. It is headed "Galvanized Sheet Procedure," following page 7 of my statement. If you look about one-third of the way down the first page there, you will see our proposal on quality. 

And it provides that all Vietnam commercial import requirements of galvanized iron and steel sheet shall comply to ASTM specificationsthat is the American Society for Testing Materials Specifications- 

A-525-64-T, under coating class designated as 1.25 ounces commercial, subject to inspection at source by an independent inspection company. 

In other words, we have issued, we did this a month ago, a very specific requirement that any further galvanized sheets which are imported into Vietnam must meet this specific requirement of the American Society for Testing Materials and before anything may be shipped into Vietnam, it must be tested and inspected by an independent inspection company. I don't see how we could be more precise, more specific. I respectfully submit that there just isn't any question in the case from here out about quality. Whether this comes from Korea, whether it comes from the United States or whether it comes from anywhere else, it all has to meet this very specific requirement which is an American requirement. 

With respect to the prices we found indications of possible collusion and kickbacks by suppliers to Vietnamese buyers. Our new rules will guard against this in several ways. We will require that procurement invitations and awards be managed by a single Vietnamese authority with official U.S. participation, so as to prevent contact between supplier and buyer. We also will set reasonable price ceilings above which awards will not be made.

With respect to source, we have considered three alternatives: requiring procurement only from the United States, or requiring the use of U.S. black plate, or requiring that procurement be split between 

United States and Far Eastern suppliers.

We concluded that a shift to total U.S. procurement would not only deny Korea the opportunity to bid but also would greatly increase the cost of galvanized sheets at a time when we are trying to check inflation in Vietnam. Our cost analysis indicated that if we insisted on the use of U.S. black plate, Korean suppliers could not deliver galvanized sheets to Saigon at prices competitive with U.S. galvanized sheets. Consequently, this alternative would exclude Korea from a chance to compete unless we barred U.S. galvanizers from competition. 

This we are not prepared to do.

The third alternative was to require that some part of the business be directed exclusively to the United States. We have decided to adopt this alternative, requiring that at least half of the galvanized sheet by value be bought from the United States. To the extent that Far 

Eastern countries obtain the other half of the business, they will be paid in dollars which can be spent only in the United States and only for iron and steel products including machinery, but excluding scrap. 

Thus, although U.S. industry will not be guaranteed all of the Vietnamese market for galvanized sheet, it will get at least half directly and will get indirectly an amount of business equal to the remainder through sales of other iron and steel products to the countries earning these tied AID dollars.

Attached to this statement is a detailed outline of our new procedure. In summary, it will insure that— (1) All AID dollars spent on galvanized sheets for Vietnam will remain in the United States and be spent on U.S. iron and steel products. (2) U.S. galvanizers will obtain 50 percent of the galvanized sheet business and will be eligible to compete for 100 percent of it. 

U.S. iron and steel and machinery manufacturers will ultimately receive business on the balance through tied letters of credit. 

(3) Eligible Far Eastern countries such as Korea, Philippines, 

Singapore, Taiwan, and Thailand will be able to compete for 50 percent of the business and use black plate from any free world source galvanized by them. These countries will be paid not in free dollars, but in a specially restricted tied letter of credit which can be used only for the purchase of U.S.-produced iron and steel products, including machine tools. (4) U.S. "commercial" grade quality standards will be required as a minimum from all sources, with independent inspection before shipment. (5) We will finance only U.S.-produced gages, up through 31. (6) We will insist on procurement on an aggregate basis, man aged by a Vietnamese Government authority with U.S. Govern ment participation in the award decisions. (7) AID will not finance purchases at prices above those it determines to be reasonable. (8) Existing offers made to Vietnamese importers will be rejected, and all new business will be conducted on the basis of our new procedure.

We believe the revised procurement arrangements are fair and meet our various objectives. We will keep this matter under constant review, and will adjust our procedures as may be necessary to protect U.S. interests. We do not believe that a highly complicated and technical administrative matter such as this need be dealt with by legislation. 

I will be glad to respond to your questions. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Gaud.

Your statement today reassures the committee that a new procurement formula covering galvanized sheets has been initiated because of the complaints and questions raised about past procedures. The question still remains, Why has not AID followed these procedures or plans or formulas in the first instance? Why should complaints have to be made before AID corrects its procedures?

Mr. GAUD. There are several points on that, Mr. Chairman. As far as the quality is concerned, we walked into a market where the Vietnamese were already buying galvanized sheet according to their own specifications from the Koreans and others, and we took the market as we found it. We did not feel that it was incumbent on usor that it was desirable to move in and insist upon American specifications. As time went on and when Senator Bayh called our attention to these questions of quality, we concluded that we had made a mistake and that we should change our standards.

As far as price is concerned, the same thing. When we looked further into this, we found that there was good basis for his complaint and we decided we should take action to rectify it. 

Now, as to why we didn't do this sooner, why we didn't discover it sooner, you raise a question there which is very pertinent and which applies to much of our Vietnamese program. It has increased, as this committee knows, enormously within the last 6 to 8 to 9 months. This is particularly true of the commercial import program. We have had a very difficult time recruiting people to go to Vietnam. We have increased our staff considerably in the last 6 months. We are still working on increasing it. We are spending a lot of time recruiting for it. And I am frank to say that we have not had in Vietnam in the past enough people to manage this program the way it ought to be managed. We are getting geared up and in the future, I think we will do a better job, I am confident we will do a better job on these things than we have done in the past.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. On page 2, you refer to the Koreans working out a procedure which we found was in violation of our 90/10 componentry rule. But what about the charge that the Koreans and the Chinese have violated the 70/30 and 90/10 import rule for several years?

Mr. GAUD. I don't think that that charge is substantiated by the facts, Mr. Chairman. We put the 70/30 componentry rule into effect in June 1963. Prior to that time, there was no such rule and the 

Koreans were selling the sheets to the Vietnamese perfectly legally and perfectly properly. Between July and December of 1963 for the first 6 months after the 70/30 rule was issued, no licenses were issued for AID-financed galvanized sheet from Korea or from Taiwan. In

January 1964, we adopted the 90/10 rule and similarly, except for deliveries under prior contracts, issued before the componentry rule went into effect, no licenses were issued under the 70/30 rule. Later in 1964, the Koreans attempted to get back into the market by using

U.S. black plate. They couldn't-they weren't competitive, they lost money. And there were no further Korean sales to Vietnam other than a few small deliveries using U.S. black plate until 1965. 

They then started under this procedure that I outlined in my statement, when the Koreans bought scrap from the United States, sent this to Japan, and in theory, it was used in making up the Japanese black plate which was then exported to Korea and made up into galvanized sheets for shipment to Vietnam. That procedure was followed for a number of months. We discovered it and we ruled that it did not meet the componentry rule. So the fact of the matter is that the violations of the componentry rule were over a limited period of time and did not extend over a period of several years. In the case of 

China, orders in violation of the 90/10 rule were placed, only from the fall of 1964 until March 1965, when we suspended them. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Further, I gather from your statement, you are saying that the Korean suppliers could not deliver galvanized sheets to Saigon at competitive prices using U.S. galvanized sheets; that the 

Koreans have tried before to galvanize U.S. black plate and to sell competitively. 

Mr. GAUD. That is correct. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. But they found it impossible.

Mr. GAUD. They were unable to compete. I have a statement that 

I would like to put in the record on that if I may, Mr. Chairman, which will show a price and cost comparison of galvanized sheets. I would like if I may not only to put that in the record but to distribute copies to the committee at the moment.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Yes.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Do you have the estimated volume of AID-financed galvanized sheets in tons that you might provide for the record? 

(The information follows:) QUANTITY OF GALVANIZED SHEET FOR VIETNAM 

AID's latest estimates are that the demand for galvanized sheet will lead to new licenses (i.e., Vietnamese Government-approved importer procurement orders) for galvanized sheet totalling $18-$20 million during fiscal year 1966 and $25 million during calendar year 1966. In metric tons, for fiscal year 1966 the total would be 60,000–75,000; for calendar year 1966, 85,000–90,000 metric tons. 

These are estimates only, obviously subject to change in a wartime situation. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mrs. Bolton. 

Mr. GAUD. Excuse me. May I say a word about this price sheet as soon as it gets distributed? 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Yes, Mr. Gaud, if you will, please.

Mr. GAUD. As the members of the committee probably know and as 

I have learned relatively recently, galvanized sheet is made in many different thicknesses or gages. Each gage or thickness varies in price. 

It has different characteristics and it requires different qualities of black plate. Gage 31 is the thinnest gage made in the United States.

It is a very popular gage in Vietnam. When you start talking about the prices, of galvanized sheets, you get into trouble right away unless you concentrate on a particular gage and all of your figures relate to that gage. Otherwise, you are going to be mixing apples and pears.

My figures here include and are limited to 31-gage galvanized sheets. 

Also, the Vietnamese have the metric system, not ours, so my tons here are metric tons. And they buy their sheets in sizes of 1 by 2 meters; this again, of course, affects the cost of making them. Šo as I say here at the top of this sheet, this price and cost comparison assumes metric tons of 31-gage galvanized sheet, 1 by 2 meters, all of them meeting the ASTM standard which we are now requiring. If you will look at this sheet, the column on the left shows the Korean price, delivered to Saigon, of galvanized sheets according to the latest quotations we have from Korean industry-$276.75. Now for the purposes of price comparison here, let's take out some items over which there can be no argument. 

First, freight from Korea to Saigon, $32.25, you come down to $244.50.

Black plate, it takes 85 percent of a metric ton of black plate to make a ton of galvanized sheet. So taking the cost of Japanese black plate, which is $139.50, the amount of black plate that would go into a ton of galvanized sheets made in Korea, would be $118.58. That is 85 percent of $139.50. You subtract that and you get $125.92. Zinc is used by the Koreans for galvanizing this black plate. We have subtracted the cost of the zinc based on market costs in Korea. You come down now to $60.33. They use certain other metals in galvanizing the sheets, costing $10.01. You end up with $50.32. This is for Korean sheets delieverd in Vietnam, made of Japanese black plate.

Now, if you look at the next 3 lines there, this $50.32 will cover direct and indirect plant labor, loading and unloading at dock, inland transportation, packing materials, fuel, electricity, water, depreciation, taxes, interest, selling cost, and general overhead and profit. I can't possibly estimate what those are individually. But we can take

out those items, freight, black plate, and materials cost, and see what you have left to cover these other items. 

Now, the second column begins with $317.75 which is the price given us just the other day by a large U.S. producer of galvanized sheets; that is the price he quotes for delivery to Vietnam. You go through the same calculation, taking out the freight, taking out the black plate cost, 85 percent of the cost of a ton of black plate in the United States, taking out the zinc cost and the cost of the other metals, and you have the U.S. producer left with $56.37 to cover these items that I referred to a moment ago.

Now, let's suppose that the Korean producer instead of using Japanese black plate had to use U.S. black plate and let's assume the price that he has to meet is $317.75, which is the price that we have for the 

U.S. producer in column 2. Take column 3, start with that price as the price that the Korean has to meet, subtract his shipping, which is the same as in the first column, you have $285.50. The black plate cost here will be 85 percent of the cost of U.S. black plate delivered in 

Korea which is much more expensive than Japanese black plate delivered in Korea. So, you take out $188.50, instead of $118.58. Then you substract the zinc cost, same amount as in the first column, the other materials at the same amount, and the Korean producer using U.S. black plate ends up with $21.40 to cover all these costs and his profit, whereas the American producer selling at that price will have $56.37 to cover those costs and his profit.

Now, Mr. Chairman, in addition to past experience, in 1964 and early 1965, that is the basis on which we conclude that a Korean producer using U.S. black plate will not be competitive with U.S. industry. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Gaud.

Mrs. Bolton.

Mr. GAUD. Thank you, ma'am, I'm very glad to hear it.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Hays. 

Mr. HAYS. Mr. Gaud, you present quite an impressive set of figures here and I would have to assume that they are correct, although I wonder if the statement that is before me in which the pages are numbered 1, 2, 3, 4, 7, 6, 5, and your addendum at the end is in the middle, is symptomatic of the malaise down at AID? 

Mr. GAUD. I apologize for that, sir.

Mr. HAYS. If the figures you submitted to us are no more accurate than your ability to arrange a statement in order, why then I don't know. This is the whole thing that bothers me about AID.

Mr. GAUD. I had better repeat again, very quickly, that we made a mistake.

Mr. HAYS. Two in one day; that is unusual. 

Mr. Gaud, the thing that bothers me, really, even more than the figures that you submit, or the validity of them—and I am going to phrase this question very carefully. I want to tell you beforehand that I am aware, after reading the press in the United States about the conspiracy indictments against certain large corporations, that there is a certain resiliency among businessmen as far as morals are concerned, certain elasticity I should say, maybe. But do you really think that any Vietnamese businessman is honest by American standards? 

Mr. HAYS. You do? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes; not all, necessarily, but certainly some. 

Mr. HAYS. What percentage would you like to say? Ten? 

Mr. GAUD. I couldn't possibly guess.

Mr. HAYS. I would say if even 10 percent of them, based on my observation out there, met American standards of business honesty, that would be a high average. Now, when you are dealing with people like that, how do you expect to avoid all kinds of graft and black market which AID products are up to their ears in?

Mr. GAUD. We don't expect to avoid it all, but we ought to do, and we are going to do, everything we can to limit it as much as possible. 

For example, here under our new procedure, there will be upset prices, there will be competition between Korean manufacturers, other foreign manufacturers, and U.S. manufacturers.

What we propose to do-it is outlined more fully in this outline of procedure here what we propose to do, essentially, is to cut the direct connection between the sellers of sheets in Korea, for example, and the purchasers in Saigon. As long as Mr. X is dealing with Mr. Y, you have a possibility of kickbacks and you have a possibility of collusion. 

If, on the other hand, we can set up a procedure in Saigon where there will be a central office, all of the demands and requests for galvanized sheets will be put in there and lumped; that central office will then advertise in the United States and in the Far East for bids, for the types of galvanized sheets which have been asked for. Those bids will come in and in these lump quantities, not related to the requirements of individual merchants, and the contracts will be awarded by that central office with the assistance of U.S. AID staff people. 

It will not be until after the contracts have been awarded, the prices have been determined and everything else, that a particular seller will then ship his goods to Vietnam and they will get into the hands of a particular purchaser.

Now, I don't mean to say for a moment that the system is foolproof, I don't mean to say for a moment that there aren't opportunities for trouble in any system that you set up. But that type of device should go a long way toward eliminating the trouble that we have had in this in the past. 

Mr. HAYS. Well, do you still use three or four prices for piasters out there? 

Mr. GAUD. Well, the Vietnamese Government, not the United States, the Vietnamese Government has several different exchange rates; that is correct.

Mr. HAYS. Well, how do you expect in the name of common sense to stop black marketing when you have got the possibility of juggling every single item among three or four price structures?

Mr. GAUD. Well, if we are talking about this particular situation here, every importer of galvanized sheet or of anything else pays exactly the same number of piasters per dollar for the stuff that he is importing. Every person in Vietnam, every businessman in Vietnam who sells imported articles, sells them under the existing price structure. 

Mr. HAYS. Well, how do you know that? At least when I was there, there were at least three prices for piasters. 

Mr. GAUD. I know.

Mr. HAYS. As a matter of fact, your own employees had a more favorable price than I was able to have as a Member of the U.S. 

Congress and I am positive that the fellow who arranged for the piaster pocketed the difference. I do not think there is any question about it. 

Mr. GAUD. If you are- 

Mr. HAYS. And he was an American. 

Mr. GAUD. When you are talking about the number of piasters you pay for a dollar, or the number of piasters you buy for the dollar, as

I said at the outset, there are a number of different rates that apply to different transactions. We are talking here about importing goods and every importer pays the same number of piasters and then sells in the local market, not for dollars. I am not saying there isn't a black market for dollars in Vietnam; of course there is. It is something we would like to stamp out or reduce as much as we can. But the black market pays more piasters than the importer pays when he gets goods for his dollars. These are two unrelated problems. 

Mr. HAYS. But you haven't told me why we have three different prices.

Mr. GAUD. The Vietnamese Government has three different prices. 

Mr. HAYS. What government? 

Mr. GAUD. The Vietnamese. 

Mr. HAYS. I say again, What government? 

Mr. GAUD. Vietnam like many others, has different legal rates of exchange, one for imports, one for invisibles and so on. There is a black market rate. They give a special rate to our people who are in 

Vietnam, working there permanently, there are several different exchange rates in that country as there are in many other countries, Mr. Hays.

Mr. HAYS. It seems to me, to quote a proverb, "The fellow who calls the tune pays the piper" and we are paying the piper out there and it seems to me it might be reasonable that we call the tune and insist that there be one rate and if the Vietnamese Government, quote, unquote, doesn't like it, well, there are a lot of places they could go and jump and there wouldn't be very much they could do about it, would there?

The trouble is you have a man out there who I wouldn't--and I am saying this publicly-in charge of your AID program that I wouldn't put in charge of the men who clean out my horse barn. 

I think that is part of your problem.

Mr. GAUD. That is right.

Mr. GAUD. That is right.

Mr. GAUD. If he were using U.S. black plate.

Mr. ADAIR. Yes. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes. 

Mr. ADAIR. Now, do you have any idea about the comparative costs of labor in Korea and in the United States? That might be helpful to us. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes. Well, I can't be specific. Korean labor is obviously much less expensive than U.S. labor. On the other hand, there is very little U.S. labor used, as I understand it, in a modern galvanized sheet mill in the United States. It is pretty much of a pushbutton operation. Whereas in Korea they use a great many laborers, they have a lot of people doing much less work over there. I can't give you an estimate. As a matter of fact, I want to say and I am sure it is clear already without my saying it, that these figures are our best estimates.

Mr. ADAIR. I understand that. 

Mr. GAUD. Manifestly we can't get cost figures from producers and these are our best estimates and that is all they are. 

Mr. ADAIR. I also understand that.. I have a set of figures here before me that are not identical, but similar to those that you have compiled. But the point that I do want to make is that these two figures that I have mentioned just now would have to be construed in the light of different costs of labor. 

Mr. GAUD. Absolutely, absolutely.

Mr. ADAIR. Now, before 1963, where did Vietnam purchase most of this product?

Mr. GAUD. Mostly from Japan. 

Mr. ADAIR. Directly from Japan? 

Mr. GAUD. Certainly from the Far East. They got most of it from the Far East, particularly Japan.

Mr. ADAIR. Why then the sudden change in 1962 or 1963 to procurement from Korea? Was that at our instance, did we stimulate that?

Mr. GAUD. It is primarily due to the fact that that is when the 

Koreans got into the business. 

Mr. ADAIR. I wonder why they didn't continue to procure from their previous sources. I have some figures here that I believe to be reasonably accurate, that in 1962 the price of this type of material, galvanized material in Vietnam was about $170 for the unit you describe. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes.

Mr. ADAIR. And now within a couple of years or three, it has risen to a price $100 higher than that. My point is: If they were buying at roughly $170 in 1962 or 1963, why don't they continue to procure in 

1966, at that same price?

Mr. GAUD. Well, they could procure today at a price not too far above that, if they bought from Japan. Japanese galvanized sheets can be bought today for about $190 to $200 a ton as compared to these far higher figures that we have here. 

Mr. ADAIR. At the quality you mentioned?

Mr. GAUD. Same quality as American quality. Now, as far as the increase in the Korean price is concerned, I haven't got a satisfactory answer to that question. We are still investigating this question of price and so is the GAO. We do know, however, that between those two periods that you speak of (a) there was a very considerable amount of rising costs in Korea, (b) there was a change in the Korean exchange rate, and abandonment of the import-export "link" system, (c) there was the elimination of Korean export subsidiaries which formerly made it possible for Korean exporters to export more cheaply. Korea was trying to move into the market. So that there are a number of factors which- 

Mr. ADAIR. Then-excuse me.

Mr. GAUD. There are a number of factors which go into this price rise of Korean sheets. I don't know the whole answer. We are trying to get a better answer than we have.

Mr. ADAIR. The question that persists in my mind is that if they could purchase this material from Japan at say $170, why we don't do it now? Is this a matter of policy?

Mr. ADAIR. That we are trying to help Korea?

Mr. GAUD. No. 

Mr. ADAIR. And pay this premium price?

Mr. GAUD. Forgive me, I misunderstood the question. The basic reason for the shift is our own balance of payments. In other words, prior to 1963, to the extent that we financed galvanized sheet moving into Vietnam, we bought it where we could buy it the cheapest and that was mainly Japan. Then we moved into this business of a 70-30 rule and then a 90-10 rule because of the balance of payments. 

If we weren't worried about our balance of payments we could still be importing or paying for the importation of galvanized sheets into Vietnam at $190 today. But it is the balance-of-payments problem that has intervened.

Mr. ADAIR. I obviously don't have any studies on the effect upon the balance of payments, but I think it would be worth giving thought to at the vastly lower price, whether we might still not affect our balance of payments too adversely.

I have far more questions than the chairman is going to let me ask here. One point: There has been something said and I think possibly it came up when Senator Bayh was testifying the other day, about the attitude of the American Iron & Steel Institute with respect to these purchases. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir. 

Mr. ADAIR. I am not going to ask you what you think that to be. 

I think at one time AID said that the American Steel & Iron Institute

had no particular objection to the procedure which you are proposing. 

I have here, Mr. Chairman, and I would like to read into the record a copy of a letter dated April 21, 1966, signed by John P. Roache, president of the American Iron & Steel Institute. It is addressed to 

Senator Bayh. It says: 

MY DEAR SENATOR: I thank you for the time you and your staff gave me and representatives of the steel industry in your office on Tuesday, April 19th. I wish to state that the American Iron & Steel Institute is opposed to the Agency for International Development's ruling of January 31, 1966, covering galvanized iron and steel products for Vietnam. In our judgment the interests of the domestic steel industry and the nation will be best served by a reinstatement of AID's ratio of 90-10 as issued in January of 1964. The steel industry is very appreciative of your efforts in this regard. This support comes not only from the institute, but from our corporate members as well, many of whom will be contacting you directly in the near future.

Mr. Chairman, it is further my understanding, and I have nothing in writing on this, that that statement also reflects the principle opinion of the steel workers organizations. 

Do you have any comment on those things, Mr. Gaud? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir. In, I think it was January, when we were about to adopt the policy to which Senator Bayh objected, and which we are now proposing to change, we had some discussions with representatives of the American Iron & Steel Institute. It was our understanding on the basis of those discussions that they did not object to the procedure which we proposed to put into effect at that time. 

We said that. We discovered very quickly that we were mistaken and the American Iron & Steel Institute came out and opposed that particular procedure. The procedure which I have outlined to you this morning is also opposed as you have indicated by the American 

Iron & Steel Institute. They have made that quite clear to us and we don't claim their support. Their position is that 100 percent of this business should go to the American steel industry. 

As far as the Steel Workers are concerned, we have had a number of discussions with them over the past few months. My latest information is that they are not taking a position on this at the present time. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. I have very informal information to the contrary, but I am sure we will both obtain more definite information at a later time.

Mr. GAUD. We have talked to various representatives of the union. 

Some of them take one position, some take another, at least as individuals. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The gentleman's time is up. Mr. Farbstein?

Mr. FARBSTEIN. This is so complicated a situation that I can't seem to get this straight in my mind at all. Why it is that when you have a war going, problems of this type can't be straightened out without complaints and amendments to laws. There is something a bit far more important, it appears, that confronts not alone the national security but also American industry, American labor. I take it from what you say that you have made an effort to straighten this out with the American steel industry and also with labor and yet from what you say you have reached an impasse. Now, do you have any suggestions whereby this thing can be straightened out or must you make

the appeal to us the same way that Senator Bayh made an appeal to us? And I know, I, for one, don't know enough about this to be able to judge. There are so many complexities involved. Now, how do you see this in view of what I have said?

Mr. GAUD. Well, as I see it, Mr. Farbstein, there are three issues involved here, three issues that were raised by Senator Bayh. One is the question of quality. I think we have met him 100 percent on quality. The second is the question of price. He feels that our procedures were not tight enough to eliminate the chances of price gouging and of collusion. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Let me interrupt you on that. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Take each one separately. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

Mr. FARBSTEIN. What has been the practice-we have been in several wars, even in my lifetime, very likely in your lifetime alsoprobably two wars. We have had problems of this type before. 

Have you ever researched the experience that our Government has had in dealing with subjects of this nature? We have had war production boards, we have had various other organizations who have sat as overseers in disputes of this type. What has been the outcome of those disputes? And if you haven't, may I suggest that you do some research in this connection. 

Mr. GAUD. Well, I haven't 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. So that we can possibly satisfy all the competing forces here. 

Mr. GAUD. Well, I haven't researched it. I don't know that we have ever had a situation exactly like this. Essentially we have the same problem every time we get into a war-of a very fast buildup. 

The procedures for checking and the like are slower in developing than the buildup itself, and you are always a little bit behind in trying to get as much prudence, as much judgment, as much deliberation into your procedures as are required. As I said earlier, this program grew very fast. It grew faster than our ability to hire and send out to 

Vietnam enough auditors, enough checkers, enough accountants and so forth and so on. We are gradually catching up. But as you say it is a recurring problem. 

But if I may get back, sir, to the three issues here: (1) Qualitywe think we have met that; (2) price; we think we have met that with the procedure that I outlined when I was responding to Mr. Hays a little while ago. 

There is only one issue left in this case, and that is: Is all of the business to go to the U.S. industry or isn't it? Now, U.S. industry basically has only one factor to consider. They would like to have the business, and I don't blame them. And the same thing is true for the Steel Workers: they would like to have the business. 

We, on the other hand, have several things to keep in mind. One, we want at least some of it to go to U.S. business, no question about that. We also have to worry about the price of galvanized sheets in 

Vietnam, because of the inflationary situation there. If we give all the business to U.S. industry, those prices will go a good deal higher than they are now. Third, we have to consider the fact that the 60-929-66- -10

Koreans had this business and should we take away from them at this particular time when we are trying—and I am talking now of the AID program and U.S. policy-when we are trying to help Korea build an export trade. It doesn't seem to us that we should. 

Now, what is the solution? The answer, as we see it, and the answer that we have come up here with, is that we will award some of the business to U.S. industry. We will go ahead and encourage the 

Koreans to bid, give them an opportunity to bid we are not going to award them any business out of hand; they will have an opportunity to bid for the balance.

If, however, they are awarded contracts, we will require that the money, the dollars which they earn with those contracts are spent, not only in the United States, but for iron and steel products to be exported to Korea. Now, the significance of that is that in 1965, in the first 10 months of 1965, Korea bought 79 percent of her iron and steel products from Japan and 16 percent from Western Europe. She brought only $1.3 million worth of iron and steel products from the 

United States.

Now, if Korea should get 50 percent of this business, which I have estimated at something like $20 million for the year, if she should get $10 million worth of this business, she will be paid in special letters of credit which she must spend in the United States for iron and steel products. And that will mean that instead of Korea buying only $1.3 million of iron and steel products from the United States, she will buy $10 million worth of iron and steel products from the United States. 

So that we do give additional business to U.S. industry and we do at the same time enable Korea to stay in the business. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Well, this proposal, I presume, has been presented to the other side; has it not? 

Mr. GAUD. Well, we presented this proposal to Senator Bayh. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. And he wouldn't buy it?

Mr. GAUD. He would not buy it. We think it is a fair proposal. 

We don't see how we can go any further. This is it, as far as we are concerned. We don't see how we can fairly go any further and meet the various objectives that we have to consider. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Well now, the basis for his objection, evidently, doesn't appear tenable to you? 

Mr. GAUD. No, sir; they do not. His objection, I think I can say it accurately, is that he believes that the United States should get all the business. 

Mr. FARBSTEIN. Thank you very much.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Frelinghuysen. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Gaud, I have listened with interest to your testimony. I would like to begin by saying I think the proposed tightening of procedures is commendable, and also the necessity from now on of emphasizing quality. I don't think anyone can argue that these are steps in the right direction.

You have just said that the basic remaining issue is whether or not all the business should go to the United States. I would like to pursue this. I ask these questions out of ignorance, primarily. 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. It seems to me, if you have many restrictions on the money to be made available to these other countries and that if we require that aid must be tied to purchases in the United States, that we might be somewhat more liberal in providing them with an opportunity for competition.

In other words, in spite of what has been said, I am not sure why we should set aside 50 percent of this market for U.S. domestic industry. I realize I am stepping on toes by saying this. But the dollar amount is very small, microscopic in proportion to the billions that we are spending in Vietnam. I don't see how any major domestic interests are being invaded by allowing foreign, friendly countries to compete with us for relatively small amounts of business. In other words, it would seem, on the face of it, quite an arbitrary position for us to have that much of a tightening up. 

Would you care to expand on why we feel this necessity to insure 

U.S. industry against competition? Quite obviously I suppose there would be no market for U.S. galvanized sheet unless they had this kind of assurance. That will be one of your answers.

Mr. GAUD. Well, as I said a while ago, different gages cost different amounts. The United States is much more nearly competitive with the Korean and other Far East manufacturers in the heavier gages. The difference is not as great as it is in, for example, the 31 gage. Our assumption is, and this is borne out by past experience, that a fair amount of the galvanized sheet which will be ordered by the Vietnamese importers will be of fairly heavy gage, and the price differential between the United States and the foreign producer not being very large there, it didn't seem unreasonable to us to provide that, say, 50 percent of the total business should go to the United States.

Now, I will be perfectly frank to say, Mr. Frelinghuysen, as I have already made clear in my statement, that was not our original proposition. In January our intention was to leave the whole thing open to competition. But this is the American taxpayer's money, this is the 

American aid program and the objection to our course seems substantial to us and we have gone as far as we felt we could go to meet them. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I don't see how the U.S. interest is benefiting by this. As you pointed out we are financing this whole thing in any event. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. So, it is out of one pocket into another. What we are doing is using aid money to protect, in a very small area, a part of the domestic steel industry. 

Mr. GAUD. This is right.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. And as you say, in other areas they should presumably be more directly competitive anyway. I would think this would be an argument against providing an automatic protection against competition in this one particular area.

In other words, I would think that the additional $10 million might well, as a matter of policy, be divided amongst our Far Eastern friends. 

Granted, they are providing us with a small amount of direct competition which we probably will not be able to meet. 

Mr. GAUD. Well, of course, this is only one of many manifestations of the effect on the AID program of the balance-of-payments problem.

There are many things that we are buying in the United States today under the AID program for a good deal more than we could buy them overseas. The only reason we are doing it, and the only reason we get into this situation here, is basically the balance-of-payments situation. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Yes. But we try to control that problem by restrictions on the use which these countries can make of- 

Mr. GAUD. That is right, the dollars.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN (continuing). The money. They must buy goods in this country, but this is a very insignificant aspect of the balance-of-payments problem, with respect to a very small segment of our overall business. Yet this trade might be significant to a developing country.

Mr. GAUD. Well, this was a difficult problem, it was an awkward problem. We did our best to work it out without legislation. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. I have no further questions, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Monagan. 

Mr. MONAGAN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Gaud, I have just come back from Saigon and I can say that the commodity import program there is in a mess. Now, there are several aspects of it that concern me.

First of all, I think at least to some degree that what you have said is correct. There has been a sudden increase in the program of very marked degree in financing. But, why is it that we do it that way? It is true that there were no end-use checkers there, it is true that there hasn't been any post audits, so to speak. There are many other aspects of it, but even if the funds are available, we have done this before, we have run into these same difficulties, and it is rather ironic that the people that are running the program there are the same ones who ran it in Laos, where we had a similar experience. 

So why is it that we can't learn and build these things up in a modwhile we can still hold control over them? erate way, Mr. GAUD. Well, I suppose the answer is that we can't wait because of the urgency. You get into a situation such as we were in in Vietnam, last summer and early fall, when the number of American troops moving into Vietnam was increased enormously and they were moved in very fast.

This required a tremendous increase in military and other construction in Vietnam. This, in turn, created a large consumer demand. 

Inflation started to set in, or to grow very materially from what it had been before.

The question is: What do you do about it, what can you do to sop up some of those dollars and prevent inflation from getting out of hand? 

One of the answers is to increase the import of commodities into Vietnam, which is what we did. Now it is fairly easy to increase your imports of commodities into Vietnam, assuming you can get them through the port, which is also a problem. 

Mr. MONAGAN. Yes.

Mr. GAUD. But it is a lot harder for us to hire people who know commercial practices and who are willing to leave their families and go to Vietnam, some of whom have to speak French, some of whom should speak Vietnamese. It is hard to recruit people, it takes time to recruit people. As I said earlier, Mr. Monagan, I recognize 100

percent that your criticism is justified. At the same time I think it is fair to say that the number of people that we have working on these problems has increased very considerably in the last few months and it is still increasing further. We now have in Vietnam 18 U.S.- 

Mr. MONAGAN. I know there has been some increase in the last few weeks at any rate, but the point is that obviously you can get to a point where even though you are pushing the commodities in there, the concomitant defects and problems are going to be such that in the long run it will be questionable whether we should have pushed the commodities along without ordinary business safeguards. 

But Mr. Hays was talking about this exchange problem and it seems to me there is another aspect of it that he touched on that is questionable. I would like to hear your justification for it. But, as I understand it, there are several exchange rates there, but the importers are, in essence, able to get $1 for 60 piasters, while the actual value of the piaster is about 120 to the dollar on the black market.

Now, in permitting this situation, you are giving a windfall of 100 percent to the importer whose license is obtained through the Government, and then he is permitted to make a profit on top of that, I assume, when he sells the commodity in the market. 

Now just what is the justification for that? 

Mr. GAUD. Well, the importer who brings goods into Vietnam and as you say has a rate of 60 piasters to $1, when he sells those goods he sells them in a market which is subject to price controls. Granted these are imperfect. We all agree the situation in Vietnam is not what we would like it to be in terms of Government enforcement of its price control system.

Nevertheless, he sells these goods under a system of price control. 

He doesn't get any dollars, all he gets is piasters. He is not in position from selling the goods that he brings into the country to salt away dollars in Switzerland.

Mr. MONAGAN. I know that. He gets the goods. 

Mr. GAUD. The question is- 

Mr. MONAGAN. But the dollar price is financed at the rate of 60 piasters? 

Mr. MONAGAN. If he had to acquire the dollars to buy them directly, he would have to pay 120? 

Mr. GAUD. That is right. But he can't do that.

Mr. MONAGAN. He can't do that directly, but in effect this is what happens. 

Mr. GAUD. Of course, by bringing these things in, he has an opportunity to sell them in the black market, he has an opportunity, if he can get away with it, to overinvoice that sort of thing.

Mr. MONAGAN. Those are fraudulent practices. 

Mr. GAUD. Fraudulent devices which we try to stay on top of. 

Mr. MONAGAN. Of course, you wouldn't be able to without the personnel there.

Mr. GAUD. Well, we have now in Vietnam 18 U.S. employees and 

40 locals, most of them auditors, working on this program alone, devoting full time to it—a total of 58 people. In addition to that we have a number of other people in our management and inspection

staff, other auditors, end-use checkers and the like who are working on this program.

Back here in the United States we have nine people working on this program full time. It is a total of 67 people who work on this program alone between Vietnam and here, plus the part time of others. It still isn't enough, as I said. We are doing our best to build up the auditors, end-use checkers, and all the rest of it. It is an endless problem; you are quite right.

Mr. MONAGAN. How is the determination made as to the type of commodities that are permitted to be imported? 

Mr. GAUD. The theory, the overall theory, is that we will import only those items which are essential to their economy, not luxury items; the decisions are made basically in our program office in Vietnam, in conjunction with the Vietnamese Government, which likewise has a staff, I may say, of 50 people that work exclusively on this program. 

But the decision is made essentially in our program office in Vietnam. I can give you some figures: For 1966, for example, iron and steel mill products would be about $72 million; industrial machinery and parts, $71 million; rice, $35 million; petroleum products, $27 million; chemicals and related products, $43 million; fertilizer, $17 million. Now those items account for $265 million out of the total of about $360, $370 million. 

Mr. MONAGAN. Have you been able to make end-use checks up to this point as to the use of any of these commodities that have been imported under the program?

Mr. GAUD. Some; not nearly enough. This is another part of our staff we are trying to beef up. We have set up, as I am sure you know from your trip, a special unit in our staff out there to do just this sort of thing, to try to find out which importers are phonies, which importers we ought to strike from the list altogether, to what extent his stuff is getting into the black market or into the hands of the Vietcong.

This is something we are working on. We have not had nearly enough of that, we have to do more. 

Mr. MONAGAN. I do not have time to go into any more questions, but 

I certainly do want to take this opportunity to emphasize the fact that unless this program is checked, and checked carefully, and followed through, that it will be very difficult to support its continuance.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Fraser. 

Mr. FRASER. Mr. Gaud, it seems to me that your description of the problems that you are having with respect to the commodity import program is the tip of an iceberg which is much larger. What I am about to say is rhetorical, but we spend around $50 billion a year to maintain an Army in being, millions of men, and developing hardware and capabilities and trained specialties, such as the Corps of Engineers, the various support personnel, and so on; all these specialties needed to wage war.

Do you have any similar operation that permits you to develop specializations and capabilities on a contingency, standby basis to be brought to bear in a case like Vietnam when it comes into a crisis situation? 

Mr. GAUD. No, sir; we do not. 

Mr. FRASER. In other words—well, would it be your judgment that if you had had a reservoir of trained personnel that were available on call with training in these kinds of problems that this would have been helpful to you?

Mr. GAUD. It certainly would have been helpful if we had had it. 

The question is how to get it. 

Mr. FRASER. I understand that.

Mr. GAUD. As you know, we have our staff divided into two groups, the Foreign Service Reserve, and the civil service. We have, of course, many auditors and others here in the United States who are working on civil service. We have no way of sending them or directing them to go to Vietnam. They are available, but we can't order them to go there. They are not eligible for foreign duty unless they want to go.

As far as the Foreign Service Reserve people are concerned, they are serving overseas, most of them, and there have been many instances in the last 6 or 8 months when we have ordered people from other posts to Vietnam to try to meet the situation, and they have gone. If they don't go, they have to resign from the agency.

One of the great features of the Hays bill, as we see it, is that it will get us into a position where we can more nearly meet this type of situation than we can today, and we devoutly hope that the Senate will pass that bill this year. 

Mr. FRASER. Well, I don't know the answer to this problem, but where the kinds of wars we are now involved in require our intervention into the economy of the country, controlling and regulating commerce, supplies, and so on, especially those situations where we are entwined with the politics of those countries, we don't have anybody who is really available in the same way that we prepare for these contingencies at the military level.

I don't know that there is any simple answer. It doesn't seem to me feasible to put a lot of people on the payroll and have them stand around doing nothing, waiting for another Vietnam to break out. 

But it seems to me that this matter demands more attention in our contingency planning. We ought to have a broader spectrum of capabilities which are developed and ready to go than we do have under our regular aid programs around the world. 

Would you agree?

Mr. GAUD. I agree, yes, I do. 

Mr. FRASER. The last thing I would like to ask-I need the chairman's attention, but I had three questions I submitted to your agency relating to the cooperative movement. I would like to ask permission from the chairman if I may have those questions put in the record with the answers that the Agency is in the process of preparing. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Without objection.

(The information follows:) SHIPPING TO NORTH VIETNAM 

While free world shipping to North Vietnam has declined sharply since the fall of 1965, Communist-flag shipping has increased slowly from its already substantial level in the spring and summer of 1965. 

The monthly average number of free world arrivals for the last quarter of 1965 and the first quarter of 1966 was about 13. The average number of Communist-flag arrivals for the corresponding period (last quarter of 1965 and first quarter of 1966) was considerably higher.

Cargoes arriving at North Vietnamese ports on free world ships have been nonstrategic and small-primarily fertilizer, soft coal, some foodstuffs and consumer goods. Many of the free world ships going to North Vietnam in recent

months have arrived in ballast, their assignment being to pick up anthracite coal at the ports of Hong Gay or Cam Pha.

Communist-flag ships, on the other hand, have been carrying a wide range of items essential for North Vietnam's economy and the maintenance of its industry. its transport and communications systems. These cargoes have ranged from rice, sugar, and fertilizer to POL, motor vehicles, structural steel, machinery and spare parts, and pharmaceuticals. Deck cargoes of motor vehicles (primarily trucks) on Soviet ships have been photographed by the U.S. Navy and some pictures have been released to the U.S. press. (Further classified information also has been made available to the Committee.) 

Mr. FULTON. Another point I would humbly suggest, that on the people I have recommended who have volunteered from my district for Vietnam. One is a retired fire chief, one I think is a school teacher. They have volunteered to go over to Vietnam and serve, either at little or no pay, for 1 or 2 years out of their civic and patriotic interests. I am unable to get them even considered. We have been told, they have to be close to $12,000 or $13,000 a year before the agency would even consider their employment, and they would have to have college degrees instead of adequate experience in the United States in public institutions. In view of your shortage of people on recruiting at the present time I would therefore suggest that you take off the name of a Republican Congressman named Fulton sponsoring them in order that they might get some further consideration. 

That is all, Mr. Chairman, thank you.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Roybal.

Several witnesses before this committee have said that the situation in Vietnam is one of complete corruption, not only on the part of the Vietnamese people but particularly on the part of American businessmen and members of their families. This has been the case-it has been expressed not only before this committee but people in my district and people in California, and no doubt throughout the country, seem to think this is the situation. I have been unable to present a defense outside of the fact that AID is doing the very best they possibly can. Now what do you suggest would be the proper defense that could be made with regard to this statement? Mr. GAUD. Well, I hardly know how to answer you, Mr. Roybal. A statement so extreme as one to the effect that all Vietnamese or all Americans in Vietnam are crooked and corrupt, it seems to me answers itself. I mean this is just ridiculous.

Now, you can't prove to someone who believes it that it is ridiculous I don't know what more we can do than supply you with the best information we can as to the efforts we are making to root out corruption and to prevent corruption. There has certainly been some of it. There have been convictions of Americans and Vietnamese both, but I am afraid I can't give you a satisfactory overall answer. We would be glad to come up and talk to you about it and give you such information as we can. 

Mr. ROYBAL. I wish you would, because this presents quite a problem to me. We are placed in the position where we have to admit that there is corruption and then the next question is: Well, what are

you doing about it? Most people think that members of this committee have full authority. We have to answer to the voters of our district. So I wish that you would brief me on it. 

Mr. GAUD. Be glad to.

Mr. ROYBAL. So I can get a better picture of the situation. 

Mr. GAUD. Be glad to. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Particularly what is being done about it. 

Mr. ROYBAL. One more question. I realize we are being called now to answer a quorum call. Another witness before this committee said that the Korean galvanized sheet being sold in Vietnam is very inferior. He said it lasts only 1 year, while the same product being 'manufactured in the United States and sent to Vietnam would last 10 years. The price between the two they said wasn't too great and that this is one of the reasons why the recommendation is made that all of this be done by American businessmen.

Now, what is the true situation with regard to that statement? 

Mr. GAUD. There is no question about the fact, judging by some tests that we made by an independent testing company, that some of the Korean galvanized sheet did not come up to American standards. 

We have had other tests made on different material which have been much more favorable to the Korean galvanized sheets. What proportion of the galvanized sheeting from Korea is defective, I have no way of knowing. We do know some of it didn't come up to American specifications.

Now, how do you compare galvanized sheet made here and galvanized sheet made there in terms of their lasting for 1 to 10 years? I can't possibly do that. I do know that no galvanized sheet will last anywhere near as long in Vietnam-the climatic conditions being what they are as it will in the United States. I am not aware of any tests that have been made on American galvanized sheet in Vietnam and how long it will last there. So, I think there is a good deal of speculation in your statement. 

But the essential fact it seems to me, as I have said earlier in the day, is that we will from here on insist that all galvanized sheet that we will finance for Vietnam must meet the requirements of the American 

Society for Testing and Materials. So that whatever the deficiencies in this material in the past may have been, they will not get by in the future. 

Mr. ROYBAL. I wish you had insisted on that to begin with.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Mr. Hamilton? 

Mr. HAMILTON. Mr. Gaud, I was interested in your price and cost comparison statement. One of the things that struck me about it is that it is almost too good. It seems to be too pat. Mr. HAMILTON. Right. 

Mr. GAUD. Now, I have no idea, frankly, of how much of that is needed for these other cost items and whether some of that could go in kickbacks. I just don't know, but the possibility certainly is there. 

Mr. HAMILTON. Well, a very substantial amount of that $50 is obviously going to have to go for those remaining items; is it not? 

Mr. GAUD. I would certainly think so.

Mr. HAMILTON. So there would be almost no money left over for kickbacks and collusion and yet you have found evidence of this? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir.

Mr. HAMILTON. I have also been advised-and I don't know whether this is correct or not-that you have evidence in your files in some instances that Koreans claim they have to pay about $10 to $17 per ton in kickbacks to Vietnamese. Do you have that evidence?

Mr. GAUD. We don't have evidence. We have allegations to that effect. And I don't mean to dismiss them by calling them allegations; we have no evidence at the moment on which you could go into a court of law. We do have allegations, we do have indications that these practices have existed, and both we and the GAO are still looking into them.

Mr. HAMILTON. These are allegations made by whom? 

Mr. GAUD. Various people in both Vietnam and in Korea. 

Mr. HAMILTON. Korean businessmen? 

Mr. HAMILTON. Now, I wanted to go into another matter. Steel jumped in price; in 1963 it was $170 and in 1964 jumped to $270.

Mr. GAUD. The change in the rules may have been responsible for that because then for the first time, after we changed our rule, the Koreans tried to use U.S. blackplate, which is obviously a great deal more expensive than Japanese blackplate. Even though they raised their prices substantially, it is our information that they still lost money. They have since reduced their prices somewhat, since then, but not very much, about $20.

Mr. HAMILTON. Now, let me go to one other, because I know we have time pressure here. Is my understanding correct that you proposed these new procedures only after Senator Bayh made the criticisms that he did? 

Mr. GAUD. Yes.

It seems to me his interest extends a good bit beyond that. A major part of that interest is to see that these imports are adequately policed and carried out. Wouldn't you grant that? 

Mr. GAUD. That is right. I think, sir-forgive me, I meant to say, and I hope I said, that the only issue remaining today is whether the

U.S. industry will get the whole business. 

Mr. HAMILTON. Yes, but doesn't that assume that the quality and the price that you have said are of the three issues, in fact, adequately policed?

Mr. GAUD. Yes, sir; it does.

Mr. HAMILTON. The history is that this has not been adequately policed; right?

Mr. GAUD. We have had a lot of trouble; yes. But we haven't operated under this procedure before. If we can't enforce this procedure, we are going to run into trouble again; you are quite right. 

Mr. HAMILTON. May I express my own concern that you have taken on a tremendous burden of policing here, it seems to me, in looking over the points you set out on page 6 of your statement. How many men are you going to require to get that done?

Mr. GAUD. Well, I don't agree that the burden is that great. As far as the quality is concerned, requiring conformance with the specifications that we refer to here, this is no different from any other requirement that American specifications be met.

As far as the other thing is concerned, the business of the letter of credit, I don't think Senator Bayh understands how this thing works,

Mr. Hamilton, and I think that is why he is worried that we can't enforce it. What actually happens here is, if this procedure goes into effect, the Korean manufacturer ships his goods to Vietnam, and he is paid by his own government in won, in Korean currency. We then deposit dollars in a U.S. bank to the credit of the Korean Government and those dollars can only be spent for iron and steel products. 

Mr. GAUD. Well, there will be a checkup. There will be a checkup,

Mr. Adair. To begin with, materials shipped under a special letter of credit are specially marked. In other words, the box is especially marked so that you know that those things are traveling under a special letter of credit and they are relatively easy to identify. When the stuff is shipped, the bank which has passed on the shipping documents and the like, will send a copy of the documents, if they are being shipped to Korea, to our people there, and there can be a check made fairly easily. We will also require the addition of a customs receipt to the regular shipping documents. But, here again, this is a point 

Senator Bayh raised: What is to prevent these people in Korea from buying the goods from the United States and then reshipping them elsewhere? Well, how can they, if they are going to have to pay two freight rates shipping them to Korea and then shipping them from 

Korea to somewhere else? How are they possibly going to do that on a competitive basis when the hypothetical purchaser to which they might be going can buy directly from the States and only pay one freight rate. It doesn't make any sense to me. 

Mr. ADAIR. Now, on another point: Is there any difference in your opinion-and I recognize that neither you nor I are experts in steel except as we learn something about it here-is there any difference in the quality of Japanese blackplate and U.S. blackplate? 

Mr. GAUD. Well, I am told not.

Now, Korea has very substantial requirements for iron and steel products. When a person in Korea goes to the Government and asks for an import license, he is granted it and he is told, "Buy your goods from the United States against these dollars." This is precisely the same procedure we use today under all of our program lending. We are used to it, the banks in New York are used to it, the importing countries are used to it. Vietnam doesn't have a darned thing to do

with it. The individual exporter of galvanized sheet in Korea doesn't have a darned thing to do with it. We don't pay him with a letter of credit, which seems to be Senator Bayh's impression. That pro cedure, as I say, is well known to the banks, well known to these countries with which we are doing business. So I just can't accept the proposition that we are getting into some kind of a terrifically complicated Rube Goldberg procedure.

Mr. HAMILTON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

EFFECT OF COOPERATIVES IN FOREIGN DEVELOPMENT 

Question 1. What evidence has there been compiled within A.I.D. as regards the effectiveness of cooperatives as a basis of democratic development? 

Answer. Comprehensive surveys of the impact of cooperative institutional development on the generation of democratic participation of people in the Vital decisions and affairs of their lives and communities are the A.I.D. annual reports to Congress covering cooperative growth in the A.I.D.-assisted countries for the years 1962, 1963, 1964, and the current one for 1965. copy was transmitted to Chairman Morgan on 10/25/65. A copy of our transittal letter and the report are enclosed.) (A The report covering the 1965 progress of cooperative development to the I.D.-assisted countries describes in its eight introductory pages, the growth cooperatives and mutual institutions and their significance in involving people in their own economic and social affairs. 

Examples from the 1965 report illustrating democratic participation are: a. In fiscal year 1965, more than 300,000 people attended rural cooperative meetings which were made possible through assistance originally provided by 

A.I.D. 

b. The National Agricultural Cooperative Federation of Korea which, through local cooperatives, serves 2.2 million families in more than 18,000 villages, is preparing to put cooperatives on an independent and self-supporting basis by 1973.

Question 2. On the basis of the Watson Committee report, much emphasis was given the development of private enterprise with cooperatives an important element. What implementation is being given to the encouragement of cooperative development? 

Answer. An important contribution of the Watson Committee report to cooperative enterprise implementation was to point up the significance of free enterprise as a tool of development. This report has stimulated requests for feasibility surveys for the development of cooperative institutions in several countries, for example: a. USAID/Panama has requested a full-time cooperative institution specialist. b. USAID/Honduras has requested two A.I.D. cooperative contractors to survey that country's need for cooperatives in agriculture, fishing, credit 

Housing, etc. c. USAID/India has a request for assistance from cooperatives to make a comprehensive survey for the production and distribution of fertilizer and for processing oil seeds in India. A number of Indian officials believe that cooperatives can act as a yardstick in the market place for fertilizer and oil seeds.

Question 3. What emphasis is being placed in A.I.D. on reaching the campeinos with cooperatives and other democratic organizations? 

Answer. The fostering of cooperatives to help the campesinos is a slow process. There have been resettlement projects supported with U.S. surplus agricultural commodities in northeast Brazil, Chile and India. Surplus U.S. agricultural commodities have been furnished to members through their Cooperatives. This has helped to develop working capital for the cooperative organizations to expand their members' services. Campesino cooperatives and redit unions are also in the preliminary stages of development in Panama, Ecuador, Peru and Honduras, as follows: a. Plans are being made to extend supervised agricultural credit to campeinos in Ecuador and Panama on a low-cost basis through rural credit unions. b.. An experiment in the province of Veraguas, Western Panama, has been

provided a grant of $10,000 from the Special Project Fund to USAID/Panama. 

From this grant, loans are extended to campesinos and fishermen through pre-cooperative groups which will be federated into a regional, all-purpose cooperative. c. In Peru, a Nation-wide program of cooperatives in community development called Cooperacion Popular has carried out, through its 48 field centers, 6,000 projects, of which 2,000 are completed. There are 11,000 additional community projects which cannot be carried out because of lack of funds. 

Outstanding examples of how cooperatives have made use of modern agricultural methods to increase production and income can be noted in Taiwan and Korea where approximately 2.4 million farm families get fertilizer and credit help through cooperatives.

In Taiwan, the Farmers' Associations of Taiwan are organized on three levels: (1) On the provincial level, (2) on the county and municipal level, and (3) on the district and township level. At the base, they are composed of about 5,000 small agricultural units. Members of these Farmers' Associations are limited to those engaged in land cultivation with more than one-half of the total income earned from farming. More than one-third of the delegates to the Members' Representative Congress must be owner-farmers, tenants, farmers and farm hands.

Of considerable significance is the expanded use being made by A.I.D. of non-government resources in the development of cooperatives. Under Section 

601 of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, popularly referred to as the Humphrey Amendment, cooperatives and nonprofit organizations were encouraged to carry out cooperative development work overseas under agreements with A.I.D. Presently, seven national cooperative and mutual organizations, L 

the International Cooperative Training Center at the University of Wiscon sin, Madison, Wisconsin, are providing cooperative assistance under global agreements with A.I.D. Thus, the resources of U.S. cooperatives and the active support of large segments of the membership are made available to help A.I.D. in carrying out its self-help, person-to-person type of assistance.
